new Neural Network-Based Modeling of Hysteretic Systems: From Prandtl and Preisach Neural Networks to Neuro-Madelung and Neuroplastic Continuous Models

Authors: Mehrdad Shafiei Dizaji, Farzad Shafiei Dizaji

Abstract: Hysteresis is a complex nonlinear phenomenon characterized by memory dependence, path dependence, and, in many physical systems, degradation, non-congruency, asymmetry, and rate dependence. This review examines the development and capabilities of Prandtl neural networks (PNNs), Preisach neural networks (PreisachNNs), generalized Prandtl neural networks (GPNNs), the neuro-Madelung model (NMM), extended Preisach neural networks (EPNNs), and dynamic plastic continuous neural networks (DPCNNs). Particular attention is given to their mechanisms for representing memory, network architectures, activation functions, training algorithms, treatment of deterioration and rate dependence, and applications to nonlinear structural and material systems. The PNN introduced hysteretic memory directly into neural activation through stop operators and demonstrated accurate nonlinear dynamic analysis of structures. Preisach-NNs subsequently extended this concept through an additional nonlinear hidden layer to represent non-Masing hysteresis. GPNNs introduced deteriorating-stop neurons to capture stiffness and strength degradation and non-congruent hysteresis. A different generalization was provided by the NMM, which combined Madelung's hysteresis rules with multilayer feedforward neural networks to represent diverse rate-independent and rate-dependent behaviors. These developments culminated in the EPNN, which combines deteriorating hysteretic operators, nonlinear neural mappings, and input-rate information to accommodate Masing and nonMasing, symmetric and asymmetric, congruent and noncongruent, and rate-independent and rate-dependent hysteresis.

new GridForge: An Open Physics-Informed Digital Twin and Benchmark Suite for UK 11 kV Underground Distribution Cables

Authors: Lilliane Linnet Musoke

Abstract: The UK distribution-cable monitoring sector is data-rich but framework-poor: each Distribution Network Operator (DNO) collects megabytes of telemetry per asset per day, but there is no public benchmark dataset, no shared reference model, and no commonly agreed evaluation protocol for benchmarking sectoral, data-intensive models for operational decision support; notably for maintenance planning and prioritisation. We address this gap with GridForge, an open-source physics-informed digital twin and benchmark suite for fault diagnosis and prediction, integrating four building blocks: (i) a verified steady state and transient thermal model based on IEC 60287 and IEC 60853; (ii) a calibrated synthetic dataset of 64 cables (covering 320 cable-years) representing four canonical UK 11 kV thermal archetypes and four condition modes; (iii) a Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) surrogate that satisfies the algebraic balance defined by IEC 60287 during training and reaches an RMSE of 0.148 {\deg}C on an independent held out set against the reference analytical oracle; and (iv) a five-task benchmark suite that includes three reference baseline models (pure physics, gradient-boosted trees, and the PINN). All numerical parameters are sourced from public standards, the code is licensed under the MIT licence, and the trained PINN converges in only two minutes (as run on the computing platform in Section 10.1), enabling independent verification by DNO engineering and research practitioners. At present only the virtual-sensor task (T4) discriminates between the reference baselines; the other tasks tie or are undefined on this test split, so extending the baselines with task-specific logic is a priority future enhancement. For open validation, the dataset, test sets and models are made available at https://github.com/mlily2024/Gridforge

URLs: https://github.com/mlily2024/Gridforge

new Biologically-Inspired Spike Encoding and Optimization for Spiking Neural Networks

Authors: Ayaan Qayyum, Bruce Yi Bu, Zicong Li, Jingfeng Zhou

Abstract: Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) offer theoretical energy-efficiency advantages over conventional deep networks, but realizing these advantages on vision tasks requires sparse spike representations that naive rate-coding methods fail to provide. We combine biologically motivated spike-encoding schemes with two training improvements: Rouser adaptive-threshold surrogate gradients and Kronecker-Factored Approximate Curvature (K-FAC) second-order optimization. The spike encoding schemes experimented with are rate coding, latency coding, DVS-style delta modulation, and Difference-of-Gaussians (DoG) filtering. Experiments on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST demonstrate that biologically inspired encoders yield the largest accuracy gains in capacity-constrained regimes and serve as an effective parameter-efficiency lever. DoG and delta modulation encoders produce significantly sparser spike trains than rate coding. K-FAC yields modest but consistent accuracy improvements over Adam-trained baselines, and Rouser's learnable per-neuron thresholds improve Fashion-MNIST accuracy from 87.37% to 88.13% by enabling homeostatic firing-rate regulation. Biologically grounded pre-processing methods can help close the accuracy gap between SNNs and non-spiking baselines without increasing model size.

new Goemans's Unsplittable-Flow Conjecture: The Anatomy of AI-Assisted Counterexample Discovery

Authors: Jingwei Ge, Na Xie, Li Li, Jiayang Li

Abstract: Goemans conjectured that any fractional single-source flow could be converted into an unsplittable flow without increasing total cost and while increasing any arc load by at most the largest demand. Recently, Dmitry Rybin announced a seven-vertex counterexample found through an interaction with ChatGPT, which disproved this long-standing conjecture. However, the interaction did not reveal why this particular topology and its demand, fractional-flow, and cost choices work together, whether the example belongs to a broader family, or which structural features determine where counterexamples can occur. It also left unclear why the AI-assisted search succeeded where earlier human efforts had not. In this paper, we develop a forward counterexample construction pipeline. It starts from an arborescence on which failure is impossible and adds terminal-entering shortcut arcs. The resulting load constraints define a finite set of admissible route choices; their convex hull, the conflict polytope, yields exact conditions for demands, fractional flows, and costs. Starting from a simple arborescence, the pipeline selects Rybin's topology and characterizes all counterexamples it supports. Experiments further demonstrate the pipeline's transferability: when instructed to follow it while varying the initial arborescence and shortcut placement, ChatGPT produced counterexamples on topologies genuinely different from Rybin's and found valid constructions starting from 93 other rooted arborescence shapes. The theory also explains why the original example was difficult to discover through blind enumeration and suggests that Rybin's interaction lies between blind enumeration and a complete theory-guided construction.

new Response-Relevant Information Is Not Sufficient for Neural Training-State Equivalence

Authors: Truong Xuan Khanh

Abstract: Response-relevant information is not sufficient evidence that two neural networks are in the same training state. We define training-state equivalence operationally: two checkpoints are equivalent, relative to an intervention family and horizon, if they respond alike when training is perturbed and continues. On fresh runs of a one-layer transformer learning modular addition, response to a weight-decay pulse is highly reproducible relative to between-checkpoint differences (rho_state = 0.934 at 500 steps), establishing the target as measurable. Fifteen descriptors of weight spectra, representation geometry and optimiser geometry carry within-run response-relevant information beyond weight norm and loss on a development dataset, predicting held-out response deviation across seed blocks. But their apparent 16.1% advantage at matching checkpoints by future response does not separate from a conditional placebo preserving each descriptor's fitted dependence on the conventional observables, and a calibrated positive control makes simple insensitivity an unlikely explanation. Information a decoder can extract need not be organised in the pairwise geometry that similarity judgments require.

new Chebyshev Polynomial Zonotope Abstractions for Certified Reachability of Learning-Enabled Systems

Authors: Peng Xie, Amr Alanwar

Abstract: Nearly all current methods for computing reachable sets of nonlinear dynamics propagate them with truncated Taylor expansions plus rigorous remainders. The expansion is pointwise: once its argument is a whole set rather than a point, one polynomial must stay accurate across that set, and it reaches only as far as its disc of convergence. The limit binds hardest on the rational terms and sigmoid gates of modern controllers: smooth as these look on the real line, in the complex plane they blow up at points just off the real axis, closer as a gate sharpens, and those points cap the set width a Taylor expansion can cover, most tightly on the switching surface where the controller decides. We prove a dichotomy: no Taylor degree certifies sets wider than that cap, while a Chebyshev abstraction of equal degree, anchored to the whole set in the same polynomial-zonotope pipeline, has no cap and gains width at a geometric rate in degree. Measured walls match the formulas to between 89 and 94 percent. We give closed-form rigorous remainders, among them the first polynomial softmax transformer; existing sound treatments are linear relaxations. On closed loops, certificates survive where the Taylor ones diverge, and specifications flip from unknown to proven; on trained classifiers up to real MNIST digits, the Chebyshev frontier certifies input territory the Taylor basis surrenders. Every certified claim passed large Monte-Carlo harnesses with zero violations, and an applicability map states in advance where the basis provably cannot matter.

new Budgeted Proper Loss Design for Uncertain Decision Thresholds

Authors: Ankit Sharma (International Institute of Information Technology Bangalore)

Abstract: A binary probability estimate may be used by several decision makers with different error costs. Proper scoring rules can emphasize selected probability thresholds, but a single average threshold distribution need not give a good worst-case regret guarantee. We study the choice of a proper loss when the threshold distribution belongs to a finite specified family. Among integrable loss weights with a positive pointwise floor and a fixed total mass, we derive the weight that minimizes the sharp, distribution-free multiplier from surrogate regret to threshold-averaged decision regret. The solution clips a rescaled upper envelope of the threshold densities at the prescribed floor; its normalization is found by a one-dimensional root search. We give proofs of sharpness, optimality, and stability under density misspecification, as well as a limitation for atomic threshold laws. An exact histogram implementation and two synthetic experiments across 20 seeds verify the construction. The optimized multiplier improves over uniform and average-density weights, while fitted-model performance does not improve uniformly. The result is a loss-design guarantee under an explicit budget, rather than a general claim of predictive superiority.

new Exploring Parkinson's Diagnosis Using Machine Learning Techniques

Authors: Farhaan Qazi

Abstract: In recent years, machine learning (ML) techniques have emerged as promising tools for accurate and timely diagnosis of Parkinson's disease (PD) which is an incurable neurodegenerative disorder. This study explores the integration of ML algorithms and PD diagnosis, highlighting their potential applications in various aspects of the disease. ML methods such as support vector machines, decision trees, multilayer perceptron, k-nearest neighbours, logistic regression and random forest (with and without particle swarm optimisation) were applied on a PD dataset obtained from Leeds Teaching Hospitals NHS Trust containing data from 58 people. In terms of accuracy, the Decision Tree and Logistic Regression performed the best with scores of 72-76% while multilayer perceptron was least effective. All the models were remarkable in classifying positive PD cases with sensitivity scores going up to 97.56%, with SVM classifier at the pinnacle. However, ability to classify healthy controls from PD cases was insignificant throughout the board. Although, all the employed models delivered reasonable results in categorising PD patients in the presented dataset; access to more data, further study and advancement of these ML approaches could lead to enhanced diagnostic outcomes, supplementing to an improved understanding of Parkinson's disease.

new Optimize the Cob, Then the Kernel: Optimizing Beam Search for Generative Recommendation Inference

Authors: Shing Wai Pun, Kai Wu, Pengfei Xia, Hui Zang, Wei Guo, Yong Liu

Abstract: Generative recommendation (GR) inference differs substantially from conventional LLM serving, featuring long input sequences, wide beam search, and only a few fixed output tokens. Existing LLM serving engines such as vLLM process beams as batched independent sequences, causing redundant KV cache accesses over the shared prompt prefix. Furthermore, vLLM executes each beam search generation step as a separate engine invocation, incurring host-side beam processing overhead between decoding steps and substantial runtime KV block management in PagedAttention. Using Tree Attention, we concatenate beams along the sequence dimension to eliminate redundant KV accesses, and further optimize execution through pre-generated partial-attention masks, static KV cache allocation, loop-unrolled generation dispatch, TorchAir graph compilation, automatic operator fusion, and vocabulary-constrained decoding. Without the use of custom kernels, our approach reduces end-to-end latency by up to 25x over vLLM on Ascend NPUs, and achieves generation latency comparable to a SOTA solution using custom kernels, showing that the generation pipeline should be optimized holistically before resorting to custom kernel development.

new Confidence-Based Materials Data Quality Assessment via Domain Knowledge and Graph-Structured Learning

Authors: Sihan Liaing, Ji Zhang, Kehan Wang, Zihao Gao, Wei Ma, Yangang Wang, Zongguo Wang

Abstract: The rapid development of materials machine learning relies on large-scale, heterogeneous datasets, yet model reliability is often compromised by imperfect data from diverse experiments, simulations, and database integration. Real-world materials databases may contain measurement errors, failed calculations, missing or duplicate records, and hidden violations of physicochemical constraints. Such problems are difficult to identify because corrupted samples may remain statistically plausible rather than appearing as explicit outliers. In this paper, we propose a confidence-based materials data quality assessment framework that integrates domain knowledge with graph-structured learning. Data quality is formulated as a continuous confidence estimation problem rather than a binary anomaly identification task, enabling sample-level assessment and historical-reference-based batch comparison. A nine-dimensional feature-quality prior and duplicate-aware sample initialization are calibrated using materials domain rules and statistical evidence. Confidence is further refined through sample-feature bipartite graph propagation and weakly supervised graph attention, while a Memory-GAT module provides historical reference scoring. Experiments on 23 ODDS benchmark datasets show that the generic module achieves an average AUC-ROC of 0.866. In five repeated mixed-contamination experiments on mp\_20, rule calibration improves HistGradientBoostingRegressor test $R^2$ from $0.538\pm0.008$ to $0.616\pm0.014$. Graph refinement increases average precision from $0.943\pm0.004$ to 0.953\pm0.004$, while the rule-calibrated configuration has the highest mean test $R^2$ among the evaluated screening configurations. Five repeated MACE subset experiments further show an increase in $R^2$ from $0.590\pm0.053$ to $0.880\pm0.027$ after rule-based screening.

new BiMa-ECGSR: reconstructing the 12-lead ECG from reduced lead sets with bidirectional state-space models

Authors: Ugo Lomoio, Pierangelo Veltri, Pietro Hiram Guzzi

Abstract: Wearables and monitors record few electrocardiogram (ECG) leads; most diagnostic tools assume 12 leads. BiMa-ECGSR reconstructs the 12-lead ECG from reduced lead sets with a bidirectional Mamba state-space encoder--decoder, as regression (BiMa-SR) or diffusion (DiBiMa-SR). Models were trained on PTB-XL for six input sets (I; II; I+II; I+II+V1; I+II+V2; I+II+V1+V4), see only the measured leads at inference, and were tested on the PTB-XL test fold (2,198 records) and two external cohorts (Georgia, 10,275 records; Chapman-Shaoxing/Ningbo, 43,559). From lead II alone, the pooled median Pearson correlation (PCC) over the 11 predicted leads was 0.914 on PTB-XL, against 0.784 for a least-squares linear filter (lead I: 0.906 against 0.789). As limb leads are exactly derivable from I and II, multi-lead inputs were compared on predicted chest leads: 0.939 against 0.849 for I+II and 0.979 against 0.962 for I+II+V1+V4. BiMa-SR exceeded the linear comparator in all 18 configuration--cohort pairs (by 0.012--0.136), a margin that shrank as chest leads were added. From lead II, regression was more accurate than diffusion (DiBiMa-SR: 0.903 one-step, 0.862 with six DDIM steps). A 12-lead classifier trained only on real ECGs reached a superclass macro AUROC of 0.912 on real PTB-XL ECGs, 0.843 on reconstructions from lead II and 0.908 from I+II+V1+V4; externally, reconstructions from three or four leads scored within 0.004 of real ECGs. BiMa-SR (2.68\,M parameters) takes 45.8 ms per 10-s record on a GPU. Reconstruction makes reduced-lead recordings usable by 12-lead tools; it cannot add information absent from the measured leads.

new Implementing Binary Transformers with Approximate Memories with Permutation Invariance Enabled Selective (PIES) Protection

Authors: Zhen Gao, Xiao Yin, Kexin Liang, Pedro Reviriego, Shanshan Liu, Fabrizio Lombardi

Abstract: Quantization is an effective technique to reduce memory storage, computation complexity and power for Transformers, and the binarization of the weights is important for meeting these requirements. Approximate memory can further reduce the power for memory, but it also introduces errors. Current research for binary Transformers focuses on task performance, but the model dependable operation against errors on the memory has not been well studied. In this paper, we first evaluate the dependability of binary Transformers with the Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers (BERT) as a case study. A large accuracy loss occurs only at high Bit Error Rates (e.g. BER = 5% or 10%). Then we propose a novel Permutation Invariance Enabled Selective (PIES) scheme to protect the binary weights at very low overhead. This scheme exploits the invariance of Transformers to permutations in the rows and columns to group the critical weights and perform selective protection on those weights. Simulation results show that PIES can recover most of the performance loss caused by bit errors at BERs as high as 10%, at an overhead significantly lower than traditional error correction schemes. These results confirm the feasibility of running binary Transformers using approximate memory with aggressive voltage scaling on resource-limited low power edge devices. The code for PIES is released on github (https://github.com/YangZhutian/binary-transformer-prefix-repair).

URLs: https://github.com/YangZhutian/binary-transformer-prefix-repair).

new TRACE-MAS: Diagnosing and Repairing Collaboration Failures in Multi-Agent Systems

Authors: Zekai Chen, Yihan Sun, Xunkai Li, Xun Wu, Xinmo Jin, Yinlin Zhu, Haodong Lu, Xuaner Chen, Rong-Hua Li

Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) rely on not only individual agent behaviors but also the interactions that connect agents, including message exchanges and task dependencies. Existing MAS safeguards mainly perform agent-level anomaly detection, which becomes insufficient when failures originate from agent interactions and detected anomalies do not necessarily reveal their true sources. To address this limitation, we propose \textbf{TRACE-MAS}, a finer-grained framework for diagnosing and repairing MAS failures. TRACE-MAS learns normal collaborative execution patterns from benign trajectories and detects deviations in agent state changes, message transitions, and task dependency evolutions. It further identifies responsible execution elements through source-aware attribution and selects minimal interventions to restore collaboration while preserving task-critical capabilities. Experiments on five MAS benchmarks demonstrate that TRACE-MAS significantly improves root-cause localization accuracy by \textbf{60.2}\% and achieves consistent gains in task recovery over strong MAS safeguards.

new QDA: Closed-Loop LLM Quantization Design with Agents

Authors: Xingyang Li, Tianlang Zhao, Song Han, Ligeng Zhu

Abstract: Post-training quantization is the standard way to serve large language models at low cost. However, the best quantization recipe differs across models and precisions. As a result, experts must redesign it for every new model, which is time-consuming and often sub-optimal. Fortunately, an LLM agent can take over this design by refining the recipe over rounds of building and evaluating candidates, so that more rounds lead to better recipes. However, evaluating one quantized reasoning model takes tens of GPU-hours, and combining methods from different codebases means porting each to the new architecture by hand. We present QDA, a system in which an agent designs the recipe for each model and precision. To make evaluation fast, QDA scores each candidate in minutes by its output KL divergence from the original model on reasoning traces that the agent never sees, and checks promising ones with long generations to catch failures such as repetition loops. To make building fast, QDA provides a library of public PTQ methods that are compatible with one another and with new model architectures. Across four reasoning models, QDA improves accuracy over the strongest public recipe by 0.9, 1.4 and 2.2 percentage points on average at W4A16, W4A8 and W3A16, using 64 GPU-hours per search. On the 320B-parameter MoE model GLM-5.3-Flash at NVFP4 W4A4, it recovers 98.3% of the FP8 model's accuracy, against 96.9% for the best vendor checkpoint. Every QDA checkpoint also runs on existing serving kernels with no inference overhead. We will release the library, the evaluator and the harness.

new Which Tokens, in What Order, and How Fast? A Theoretical Analysis of On-Policy Distillation Objectives

Authors: Hubert Strauss, Liam Fowl, Noam Razin

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student language model to match a teacher model based on its own generations. When using OPD, one must choose whether to apply it at the sequence or token level and which divergence to minimize. However, despite the wide adoption of OPD in practice, the formal understanding of which OPD objective is appropriate for a given setting is limited. Toward addressing this gap, we theoretically analyze how the choice of OPD objective governs which tokens are prioritized, in what order, and how efficiently. Our analysis reveals that sequence-level OPD exhibits a future agreement bias, which myopic token-level objectives avoid. This bias induces a reverse learning order, in which sequence-level OPD first matches the teacher at later token positions, while at earlier positions it can favor tokens that are improbable under the teacher, and thus often undesirable. As for the choice of divergence, we prove that it strongly influences the amenability of OPD to gradient-based optimization. In particular, while the forward KL divergence enjoys a well-behaved objective landscape, other $f$-divergences, such as reverse KL, can suffer from vanishing gradients even when the student and teacher are far apart. Across language model post-training settings, we demonstrate the implications of these theoretical findings for which OPD objective is preferable, highlighting the importance of accounting for the interplay between the student, teacher, and task at hand.

new Incremental Roseland: Batch-wise Landmark Diffusion via Kernelized Sequential Karhunen--Lo\`eve Update

Authors: Jeanie Schreiber

Abstract: Landmark diffusion (Roseland) computes a diffusion-map-style spectral embedding from the singular value decomposition of a degree-normalized data-to-landmark kernel matrix, at a cost far below that of full diffusion maps. It assumes, however, that all data are available at once. We introduce incremental Roseland (iRoseland), which casts the landmark affinity as a row-growing matrix whose SVD can be updated with a kernelized Sequential Karhunen--Lo\`eve (SKL) scheme. Degre correction is handled by a first order expansion about a reference mean kernel row and so that the landmark-side eigenbasis may be recovered from a single landmark-by-landmark eigendecomposition at each step. The presented method requires only a single pass over the data and runs in bounded-memory with state independent of the number of points processed. A forgetting factor lets the method track drifting distributions, and a sample-size-invariant embedding keeps outputs comparable across time. Experiments show agreement with Roseland to round-off on stationary data, and accuracy that exceeds equal-memory Roseland by an order of magnitude on long streams. On drifting data, we observe large gains from forgetting with a window matched to the drift.

new Stability Does Not Mirror Plasticity: A Module-Level View of Declarative and Procedural Learning in Transformers

Authors: Jinjun Peng, Wailiang Zhao, Baishakhi Ray, Zhou Yu, Jufeng Yang

Abstract: Plasticity and stability in continual learning are typically studied at the entire-model level and in a task-agnostic manner, yet prior work has reported divergent conclusions across settings. Inspired by cognitive distinctions between declarative and procedural memory, we instead study how plasticity and stability vary across transformer modules and task types. In a controlled synthetic environment with clean measurement, we show a double dissociation: query-key (QK) and value-output (VO) projections are the most plastic on procedural tasks, whereas MLPs are the most plastic on declarative memorization. Stability, however, does not simply mirror plasticity: when learning a new procedure, updating QK retains over half of the stored declarative memory, while updating MLPs retains far less and updating VO loses roughly four-fifths. Targeted module reset-and-recovery experiments explain this asymmetry: plasticity tracks which module is best suited to a task's primary computation or storage, whereas stability depends on all ways the existing capability relies on that module, including access to information stored in other modules. Experiments on a realistic model and tasks broadly echo these asymmetric dynamics. Overall, our work shows that plasticity and stability depend on different aspects of a module's functional role, offering a task-dependent, module-level perspective on continual learning.

new When Does Subspace Direction Matter for LoRA? Regime Analysis of the Magnitude Principle in Few-Shot Adaptation

Authors: Nischal Subedi, Cencheng Shen, Peng Zhao

Abstract: We show that the importance of a LoRA adapter's initial input-subspace direction is regime-dependent, and we characterize this dependence in theory and experiment. This refines the recent view that spectral initializers help through update magnitude alone. Direction matters most when labeled data are scarce relative to the representation dimension and the activation subspace used for initialization captures supervised task structure. In this regime, the adapter's row span remains sensitive to its starting point, making task-aligned initialization especially useful. A spiked-covariance perturbation analysis characterizes the finite-sample stability of the estimated subspace. A first-step analysis of LoRA training explains why the subspace's alignment with the task matters and motivates an alignment coefficient $\rho$ that closely tracks gains across classification tasks. We test this account with PivotLoRA, which initializes adapters from activation PCA at prediction-relevant positions and applies one output-preserving orthogonal pivot early in training. In few-shot BERT-base classification, PivotLoRA achieves the best average rank among 13 methods and improves over vanilla LoRA in 29 of 30 settings, even though it starts from a smaller input-factor initialization norm. Additional classification experiments extend the evaluation to 7B and 8B Llama decoders. Classification gains diminish with more labeled data, consistent with the proposed regime dependence. A targeted extension to completion-only reasoning also shows improvements on GSM8K and StrategyQA. These findings identify conditions under which initialization direction matters, with subspace-task alignment offering a low-cost diagnostic before training for classification.

new Conformally Calibrated Adaptive Computation in Neural Operators

Authors: Abdul Qadir Ibrahim, Martin Burger, Abel Maviano Jonathan

Abstract: Selecting less computation in a neural operator need not reduce inference time. We study this gap using conformal calibration to choose Fourier modes or channel width per input subject to a fixed error budget. A triangle-inequality envelope bounds all candidates simultaneously; a direct-score rule handles non-monotone candidate errors and, with an input-independent score, calibrates a static baseline under the same marginal guarantee. Under frozen-model and exchangeability assumptions, both rules bound the probability of issuing a prediction outside the budget and abstain when calibration is infeasible. On Burgers and Darcy flow across three training seeds, mode selection reduces active spectral support without a measurable latency reduction, because full-grid transforms and channel mixing dominate the cost. Narrowing channels does reduce Darcy forward cost, but reference-based selection must first compute the dense prediction. An input-only binary width selector avoids that pass and runs at 0.52-0.95 times dense latency; yet static widths calibrated under the same rule are often as fast or faster, and on a separately generated 1,000-example test set they generally achieve higher coverage, while the selector falls below its 90% target in more than half of the settings. Adaptive computation in neural operators should therefore be judged by achieved coverage and complete runtime against a matched static comparator.

new FACT: Fixed-Budget Allocation and Cross-Step Training for Speculative Decoding

Authors: Hadi Fares, Mayssa Hajj Hassan, Ali Salman, Ammar Mohanna

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates language-model generation by verifying multiple draft tokens in a single target-model pass. However, better draft predictions and longer accepted continuations do not necessarily produce faster generation. To examine this gap, we present FACT (Fixed-Budget Allocation and Cross-Step Training), an experimental framework for studying how drafter training, candidate allocation, and execution conditions jointly affect acceptance and latency. Using a lightweight feature-prediction drafter with a frozen Llama-3-8B-Instruct target under greedy decoding, we first examine how training translates into accepted tokens. Backpropagating through predicted-feature rollouts with greater weight on early steps improves acceptance by 15-24% over uniformly weighted detached training, yet average rollout proxy cross-entropy does not reliably rank drafters by acceptance. Beyond prediction quality, the placement of draft candidates also matters: with a fixed 256-node budget and matched per-depth widths, replacing static allocation with confidence-guided allocation increases speedup over autoregressive decoding from $1.46\times$ to $2.02\times$ on HumanEval and from $1.31\times$ to $1.75\times$ on MT-Bench. This allocation benefit extends to an independently trained drafter, but stronger training does not consistently amplify it, showing that the two improvements do not necessarily reinforce each other. Even these speed gains depend on execution conditions: changing only host-thread settings can nearly eliminate speedup without changing acceptance. Together, these findings show why training and allocation must be evaluated jointly using both acceptance and end-to-end latency under controlled execution conditions.

new AllotNet: an Interpretable Mixture-of-Experts Choice Model for Moral Judgement in Humanitarian Aid Allocation, Validated against a Planted Synthetic Population

Authors: Mark Nowak (Conflux Laboratory)

Abstract: Official development assistance fell by 23.1 per cent in 2025, and the decision of who is helped first when there is not enough for everyone is moving into software. A leading published machine-learning targeting system excluded 53 per cent of eligible households as deployed. If the rule of distribution ends up in code, it should carry the judgement of many cultures, and the machine should execute while a person judges. AllotNet is an interpretable mixture-of-experts conditional logit for the allocation judgements elicited by Allot, a pre-registered six-language instrument: eight linear taste experts over seven ethical criteria, weighted by a gate over participant context, with a damped residual, an encoder for household intake, an outcome head and a budget-constrained planner that builds a review queue and never rejects anyone. A model of human judgement has no answer key on real data, so we wrote one first: a planted synthetic population of 5.6 million sessions and 104 million responses (98 million usable choices), generated by the production code path from a ground truth hidden from the loss. All sixteen pre-specified checks passed: criterion ranking $\rho = 1.000$, hidden segments recovered (ARI 0.671), outcome head at 99 per cent of the noise ceiling, planner at 99.4 per cent of oracle. The caveats are reported with them: two checks are weaker than their titles, and a live-generation failure cost 260,712 seeds. The model has 23,550 parameters, is trained quantisation-aware for NVFP4 with its interpretable core in fp32 (welfare-rank parity $\rho = 0.9937$ at W4A16 on the shipped bundle; one parity threshold was relaxed post hoc), and ships as a 125 KB bundle with C11, Java, Rust and Python runtimes. Nothing has been trained on real data yet; at the study's real scale a hierarchical Bayesian mixed logit is the registered comparator.

new Stability of Parallel Transport Convolution under Riemannian Metric Perturbations

Authors: Hongnian Huang, Andrew Stump

Abstract: Geometric convolutions on Riemannian manifolds depend on the geometry of the underlying domain, making stability under metric perturbations a fundamental question in geometric deep learning. This paper studies this question for parallel transport convolution through methods from differential geometry and analysis. On a fixed compact manifold, we establish that, for a continuous input signal and a continuous reference kernel supported in a sufficiently small normal neighborhood, the convolution output depends continuously in $L^2$ on the Riemannian metric in the $C^2$-topology, under the specified localization and transport-path conventions. The analysis develops perturbation estimates for geodesics, parallel transport, Jacobi fields, and exponential maps, and uses these estimates to compare the transported kernels. A central difficulty is the cut locus, which obstructs uniform comparison of the transport construction over the entire manifold. We address this difficulty by combining uniform estimates away from the cut locus with integral bounds on neighborhoods of arbitrarily small volume, while also controlling the change in the Riemannian volume measure. The resulting qualitative stability theorem connects perturbation theory for Riemannian geometric constructions with the analysis of spatial convolution operators in geometric deep learning.

new Safe to Omit, Worth Omitting? Keeping the Answer and Cutting Latency When an LLM Agent Skips Another's Message

Authors: Xinyang Li, Chih-Hsuan Yang, Xiaolong Ma, Anjir Ahmed Chowdhury, Vikram Vasudevan, Rajeev Thakur, Feng Yan

Abstract: In multi-agent systems, a receiver agent often waits for a helper agent's message (text or its latent KV cache), which can improve performance but adds latency. We study when a receiver agent can skip the message safely (the final answer rarely changes) and beneficially (the time saved exceeds the cost of deciding). Our mechanism reads the receiver agent's confidence without the message and uses a finite-sample test to certify a threshold at which, with high probability, at most 5% of skipped answers change; if none passes, the system keeps communicating. We test it on OpenBookQA, ARC-Challenge, and MMLU-Pro with three Qwen model pairs, on cross-family agents, and on SQuAD and GSM8K. Four insights emerge. First, oracle headroom is weak evidence that messages help: control inputs without answer-specific content, matched to change answers as often as real messages, reproduce a median 76% of the oracle's gain. Second, whether a message can be skipped depends on how often and how predictably it alters the answer, not on its accuracy; every certified KV-cache path is less accurate than the receiver agent alone. Third, where text messages help, skipping keeps most of their gain: on unseen questions in the four such settings, the policies skip 59 to 80% and keep an estimated 76 to 95% of the gain. In two prospective tests with all rules fixed in advance, our mechanism skips a three-agent system's verifier on 39.9% of sealed questions, keeping 12.55 of its 12.80-point gain, and a 32B helper on 68.4% of held-out questions, keeping its whole gain. Fourth, safety does not determine efficiency: at batch size one, skipping text saves hundreds of milliseconds per query and skipping KV cache far less. Certification extends to SQuAD but not to GSM8K. In eleven tests on unseen questions, every certified policy we could test kept its change rate's 95% upper bound below 5% under our answer parsers.

new Efficient Variance-Aware Reinforcement Learning in Time-Inhomogeneous Linear MDPs

Authors: Qingyue Zhao, Qiwei Di, Jiafan He, Heyang Zhao, Kaixuan Ji, Quanquan Gu

Abstract: We study variance-dependent reinforcement learning in episodic time-inhomogeneous linear Markov decision processes (MDPs) with feature dimension $d$, horizon $H$, and $K$ episodes. While recent works have established variance-dependent regret with $\tilde{O}(1)$ deterministic-regime overhead for linear mixture MDPs (Zhao et al., 2023) and worst-case nearly minimax optimal regret $\tilde{O}(d\sqrt{H^3 K} + d^7 H^8)$ for linear MDPs (He et al., 2023),, existing variance-aware analyses for linear MDPs (Li and Sun, 2023; Huang et al., 2023) rely on a coarse concentration step whose resulting regret guarantees retain a polynomial $\tilde{O}(K^{1/4})$ additive term even in deterministic environments. We propose VA-LSVI-UCB++, a computationally efficient algorithm that achieves the variance-dependent regret bound $\tilde{O}(d\sqrt{H\min\{\mathrm{Var}_K^*,\mathrm{Var}_K^\pi\}}+d^5H^4)$, where $\mathrm{Var}_K^*$ and $\mathrm{Var}_K^\pi$ denote the cumulative conditional variances of the optimal value function and the played policies' value functions along the realized trajectory. Our result adapts to the minimum of the two realized trajectory variances, recovers the worst-case minimax optimal leading term $\tilde{O}(d\sqrt{H^3 K})$, and collapses to $\tilde{O}(d^5 H^4)$ in deterministic environments, free of polynomial-in-$K$ dependency. At the core of our analysis is a simultaneous three-way reference-advantage decomposition for the optimistic, pessimistic, and squared value function estimators coupled with variance self-bounding and Freedman-type value-difference arguments, which tightens the pessimistic and second-moment confidence radii by a factor of $\tilde{O}(d H)$ and removes the inverse-variance-floor blowup.

new DuoCL: Dual-Path Online Continual Learning

Authors: Jebacyril Arockiaraj, Dhruv Parikh, Rajgopal Kannan, Viktor Prasanna

Abstract: Online continual learning (OCL) requires learning from a data stream whose arrival rate is independent of model training speed. When training cannot keep pace, computationally expensive gradient-based methods update less frequently, limiting adaptation. Analytic methods reduce this overhead, but their frozen backbones cannot adapt representations to changing distributions. We propose DuoCL, a dual-path OCL framework whose fast analytic path accumulates feature statistics from every incoming minibatch while its slow path periodically adapts the backbone. The slow path adjusts its sample count to the available compute budget without interrupting fast-path learning. Feature alignment compensates for drift in the accumulated classifier statistics. Across four datasets and three computational budgets, DuoCL improves online accuracy by an average of 9.67 percentage points over the strongest gradient-based baseline in each setting. DuoCL consistently outperforms analytic learning under distribution shifts, with gains of up to 4.00 percentage points on CIFAR-C, and achieves higher accuracy than prior methods across disjoint class-incremental, domain-incremental, and Si-Blurry settings.

new Entropy-Regularized Integrated Gradients for Stable Feature Attribution

Authors: Rukmangadh Sai Myana, Sumit Kumar Jha, Yanzhao Wu

Abstract: Path-based attribution methods like Integrated Gradients (IG) are widely used to interpret neural networks, yet imperceptible input perturbations can drastically alter their attribution maps while leaving model predictions unchanged. We introduce Entropy-Regularized Integrated Gradients (ERIG), a path-based attribution method that intrinsically attenuates gradient irregularities along the integration path using maximum-entropy softmax weighting with temperature parameter $\tau$. We quantify the effect of the entropy-regularized weighting on attribution stability by using the Lipschitz properties of the model and its gradients. We decompose the attribution change under an input perturbation into a model-response term, whose bound is $\tau$-independent, and a weight-variation term, whose bound decreases at rate $\mathcal{O}(1/(2\tau))$. Their ratio $R(\tau)$ characterizes the trade-off between gradient information and attribution stability. We further add a new dimension to attribution evaluation by adapting classical noise estimators into two metrics, LINV-C and LINV-P, that quantify attribution noise. Across eleven CNN and Transformer backbones on ImageNet and Oxford-IIIT Pet datasets, our experiments show that, relative to the next-best method, ERIG remains comparable on ranking-based fidelity metrics while improving localization by approximately 24% and attribution stability by approximately 47-fold on average, reaching up to four orders of magnitude improvement over IG, all at negligible additional computational cost.

new JEPA as a Transferable Optimizer: Learning and Understanding Black-Box Optimization from Reward-Agnostic Pretraining

Authors: Mayukh Debnath, Manan Roy Choudhury

Abstract: Black-box optimization evaluates candidates through simulations, rollouts, or experiments, often without gradients. Learned optimizers are usually judged by whether they outperform existing methods. We ask a complementary question: what information does a learned optimizer use from its search history? We introduce JEPA-PS (JEPA Policy Search), based on a Joint-Embedding Predictive Architecture. It is pretrained on random rollouts using parameter vectors and scalar returns. Its latent-prediction objective predicts the next point in an optimization trajectory rather than its reward or gradient, allowing us to examine what was tried and how well it performed. At test time, JEPA-PS reads a short evaluation history and proposes a candidate in one forward pass, without task descriptions, reward shaping, or test-time gradients. We evaluate it on 15 control environments against 12 optimizers, including evolutionary methods, Bayesian optimization, CMA-ES, and an LLM optimizer. JEPA-PS is competitive with tuned classical baselines and outperforms the LLM optimizer by more than 2x on Swimmer at 71x lower per-step cost. It also transfers zero-shot to environments excluded from pretraining. Null-model comparisons and decoder-training ablations indicate that its latent representation carries information beyond simple history statistics; every trained decoder variant performs worse than the default frozen representation. Our main finding is that the history JEPA-PS uses depends on the task. On smooth landscapes it uses the full trajectory, whereas on rugged landscapes the best-seen point carries nearly all the signal. This memory requirement has a weak positive correlation with fitness-distance correlation (FDC; r=0.112, n=7), while an independent reward-ordering sensitivity probe does not corroborate the relationship (r=-0.024, n=7). Code: https://github.com/mdebnath22/jepa-props.

URLs: https://github.com/mdebnath22/jepa-props.

new Label-Free Role-Aware Segmentation of Classroom Recordings: Recovering the Teacher from Transferable Structure

Authors: Nitin Jain, Tim F\"utterer, Babette B\"uhler, \c{C}a\u{g}atay Y{\i}ld{\i}z

Abstract: How much the teacher talks and when are among the most informative and costly-to-measure signals of teaching. Yet, classroom audio is among the most sensitive data an AI system can process: recordings capture minors and fall under GDPR, preventing raw audio from leaving the recording device. We present a teacher-student segmentation pipeline designed to meet exactly this constraint: label-free at inference, running on a recording device, and requiring no per-session calibration. The pipeline chains a lightweight voice activity detector, compactly pretrained speaker embeddings, agglomerative clustering, and a small logistic regression that assigns the teacher role based on session-relative structure rather than voice identity. We show that our structural cue transfers across classrooms, languages, and acoustic conditions. In leave-one-teacher-out cross-validation on 292 recordings from 63 teachers across English and German corpora, our pipeline achieves teacher F1 of 0.81 and 0.87, respectively, outperforming both enrollment-based baselines and a 30-second supervised enrollment ceiling. On a commodity smartphone, its performance matches pyannote, a strong neural diarization tool, while running 4.8x faster at comparable peak memory at a real-time factor of ~0.18. We also find that transcribing filtered audio only marginally increases teacher's word error rate from $0.26$ to 0.28. Residual failures concentrate where teacher and student voices are not acoustically separable. However, we show how to flag these sessions label-free at inference so that they can be routed to human review, enabling selective, privacy-preserving deployment.

new Error accumulation in reverse diffusion with an imperfect score: beyond path-space analyses

Authors: George Giapitzakis, Ankur Moitra, Andrej Risteski, Dhruv Rohatgi

Abstract: Diffusion modeling is a popular method of sampling from a complex, high-dimensional distribution $p$ using learned approximations of its (noised) score functions. Generating a single sample requires simulating the reverse diffusion process, which requires many queries to the learned scores. Thus, a priori, the learning errors can easily compound. Indeed, in existing theoretical analyses, the sampling error (as measured in total variation distance to $p$) accumulates polynomially with the horizon of the stochastic process. This is a consequence of bounding the error of the output law by the error of the law of the entire reverse diffusion path. In this paper, we demonstrate that by circumventing the "path-space" analysis, the error accumulation of the diffusion sampler can be mitigated when $p$ satisfies a Poincar\'e inequality. Moreover, we achieve this result as a special case of a broader framework that applies to any distribution $p$, but relaxes the error metric from total variation distance to a weaker metric capturing all sample statistics that do not "witness" the multimodality of $p$. We then zoom out from analyzing the diffusion sampler to the information-theoretic question: given a learned score with some error bound, how accurately can we identify $p$? We show that, for any given bound on the Poincar\'e constant of $p$, the diffusion sampler already nearly achieves optimal total variation error.

new EEG2MCU: Sensing-to-Execution Co-Design for Wearable EEG Inference

Authors: Shoma Yukawa, Meghna Roychowdhury, Arnab Sanyal, Chengwei Zhou, Shreyas Sen, Gourav Datta

Abstract: Wearable EEG inference is constrained not only by model size, but also by the channel-dependent sensing front end that must process high-dimensional inputs before compact downstream computation. We present EEG2MCU, a sensing-to-execution co-design framework that treats electrode count as a hardware design variable through task-aware montage selection. EEG2MCU retains a compact time-frequency representation and generates only the INT8 regions required by each first-layer tile, avoiding full input materialization. Halo reuse and access-aware placement across Flash, SRAM, and tightly coupled memory further reduce redundant generation and data movement. On a Cortex-M7 MCU, EEG2MCU reduces peak SRAM by 11.38x relative to the baseline, including a 1.71x reduction from the 62-to-19 electrode montage, while reducing latency by 2.57x and energy by 2.48x. It retains 94-96% of full-montage accuracy, demonstrating practical high-dimensional EEG inference under tight embedded-resource constraints.

new Agent Verifiers in Counterfactual Analysis: A Pilot Study Benchmarking Agentic Heterogeneous Treatment Effects

Authors: Satiyabooshan Murugaboopathy, Entejar Alam, Adel Daoud, Connor T. Jerzak

Abstract: Agents can expand the covariate space of randomized experiments by retrieving fast-moving local context missed by slow-changing survey baseline covariates, helping characterize who benefits most from an intervention. Verifying such covariates is difficult: individual treatment effects are unobserved, retrieval leakage can pull post-treatment information into nominally pretreatment summaries, and encoder leakage can inject post-treatment pretraining knowledge into representations even after temporal filtering of inputs. Guided by a two-channel information bound separating summary provenance from encoder weights, we propose a three-layer benchmark protocol: (i) retrieval admissibility via pre-treatment date filtering; (ii) heterogeneity validation scored rank-weighted average treatment effects (RATEs); and (iii) encoder admissibility checked with chronologically vintaged encoders, future-fact probes, and weight perturbations. Across 11 datasets anchored by Uganda's Youth Opportunities Program, we compare session-isolated retrieval (sandboxed, bounded-budget agent runs per study) against a persistent workspace teammate (xAI's GrokBot) that builds one date-verified corpus per study while blinded to outcomes. The workspace teammate yields higher mean RATE ratios in 6/11 datasets for text alone and 7 for combined features, though paired tests show neither workflow universally dominates. Even on fixed retrieved text, encoder choice significantly shifts heterogeneity signal. Chronologically vintaged probes show no clear evidence of weight leakage, but single-record adapter perturbation analyses show that memorized future facts, once present, persist until injected noise degrades model output. Temporal filtering of retrieval is necessary but insufficient: verifying agent-generated covariates requires jointly examining source provenance, out-of-sample treatment-effect signal, and encoder weights.

new What Interpolators Store and What They Express: An Information-Theoretic Framework for Benign Overfitting and Label-Free Model Selection

Authors: Nikolaos Nikolaou

Abstract: Overparameterized learners can fit noisy training labels perfectly and still predict well, which neither classical complexity measures nor information-theoretic generalization bounds explain. We develop an information-theoretic framework that separates two things an interpolating learner does with its sample: storage, the information the trained model retains about its training labels, and expression, the information about the sample that reaches its predictions at fresh inputs. We first characterize the ideal model: for a loss l, the least complex l-sufficient representation is the Bayes act, whose entropy is the minimal sufficient complexity; this generalizes, and corrects, an earlier "lossless maximal compression" view of boosting. We then show that every interpolator stores exactly nH(Y|X) bits of label noise, so storage, and bounds built on it (including CMI bounds), cannot tell interpolators apart. Expression can: excess risk is bounded above and below by the expressed information V, the entropy over training samples of the prediction at an input, plus a modal-bias term, exactly so for binary labels. Benign overfitting is therefore vanishing expression, a picture that covers nearest-neighbour rules, averaging, minimum-norm least squares and double descent, and that links V to classical bias-variance decompositions and to model disagreement. Finally, V gives a model-selection criterion that needs no held-out labels. Among overparameterized learners on 15 noisy datasets, V estimated from two disjoint halves has 60% less regret than holdout validation at the same cost and matches 5-fold cross-validation at a quarter of its cost; at matched cost it also beats selection by conditional mutual information. We characterize when it fails and give remedies. Code: https://github.com/nnikolaou/GLMC

URLs: https://github.com/nnikolaou/GLMC

new FairPGT: Partitioned Graph Transformers for Dual-Level Fair Graph Clustering

Authors: Siamak Ghodsi, Amjad Seyedi, Sahar Tahmasebi, Eirini Ntoutsi, S\"oren Auer

Abstract: Clustering on attributed graphs can produce demographically biased clusters, particularly when communities align with protected groups. Graph transformers can amplify this bias through same-group attention, while global attention limits scalability. We propose FairPGT, a partitioned graph transformer for fair graph clustering. FairPGT restricts attention to METIS partitions and connects them through routing tokens, which divides the quadratic cost of attention by the number of partitions. A graph-aware self-training target guides clustering, with a provably bounded deviation from the standard DEC target. Fairness is imposed at two levels: a mutual-information term promotes demographic parity in cluster assignments, while a normalized cross-group attention ratio (nCGAR) guides attention toward group neutrality through self-distillation. We prove that the nCGAR correction moves attention toward neutrality and establish a condition preventing overshooting. Across seven benchmarks, FairPGT achieves the best clustering performance and statistical parity on the three largest graphs (Credit, Pokec-n, Pokec-z), with a statistical-parity gap approximately ten times lower than DMoN, and is Pareto-dominated on only one dataset. On controlled stochastic block models, FairPGT attains 97% of the derived upper bound on NMI for exactly fair clustering when communities and protected groups coincide. These graphs further show that, under strong alignment, assignment-level fairness alone makes the attention group-heterophilous, whereas attention-level fairness alone improves neutrality without ensuring fair clusters. Code for reproducibility is available at https://github.com/SiamakGhodsi/FairPGT.

URLs: https://github.com/SiamakGhodsi/FairPGT.

new Toaster: Nonlinear Memory from Cascaded Linear Memories and Target Propagation

Authors: Zeman Li, Ali Behrouz, Yuan Deng, Praneeth Kacham, Rami Al-Rfou, Meisam Razaviyayn, Vahab Mirrokni

Abstract: Recurrent memories let language models process long sequences with a fixed-size state. Linear memories such as DeltaNet and KDA store one matrix and take one delta-rule step per token. The product of a chunk of such steps has an exact WY form, so they train fast. Deep memories such as TTT-MLP and Titans store a small MLP, which can hold maps that no matrix can, but their update has no WY form. Toaster bridges the two by building on a known fact: a gradient step on a linear layer is a delta-rule write. It builds a two-layer nonlinear memory from two linear memories with an activation between them. We use target propagation to give the hidden memory its values by projecting the output error back through the second memory. Given a chunk's cross-layer signals, approximated once per chunk, each layer can then run on an unmodified delta-rule kernel. Our default runs this kernel on the output layer and updates the hidden layer once per chunk. Running it on both layers gives a better model in our ablation. At matched backbones and training-token budgets, Toaster has lower loss than KDA at all five tested sizes (0.2B-1.6B non-embedding parameters) on both C4 and PG19. Its C4 perplexity also matches or slightly improves on the attention baseline at every size. At 1.2B it is also below Titans. In a 3:1 hybrid with global attention, the Toaster hybrid has the lowest perplexity of the four hybrids tested, and it is 0.18 (C4) and 0.44 (PG19) below the attention baseline. At the largest size, Toaster's mean three-shot accuracy on 13 tasks is 51.84%, compared with 50.52% for KDA and 50.04% for Titans. In length extrapolation, Toaster's loss stays below KDA's up to 20K tokens (5x the training length). On the 145M backbone, at a hidden width matched to Titans' memory, Toaster takes at most 1.33x KDA's training-step time and is 2.8-5.5x faster than the tested Titans configurations at 32K context.

new Communicative On-Policy Distillation: Distilling Black-Box Teacher Capabilities through Communication for Long-Horizon Tasks

Authors: Lirui Luo, Kelong Mao, Xinwei Yang, Rongqing Li, Luyu Chen, Kieran Wong, Yudong Guo, Xinrui Wang, Jiayin Zhu, Gu Simiu, Sulong Xu, Cong Fang

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) is a powerful paradigm for improving language-model agents on long-horizon tasks, yet its conventional formulation relies on teacher logits that text-only black- box models do not expose. To address this limitation, we introduce Communicative On-Policy Distillation (COPD), which uses natural-language communication as a medium for distilling a stronger model's capabilities into a smaller student. During task execution, the student au- tonomously decides when to consult a black-box teacher and formulates open-ended natural- language requests without predefined question templates or intervention schedules. Successful student-teacher interactions are retained as privileged references to supervise fresh adviser-free student rollouts through on-policy self-distillation, jointly with task-reward reinforcement learning. On CAMG-II, COPD achieves 2.46 times the gain of the strongest black-box OPD baseline. The capabilities it learns through communication generalize to Terminal-Bench 2.1 and SWE-Bench Pro V2, improving task success without adviser access.

new Simpler Tiny Recursive Models

Authors: Torben Glass, Zhengxi Zhang, Paul Swoboda

Abstract: Small models that execute deep looped computational graphs have shown great experimental results on puzzle-like problems like Sudoku, finding shortest paths through mazes, etc. The most well-known one is TRM (tiny recursive model), a simplification of HRM (hierarchical reasoning model). However, the underlying recursion is complicated and involves a high-level and a low-level state, a nested loop recursion, and the network calls with different input and output signatures depending on where we are in the loops. These mechanisms are needed to avoid memorization and overfitting. We propose STRM, a simplified design with a single state and a uniform update. STRM does not sacrifice generalization, improves training speed and final performance. Our key architectural choices that allow our simpler recursion to work are exponential moving updates on the states with varying rates that are jittered during training and readout of the solution on slow-updating channels only. We test STRM on Sudoku-Extreme, PPBench and Maze-Hard with the same data, parameters and compute as TRM: we improve over TRM by $3.8$ points on Sudoku-Extreme and $8.4$ points on PPBench and match it on Maze-Hard. On ARC-AGI-1 and ARC-AGI-2, STRM improves over the published TRM results by $5.4$ and $0.5$ points. We will release our code upon acceptance of the paper.

new Variable Length Diffuse Everything: Arbitrary Length Generative Modeling on Arbitrary State Spaces

Authors: Kevin Rojas, Felix X. -F. Ye, Molei Tao

Abstract: Diffusion and flow-based generative models have achieved remarkable results across diverse applications, but most assume data of fixed-dimension/length. Meanwhile, real-world problems such as layout generation often involve variable-length, multimodal data, such as continuous bounding box coordinates paired with discrete types. Existing methods typically address variable length by padding data to a fixed size or by introducing specialized objectives to model lengths explicitly, adding complexity and limiting generality. We propose Variable Length Diffuse Everything (VL-DE), a simple and general framework for variable-length multimodal generation on arbitrary state spaces. Instead of padding, VL-DE handles variable length through an insertion-based mechanism grounded in a principled theoretical framework, yielding a tractable training objective. In addition, by utilizing the fact that VL-DE is based on a generator with an additive form, we develop a higher-order sampler by appropriately handling the time dependence. Extensive experiments on layout and molecule generation demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach.

new Learning through Discernment: when a small language model learns from user feedback on the job, and when it decays

Authors: Sirisilp Kongsilp

Abstract: Small language models on devices could keep improving by learning, overnight, from their own daily work. Without a true outcome, however, something must decide which attempts are worth learning from, and both the model's own judgment and the user's reaction can be wrong. We study a small model (Qwen3 0.6B and 1.7B) with a LoRA adapter, fine-tuned each night on the attempts a judge accepts, over 127 lives of up to a year on WebShop and $\tau^2$-bench. We compare the true outcome, the user's stated verdict and the model's own judgment with a separate, frozen "feedback sense" that reads the user's reaction, built in three versions. Judging itself, the model improved and then decayed, over a year and whatever its adapter. A sense that took polite give-ups for success collapsed 3 of 5 lives; one built for discernment, which first asks whether the user gave a clear verdict, kept 4 of 5 near the oracle for two weeks, then faded on clean but scarce lessons, to the level of self-judging by day 60. When the user cannot see the outcome ($\tau^2$-bench), the sense read reactions correctly, yet 80% of its lessons were failures the customer had thanked. Across 97 lives, the share of failed attempts among the lessons predicted the outcome (Spearman $\rho$ = -0.81). In our experiments, learning from users held up only where the judge kept failures out and the user could see the outcome; lopsided lessons may also narrow the model.

new HypoLoop: Revising Weight-Editing Plans from Scientific Feedback

Authors: Mengran Li, Xichen Yan, Bo Li, Chengyang Zhang, Jinfeng Xu, Zheng Lin, Zelin Zang, Zhen Lei, Jiebo Luo, Victor C. M. Leung, Stan Z. Li

Abstract: Scientific experiments continually reveal prediction errors. Effective updates depend on which parameters change and which errors drive the correction. We introduce HypoLoop, a family of native weight-editing methods that connects these choices to experimental feedback. Fixed, numerically adaptive, and LLM-guided policies produce editing plans; numerical solvers compute weight patches, and internal validation selects the deployed predictor. Successive observations and validation records support revisions to editable locations and fitting rules. Experiments cover 22 scientific tasks and both DeepSeek and Claude. On Burgers, reallocating a matched parameter budget reduces error by 58.80% and 47.72% relative to fixed locations. On DIODE, positive-residual weighting reduces absolute relative error (AbsRel) by 13.35% in internal cross-validation, with the same 33 editable coordinates. Revising the complete plan across rounds lowers final AbsRel by 10.59% relative to keeping the initial plan fixed. SpaceNet7 and Make3D connect accepted edits to improved spatial predictions. These results show how experimental evidence can shape the update problem itself. Code: https://github.com/limengran98/HypoLoop.

new Toward Out-of-Distribution Generalization in Neural AC Power Flow

Authors: Ojas Sanghi, Anvita Bhagavathula, Alvaro Carbonero, Priya L. Donti

Abstract: Growing uncertainty in power system operations from renewables integration, extreme weather, and data center loads challenges the reliability of traditional power flow solvers at scale. Machine learning-based proxies are a potentially promising alternative, but the prohibitive cost of generating training data for large grids makes out-of-distribution generalization a key capability for deployment; currently, models trained on small grids lack that capability. In this work, we systematically evaluate how physics-informed losses, multi-grid training, and perturbation-based data augmentation improve the zero-shot transfer of three graph neural network-based power flow models from smaller systems to a larger, unseen 500-bus system. We find that (1) physics-informed losses improve transfer relative to purely supervised objectives; (2) multi-grid training on smaller systems matches the best single grid but does not close the gap to target-grid training; (3) perturbation-based augmentation on edge attributes improves transfer by up to 54.6%; and (4) random line/generator dropout does not consistently improve performance.

new Same Rank, Different Needs: Rethinking LoRA Redistribution in Federated Learning

Authors: Jinqian Chen, Jihua Zhu, Chang Liu

Abstract: Federated low-rank adaptation (LoRA) enables clients with different resource budgets to collaboratively fine-tune foundation models. To distribute an aggregated update within each client's rank budget, existing methods commonly retain its leading singular directions. However, directions that best reconstruct the update matrix need not best preserve its responses on a particular client's inputs: a lower-energy direction may matter more to that recipient. We formalize this mismatch and show that matrix-optimal truncation can incur arbitrarily larger response error than another readout of the same rank. Motivated by this result, we propose Receiver-Aware Subspace Adaptation (RASA), a plug-in framework for receiver-aware knowledge transfer. RASA uses compact diagonal input second moments to perform uncentered PCA of receiver-weighted update responses. The resulting readout is globally optimal for the rank-constrained receiver objective and preserves at least as much response energy as spectral truncation under the diagonal metric. RASA also introduces spectral-reference aggregation, which removes the personalized initialization offset while retaining the locally learned increment. Experiments on DomainNet-100, NICO++, GLUE, and SuperNI demonstrate RASA's effectiveness across methods under resource, task, and data heterogeneity. In particular, RASA improves DomainNet-100 accuracy by 3.23 percentage points with FlexLoRA and achieves gains of up to 8.33 ROUGE-L points on seen SuperNI tasks and 4.37 on unseen tasks with modest overhead.

new Interpreting Timestamp Permutations in Dynamic Link Prediction

Authors: Ilia Karpov, Kirill Koloskov, Valeriy Kuleshov, Dmitry Ilvovsky

Abstract: Timestamp permutations are used to test whether dynamic link predictors exploit temporal information, but a performance gap can reflect changes in training, evaluation history, or both. We separate these effects with a crossed diagnostic and measure input exposure: whether altered histories actually change the predictor's inputs. On MOOC, TCL trained with binary cross-entropy gains approximately eight pairwise-accuracy points from original-chronology training under either evaluation history, while changing history at fixed weights gives only 0.03 points. The small history contrast reflects unchanged inputs for 68.37% of queries, stable rankings for most others, and cancellation of gains and losses. Under original evaluation history, suppressing explicit time encoding reduces the training contrast from 8.03 to 3.64 points, a paired reduction of 4.39 points (95% interval [3.68, 5.09]) across six seeds. DyGFormer shows corresponding training and history contrasts of 22.34 and 0.38 points across two seeds, despite greater exposure. Partial history permutations further separate nominal intervention strength, input changes, and aggregate accuracy. These comparisons turn a single permutation gap into interpretable statements about training conditions and model responses, while leaving the internal mechanisms of temporal learning open.

new When Normal-Only Updates Are Not Enough: Test-Time Adaptation in Reconstruction-Based Time-Series Anomaly Detection

Authors: Jianhuan Mao, Mengxiao Zhu, Yue Pei, Lei Li, Haogang Zhu

Abstract: Normal operating conditions can evolve after deployment, motivating test-time adaptation (TTA) of time-series anomaly detectors using incoming data. Yet the same stream may contain anomalies, so TTA with online updates must decide which test samples should be used for adaptation. This leaves a more basic question: even if updates are restricted to samples labeled as normal in the benchmark, does TTA reliably improve anomaly detection? We study this question with a controlled intervention that replaces the practical update selector with a rule that uses only samples labeled as normal in the benchmark, while keeping the rest of the online adaptation procedure unchanged. Across nine benchmark families, four optimizer configurations, and five random seeds, we obtain three main findings: (1) using only samples labeled as normal in the benchmark does not reliably improve detection; (2) practical TTA failures exhibit two distinct responses to this intervention, with 63 of 180 settings recovering a clear detection gain while 90 still show no clear detection gain; and (3) the updates reduce their immediate reconstruction objective across the evaluated settings, yet these reductions do not consistently lead to better anomaly ranking. Both failure patterns persist across alternative practical adaptation variants and modest perturbations of the benchmark labels. Together, these findings show that improving sample selection alone is not a complete solution to reconstruction-based TSAD TTA.

new A Systematic Analysis of a Small Optimal Subspace for Physics-Informed Neural Networks: $n$-DAS $\subset \mathbb{R}^p$

Authors: Ahmad AliAhmad, Khemraj Shukla

Abstract: The search for a low-dimensional subspace that governs neural network training has been a persistent goal in optimization. Yet subspaces defined by Hessian eigenvalue magnitude, such as the dominant/bulk decomposition underlying Dom-SGD and Bulk-SGD, have repeatedly failed to capture where optimization takes place, and adaptive Krylov-based alternatives built on energy-fraction thresholds show promise but still struggle at lower eigenmodes. We argue this failure is structural: the directions of highest curvature are not, in general, the directions along which training moves. We introduce the dominant aligned subspace (DAS), constructed from a full eigendecomposition of the Hessian but selected by gradient alignment rather than eigenvalue magnitude, as a tool for understanding optimization dynamics and a foundation for designing methods that exploit them. To expose this structure under the hardest conditions, we use physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) as a testbed: differential operators from the parabolic, elliptic, and hyperbolic classes induce severely ill-conditioned, anisotropic loss landscapes. Across Poisson, heat, wave, and viscous Burgers benchmarks DAS-restricted training with a line search matches full-space training even where the projected Hessian remains ill-conditioned, showing empirically that convergence does not require all Hessian eigendirections but only a low-dimensional, gradient-aligned subset. We extend the analysis to Gauss-Newton within the DAS and discuss what this structure implies for future subspace-aware optimizers.

new TabBioMed: A Large-Scale Benchmark for Biomedical Tabular Learning

Authors: Pau Mateo-Bernad\'o, Saivenkata Nagavyjayanthi Polapragada, David Bonet, Pol Arbiol Rakuljic, Laia Mogas Pladevall, Mar\c{c}al Comajoan Cara, Jesus Gonzalez-Ferrer, Jordi Abante, Daniel Mas Montserrat, David Haussler, Alexander G. Ioannidis

Abstract: Recent advances in machine learning have accelerated biomedical prediction, but evaluation on structured biomedical data remains fragmented, especially for datasets with high dimensionality, large sample size, missingness, and class imbalance. TabBioMed unifies these datasets through a standardized, dataset-aware preprocessing and evaluation framework, enabling reproducible comparison across classification, regression, and multi-target tasks. We benchmark 23 models across classical baselines, gradient-boosted trees, neural tabular architectures, and tabular foundation models. Foundation models deliver the strongest family-level predictive performance. However, their gains are regime-dependent: tree-based models and tuned multilayer perceptrons remain highly competitive, often dominating under inference-time or training-cost constraints. Overall, TabBioMed shows that biomedical tabular learning is strongly context-dependent, with optimal model choice depending on data regime, task type, and computational budget. We release TabBioMed with curated datasets and splits, preprocessing code, baselines, and results, providing a reproducible benchmark for practical model selection and future methods development in biomedical machine learning, available at https://github.com/AI-sandbox/TabBioMed.

new CAFE-AQI: Coverage-Aware Day-Ahead AQI Forecasting with Early Warning and Conformal Uncertainty in Bangladesh

To prevent duplicate records from dominating training and evaluation, identical trajectories were weighted. new Online State-Representation Adaptation under Synthetic Interaction-Cue Remapping

Authors: Bin Yin

Abstract: When cues change what they predict, continuing to update a learned mapping may help an agent choose the appropriate action. We tested this in a one-step synthetic task by comparing a logistic state predictor that continued to learn from state labels with the same predictor frozen at transfer. new Beyond Cross-Modal Reconstruction: Participant-Level Evaluation of sEMG-Kinematic Coupling in Post-Stroke Gait

Abstract: Accurate cross-modal reconstruction is often treated as evidence of physiological coupling, although it may simply reflect temporal patterns shared across participants.
