Together, these findings identify a reasoning-related region whose weights improve reasoning with low interference in the host's existing mathematical competence.

new Choosing the Right Representations: Task-Aware Fusion for Multimodal Materials Language Models

Authors: Huu Binh Ta, Alex Morehead, N. Benjamin Erichson, Ferdinando Fioretto, Sandeep Madireddy

Abstract: Multimodal language models for materials science rely on effective mechanisms for incorporating three-dimensional structural representations. MatterChat addresses this challenge using a large bridge module between a pretrained structural encoder and the LLM, together with a separate contrastive alignment stage. We instead ask whether this bridge can adaptively select and combine representations from multiple structural encoders according to the target property. We find that supervised and generative material encoders provide complementary information for property prediction. We found that, for Zatom-1, this information is not concentrated in the final layer. Instead, the most informative representations occur at different depths depending on the target property. This motivates Task-Conditioned Attention Fusion (TCAF), a lightweight attention-based bridge that uses task-conditioned latent queries to combine CHGNet features with intermediate Zatom-1 representations. The bridge is trained directly on downstream prediction tasks, allowing representation selection and material-language alignment to be learned jointly without separate contrastive pretraining. Across classification and regression tasks, TCAF outperforms MatterChat, with up to 47.8% lower RMSE, while using substantially fewer trainable parameters and halving the overall training time. Further experiments incorporating UMA-S demonstrate TCAF's flexibility across encoder combinations, with the three-encoder configuration achieving the highest macro-average classification accuracy of 89.61%.

new Equal Steps, Unequal Curvature: Where Muon's Update Goes Wrong

Authors: Biswa Sengupta, Jinhua Wang, L\'eo Maxime Brunswic

Abstract: Muon updates each hidden weight matrix along the polar factor of its momentum, the steepest-descent direction under the spectral norm. That choice fixes two allocations without consulting the loss: every singular direction of the momentum receives the same step, and in a non-square matrix each neuron's step is set by its leverage score. Whether they are good is a question about curvature, which steepest-descent arguments do not answer; we measure it. Using held-out gradients and Hessian-vector products, we decompose Muon's step by singular-value band and by neuron group and compute each group's second-order optimal multiplier. In a 124M-parameter decoder trained on FineWeb-Edu, the multipliers rise monotonically from the top singular directions to the tail: Muon over-steps the high-curvature top of its spectrum. We prove in Lean when such a diagnostic licenses an intervention at Muon's own update norm. On the measured curvature, the criterion predicts, and held-out losses confirm, that moving step length off the top helps mid-training and early in the cooldown, but not at its end. We then test interventions under per-step norm matching, tuned baselines and paired seeds. Orthogonalizing Q, K and V separately rather than as one fused matrix lowers the loss by 0.0250 nats and accounts for all of the gain of block-wise variants. A matmul-only, rotation-equivariant spectral tilt $\sigma^{-1/4}$ applied in the cooldown lowers the loss of this stronger baseline by a further 0.0120 nats, additively with NorMuon (0.0158 nats together, three seeds; the baseline needs about 4.3% more tokens to match), and by 0.0188 nats at 355M parameters (three seeds). Energy-matched Schatten-1 weight decay adds a further 0.0052 nats. Geometrically principled changes that do not act on the measured miscalibration, namely exact polar factors, steepest-descent input whitening and top-heavy decay, do not help.

new Zonotopic Analysis of Quantization Error in Neural Networks

Authors: Robert Debbas, \'Eric Goubault, Sylvie Putot

Abstract: Quantized neural networks achieve computational efficiency by reducing precision, but bounding their quantization error remains a challenge due to discrete rounding and clamping operations. We present a zonotope-based abstract interpretation framework for quantized ReLU feed-forward network verification, introducing a unified round-clamp abstract transformer that jointly handles both operations. We extend this framework to constrained zonotopes, which add linear constraints to tighten bounds, and derive sound quantization error representations. We establish soundness and affine-strip optimality of the round-clamp abstract transformer, as well as convex-hull optimality of its complete constrained formulation. On the ACAS Xu benchmarks, our constrained zonotope method reduces average error-bound widths by approximately 70-85% and runtimes by 65-80% compared to QEBVerif's symbolic DRA propagation at the two smallest tested perturbation radii (r=3 and r=6). These improvements are not uniform across bitwidths, and symbolic DRA achieves tighter bounds at larger radii. Our plain zonotope method delivers competitive sound bounds nearly 300 times faster on average.

new Stealing Exact Training Data from Other Clients during Federated Learning

Authors: Omkar Shrikanth, Soham Dambalkar, Parag Jyoti Pal, Advik Raj Basani, Arnab K. Paul

Abstract: Malicious adversaries can employ gradient inversion attacks to reverse-engineer private data using the model updates of a round in Federated Learning. While there have been developments in gradient inversion for text, exact text recovery by a malicious client has so far remained infeasible, as FedAvg entangles the gradient contributions of many clients and obscures the signal of any individual sentence. We introduce a novel attack pipeline that unwinds this entanglement progressively through an iterative loop, in which the gradient contributions of sentences recovered exactly in one round are subtracted from the aggregate, exposing the remaining sentences for recovery in the next iteration. We show, for the first time, exact sentence recovery, with 100% precision, and on aggregates containing up to $4\times$ more tokens than the model's hidden dimension, we recover up to 80.2% of client sentences exactly, a regime where existing attacks recover none exactly.

new Openhagen Worlds: Calibrated Environments for Measuring and Training Scientific Discovery Agents

Authors: Alexander Boesgaard Lorup

Abstract: Agents that do science must choose informative experiments and reason from their results, but most discovery benchmarks cannot separate that skill from recall, unstated scoring rules or shortcut policies. We introduce Openhagen Worlds, a suite of 27 procedurally generated environments (some with finite truth spaces), from classic puzzles of experimental reasoning to simulator-grounded physics and chemistry, in which an agent runs experiments under a budget and commits an answer that a deterministic verifier checks against a hidden truth. Every observation states the complete scoring rule, and each of the 178 difficulty levels is calibrated: it is admitted only if, on 64 development instances, a scripted oracle succeeds on at least 0.75, the strongest plausible non-deductive policy on at most 0.25 and each gated shortcut on at most 0.35. The 92 core levels span all 27 families and are sized to train small models; the 86 frontier rungs extend the 15 discovery worlds with harder questions and with campaigns of three, five and eight or more committed stages on one hidden system. On the same 84 withheld frontier instances, one per family and rung, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash solves 32 at its default reasoning effort with 16,384 tokens per call and 57 at its maximum effort with up to 393,216 tokens per call (oracle: 79); the headroom that remains is largest in eight-stage campaigns. On-policy expert iteration gives a 9B model modest gains: its core-tier success on trained families rises from 0.090 to 0.148, partly through better action format, with no detectable loss on GPQA Diamond. A hosted service plays the core tier's withheld instances.

new Meta-LSQ: A Closed-Form Sobolev Inner Solver for Few-Shot Meta-Learning

Authors: Antonin Sulc

Abstract: Gradient-based meta-learners such as model-agnostic meta-learning (MAML) adapt to a new task with an inner loop of gradient descent that has to be differentiated through, and they meta-learn every network weight. Meta-LSQ instead fits each task with one regularised least-squares solve over a fixed random Fourier basis and meta-learns only three scalars, a bandwidth, a ridge and a derivative weight. Since the derivative of a sinusoid is again a sinusoid, the same solve can also take derivative observations, and the fitted model has exact closed-form derivatives of any order. On 10-shot sinusoid regression Meta-LSQ trains in 14 s and ends with a 5.5 times lower error than a first-order MAML baseline that trains 36 times longer, and a Gaussian-process control suggests that most of this gain comes from sharing one kernel across tasks. In simulated beam tuning at DESY's ARES facility with model derivatives it is slightly more accurate than an ALPaCA-style model and well ahead of gradient descent. Over DESY's full range of target beams, again in simulation and from measurements alone, its best setting has a median error of 7.3 $\mu$m, against 14.4 for the published final beams of their reinforcement-learning agent, which has the lower median on their 300 trials of small targets. On real sensor-calibration data a learned prior mean puts it ahead of MAML on two of four targets, but an exact Gaussian process with the same change and an ALPaCA-style model stay more accurate on most targets, and it breaks down on non-smooth targets. On noiseless targets outside the span of the basis, in-sample criteria such as generalised cross-validation choose too small a ridge.

new How Generative Models Generate: A Concise Guide to the Generative AI Landscape

Authors: Tianhua Chen

Abstract: The rapidly expanding generative AI landscape brings together methods that differ in architecture, learning objective, mathematical representation and sampling procedure. A concise, selectively organised technical guide can provide an accessible starting point for developing a coherent understanding of this increasingly complex landscape. This article takes a generation-centred approach, connecting how models produce new observations with the modelling and learning principles that make generation possible. We develop a two-level perspective: first, distinguishing three recurring generation routes-direct transformation or decoding, sequential conditional construction, and iterative updating or transport; and second, examining the modelling and learning principles underlying these routes. Representative approaches are introduced through their key components, learning objectives and sampling procedures. We then examine their broader connections, including how different architectures and generation mechanisms can overlap or be combined. By bringing together essential technical explanations within a compact conceptual framework, the article aims to help readers develop a working understanding of unfamiliar approaches, recognise connections among models they have encountered, and navigate different approaches within the broader generative AI landscape.

new Approximate Dynamic Programming with Basis Function Learning

Authors: Mohammadreza Fathi, Chi-Guhn Lee

Abstract: This work presents a novel approach for approximating the value function in Markov decision processes (MDPs). Our approach is grounded in the approximate linear programming (ALP) framework, wherein basis functions (BFs) play an important role. Traditionally, BFs have been pre-defined, randomly generated, or restricted to specific structures, which both limits the accuracy that can be achieved and leaves no mechanism for improving a given approximation. We instead learn BFs from the model itself, by repeatedly applying the Bellman operator to the current value estimate. We introduce the Bellman operator basis function (BOBF) learning method, and prove that each learned BF improves the approximation by a quantifiable amount and that the method converges to the optimal value function. Its shrunken counterpart (SBOBF) retains this guarantee while capping the number of BFs, and therefore the computational effort per iteration. For large-scale problems, where the number of constraints is prohibitive, we develop hybrid variants (HBOBF and SHBOBF) that combine constraint sampling with BF learning, and we show that the improvement and finite-termination guarantees are preserved under explicitly stated conditions. Numerical experiments on (continuous) perishable inventory and queueing control problems demonstrate that our BF learning techniques significantly outperform existing methods in the literature.

new Uneven gains in protein-RNA affinity and mutation-effect prediction

Authors: Shangkun Sima, Tongfei Shen, Tianchi Lu

Abstract: Accurate matched affinities determine mutation effects, but gains in aggregate affinity prediction may transfer unevenly to average effects, ordering and local accuracy. We evaluated eleven method families using method-specific matched populations. Controlled CoPRA reduced absolute-affinity mean absolute error from 1.2898 to 1.1431 pKd. Across 220 mutation pairs from 20 families, it generated stronger responses and improved mean-effect accuracy, with uncertain ranking gains and no decrease in mean centered local error. In an independent external test (24 quantitative pairs from three source-matched families), absolute and ordinary mutation-effect errors fell in all three families. Mean centered local error fell from 0.4198 to 0.3761 pKd, with improvements in two families and higher error in one. Error decomposition localized gains in landscape means. PRA-Pred feature tracing, fixed-weight M2-PRNet input controls and matched supervision interventions identified specific restrictions on mutation response. These results establish the need to evaluate average effects, ordering and quantitative local variation jointly.

new Variance-Optimal Counterfactual Policy Design for Sample-Efficient Online A/B Testing

Authors: Shashank Gupta, Subhadeep Maji

Abstract: Online A/B testing is the standard approach for evaluating changes to user-facing systems, such as a new recommendation or ranking policy, before deployment. While the difference-in-means estimator of a standard A/B test is unbiased, it often suffers from high variance. When the treatment and production policies choose the same actions with similar probabilities, the resulting observations can add noise while providing little information about the difference between the policies. Consequently, A/B tests can require large amounts of traffic and long durations to detect small treatment effects. Existing variance-reduction methods mainly improve the estimator after the data-collection scheme has been fixed, and recent overlap-aware designs only optimize a single traffic split between the two policies; thereby, they cannot direct traffic to the actions on which the policies disagree. In this paper, we jointly optimize how actions are sampled and how the resulting observations are used to estimate the treatment effect. Our contributions are two-fold. First, building on pairwise off-policy evaluation, we derive the variance-optimal action-level logging policy for estimating the average treatment effect between two policies, which has a closed-form solution for any fixed control variate. Second, we use historical logs to jointly optimize the logging policy and a control variate to further reduce variance. Our experimental results in simulated policy A/B tests show that our designs estimate the average treatment effect more accurately than existing variance reduction methods on every evaluated setting, while generally improving statistical power and reducing how often the worse policy is selected. As a result, our designs enable practitioners to reach reliable deployment decisions with considerably less online traffic.

new Elysium: Simulation-Free Unbalanced Schr\"odinger Bridges

Authors: Zihan Wang, Jianzhe Li, Zhenyi Zhang, Peijie Zhou

Abstract: Reconstructing cellular dynamics from snapshots is challenging because the destructive nature of single-cell measurements makes tracking the same cells over time impossible. Cellular dynamics are inherently stochastic and involve changes in population size through proliferation and apoptosis. Growth-based methods represent population change as a rate on the density, which does not assign exits and entries to individual cells. Unbalanced Schr\"odinger bridges model individual events, but neural solvers for killed diffusion bridges simulate trajectories during training. We introduce Elysium, a simulation-free solver that jointly learns state transition and mass change. Using a killed Brownian reference and its time reversal, Elysium constructs direct regression objectives from an optimal endpoint coupling and conditional bridge distributions, without simulating trajectories during training. We show that the minimizers of the expected training objectives recover the optimal unbalanced Schr\"odinger bridge. Empirically, Elysium reconstructs state transitions, population size, and event distributions on simulated benchmarks with known killed-diffusion ground truth, outperforming existing deterministic and bridge-based solvers. On single-cell time courses, it interpolates cell state distributions at unobserved time points, tracks population expansion and contraction, and captures biphasic drug-response dynamics.

new Horizontal Flow Matching for Single-Cell Spatiotemporal Dynamics

Authors: Zihan Wang, Jianzhe Li, Zhenyi Zhang, Peijie Zhou

Abstract: Learning the spatiotemporal dynamics of cells from spatial transcriptomics requires connecting snapshots of different cells acquired in different coordinate frames. A rotation or translation of a section can appear as cell movement and distort the inferred correspondence. We introduce horizontal flow matching, which learns changes in tissue organization, molecular state, and cell abundance up to shared rigid motion. The dynamics follow a continuity--reaction equation whose action assigns no cost to shared rigid motion, and the resulting horizontal projector removes this component from spatial prediction errors during learning and from predicted velocities during generation. An equivalent endpoint problem jointly estimates correspondence and relative pose in Wasserstein--Fisher--Rao geometry and yields analytic paths whose velocity and growth serve as regression targets. Regressing these targets recovers the horizontal dynamics under ideal population conditions. Our model, HORIZON, uses an equivariant transformer to predict velocity and growth from the current tissue, so that a tissue can be generated continuously from a single initial snapshot. On a simulated tissue observed in perturbed coordinate frames, alignment improves reconstruction and projection reduces rigid drift. On four spatial transcriptomics time courses, HORIZON reconstructs tissue geometry, gene expression, and cell abundance more accurately than existing spatial and unbalanced trajectory methods, and interpolates held-out developmental stages.

new Counterfactual World Models: JEPAs That Reason About "What If"

Authors: Randall Balestriero, Yann LeCun, Kerda Varaku

Abstract: World models operate in a world they cannot fully observe. When a hidden disturbance, such as a current pushing a boat, changes the outcome of an action, a counterfactual question follows: what would have happened under a different action, subject to the same disturbance? Its answer separates bad decisions from bad luck. We prove that world models trained on factual transitions cannot answer it on their own, whatever their data or capacity. For many physical systems, the observed outcome and a simple property of the dynamics suffice: natura non facit saltus, nature makes no leaps. When actions and disturbances act as separate forces, their interaction is second-order in time, so the counterfactual outcome is, to first order, the observed outcome plus the difference between the world model's predictions under the alternative and factual actions. This yields a counterfactual world model with no counterfactual data, extra parameters, or retraining. For transitions of duration $h$, we prove an $O(h^2)$ bound on the counterfactual error relative to the disturbance variance, establish a matching minimax lower bound from unpaired factual data, and characterize failures at discontinuities, coordinate folds, and disturbances that scale the command. We validate these predictions from pixels with JEPA world models on frozen DINOv2 and masked autoencoder (MAE) encoders. On four rendered simulators, our approach improves counterfactual $R^2$ from $38$--$46\%$ to $86$--$96\%$ with the same world models; on a fifth, an underwater vehicle, it reaches $90$--$94\%$ with sixteen times more factual data. Four further frozen encoders, DINOv3, SigLIP~2, CLIP and EVA-02, give $81$--$95\%$ on the same simulators. In hindsight planning, it finds the action that would have reached the goal in $35$--$69\%$ of events, against $14$--$23\%$ for an oracle planner that knows the simulator but not the outcome.

new Reconstruction vs. Prediction Pre-Training of Discrete Video Representations for Continuous Control

Authors: Ron Kosova, Francesco Croce

Abstract: Self-supervised video encoders are commonly trained to either reconstruct pixels or predict latent features, yet it remains unclear how these objectives shape representations for downstream dynamics. We present a capacity-matched study isolating this choice: two identical video encoders with a shared FSQ bottleneck are trained under autoencoding and V-JEPA objectives on a continuous control environment. We introduce a simple asymmetric discretization scheme to apply the same bottleneck to the predictive model. The resulting representations exhibit a clear trade-off: the autoencoder preserves substantially more physical state information and achieves slightly better one-step latent prediction, while the predictive encoder accumulates less error under autoregressive rollout. Structural probes suggest that this advantage is associated with greater robustness to low-level visual perturbations and stronger dependence on temporal context. These results indicate that predictive and reconstructive objectives induce distinct representations of dynamics, trading state fidelity for properties that may benefit long-horizon prediction.

new Learning Trajectories Leave Decodable Traces in Neural Network Weight Space and Internal Representations

Authors: Patrick Krauss, Achim Schilling, Andreas Maier, Thomas Kinfe, Claus Metzner

Abstract: Neural networks trained on the same task develop related weight-space structures, yet remain distinguishable after learning. Do these differences merely reflect alternative solutions, or do they preserve information about individual learning histories? We tested whether final weights reveal initialization, task order, and earlier trajectory states. Training reorganized the ensemble: initial pairwise distances did not consistently predict final distances. Nevertheless, all 30 trained networks were correctly matched to their initial weights among ten candidates per dataset. Networks sharing an initialization also developed recognizable final hidden representations across different batch schedules, although direct comparisons with initial activity did not recover identity. Learning order remained readable as well. A linear probe decoded the order of two earlier tasks with 100% accuracy on 80 held-out networks after ten epochs of a common final task. Earlier intermediate weight states were partly predictable, but individual initial weights could not be reliably reconstructed. Final weights thus retain selective historical information rather than a complete trajectory record. Perturbed networks recovered classification performance without returning to their unperturbed training paths. This separation persisted across datasets, intervention times, and network layers. Moreover, 2,157 of 2,160 aligned endpoint pairs showed no positive loss barrier at sampled interpolation points, indicating low-loss connections between distinct solutions rather than convergence to one weight vector. Together, these findings show that a shared task constrains networks to related successful solutions without erasing their individual histories. Final weights reflect not only what a network has learned, but also where learning began and how it unfolded.

new Bias-Only Training Dynamics in ReLU Networks

Authors: Shreyas Chaudhari, Jose M. F. Moura

Abstract: Neural networks with randomly initialized, fixed weights and trainable biases are universal approximators, yet the dynamics of bias-only training remain largely unexplored. We study these dynamics in one-hidden-layer ReLU networks through activation codes, the binary patterns of active neurons that partition the input space into convex polyhedral regions. We show that the gradients with respect to the biases depend on each input only through its activation code, and hence the training dynamics can be characterized with these codes. In particular, as long as the activation codes remain fixed, training reduces to a linear least-squares problem with the codes as features. Training can then be understood as a sequence of least-squares problems, with transitions occurring when neurons change their activation states. We show that the fraction of switching neurons vanishes as the network widens, and the training dynamics approach those of linear least squares on the initial activation codes in the infinite-width limit. Our experiments support these theoretical findings, showing that while activation switches can noticeably affect training in narrower networks, their relative frequency and the associated changes in the dynamics diminish as the width increases.

new Prognosis Without Prediction: The Intrinsic Geometry of Survival

Authors: Atahan Karagoz

Abstract: Modeling systemic failure in high-dimensional environments is fundamentally limited by supervised algorithms that attempt to predict sparse, noisy or censored endpoints. To break this reliance on explicit outcome regression, we engineered the Self-Organizing Survival Manifolds (SOSM) framework, a deep learning architecture that decouples unsupervised representation learning from survival labels and treats time-to-event progression as an emergent geometric property. The system projects heterogeneous multi-omics arrays into a unified latent space governed by a pseudotime-weighted, curvature-minimizing graph Laplacian, organizing patient trajectories along low-dissipation geodesic flows. To validate the framework, we applied this architecture to a 184-patient clear cell renal cell carcinoma multi-omics cohort. Across a deterministic 5-fold cross-validation pipeline, SOSM achieved a mean Concordance Index of $0.768 \pm 0.068$, while out-of-fold risk stratification yielded significant survival divergence (log-rank $p < 0.001$) accompanied by a 14.7% increase in local manifold dissipation among high-risk patients. Furthermore, interrogating the network's encoder weights confirmed that the model autonomously recovered canonical metabolic and microenvironmental drivers of disease progression without supervised feature selection. By demonstrating that systemic failure can be mapped as a geometric phase transition, SOSM establishes a generalizable computational engine for prognostic modeling across biological, industrial and financial complex systems.

new Synthesizing hyperspectral Data using generative Models to train spectral Unmixing Methods for low-cost Crop Residue Cover Mapping

Authors: Ege Artan, Shuo Chen, Andrew Peng, Sammuel Aldrich Karya, Kyaw Thiha

Abstract: The spectral range of the Field Imaging Nanosatellite for Crop residue Hyperspectral Mapping (FINCH), developed by the University of Toronto Aerospace Team's Space System Division, poses significant challenges for hyperspectral unmixing to determine crop residue cover fractional abundances. The severe lack of standard indices necessitates the use of complex, data-driven unmixing models. Complex unmixing models require dense, well-distributed manifolds, whereas ground-truth datasets are sparse and expensive. To better leverage the information content in existing ground-truth datasets, this study presents a framework that decouples interpolation and abundance-mapping estimation via an intermediary conditional data generator, contrasting with traditional single-model unmixing pipelines. This decoupling enables the inclusion of physical prior knowledge about spectra, which is not natively accessible to unmixing models, thereby artificially expanding the dataset to serve as a Monte Carlo approximation of the population risk. To test this hypothesis, we have proposed two generator models: a 1D Conditional U-Net with Conformer Layers (GD-Streamline) and a Dual-Path Transformer Conditional Variational AutoEncoder (TCVAE), and two unmixing models: Multi-Layered Perceptrons (MLP) and Fourier Neural Operators (FNO). The results of the study indicate a need for rigorous integration and introduction of spectral priors within the scope of the proposed decoupling framework to prevent domain and mode collapse and hallucination by the generator models; otherwise, this leads to falsely approximated data manifolds, ultimately resulting in unsatisfactory and out-of-distribution unmixing performance.

new Overlooked and Underutilized: Prototypes are the Missing Piece in Tabular XAI

Authors: Jacek Karolczak, Jerzy Stefanowski

Abstract: Explainable AI (XAI) is frequently applied to high-stakes tabular data, yet the field relies heavily on feature attribution methods. While these approaches offer technical transparency, evaluating dozens of isolated features demands excessive cognitive effort from non-expert users. In this paper, we argue that prototypes remain overlooked and underutilized, and should be integrated as a complementary, intuitive gateway to auditing complex machine learning models. Although entire exemplars function as an intuitive baseline for prototype-based explanations, we highlight prototypical parts as particularly valuable for tabular data. By grouping related features into bounded subspaces, prototypical parts technically embody human-understandable concepts, replacing isolated features with cohesive semantic units. We support this integration through two primary lenses. Cognitively, these parts align with Rosch's categorization theory and Kahneman's System 1 thinking to naturally reduce cognitive load for humans. Then, we introduce a Geometric Validity Triad to conceptualize why prototypes validate the data manifold - a critical dimension often neglected by existing methods. We contend that making tabular XAI genuinely comprehensible requires shifting toward composite explanation pipelines that pair intuitive similarity-based reasoning with rigorous feature-level auditing. The paper concludes by outlining open challenges of prototype-based methods and discussing potential solutions to guide future research.

new What Survival Benchmarks Don't Tell You: Impact of Model Selection and Dataset Regimes

Authors: Sandra Dening, Emre Kavak, Christian Wachinger, George H. Chen

Abstract: Despite the proliferation of survival analysis methods, current benchmarks often operate under narrow conditions: they heavily rely on the concordance index (C-index) for evaluation and model selection, and frequently either exclude deep learning (DL) methods or underrepresent challenging dataset regimes. To address these limitations, we conduct a large-scale, neutral benchmark comparing 10 classical, tree-based, and DL methods across 49 diverse datasets. Crucially, we treat the model selection criterion (optimizing for C-index, Integrated Brier Score (IBS), or Mean Absolute Error (MAE-PO)) as an independent experimental variable. Using Bayesian mixed-effects modeling, we demonstrate that evaluation design heavily dictates conclusions. For complex DL and tree-based architectures, selecting hyperparameters to optimize for IBS instead of C-index yields substantial improvements in probabilistic accuracy and calibration at a negligible cost to discriminative ranking. Furthermore, method performance (C-index, IBS, MAE-PO, and X-CAL) is fundamentally governed by dataset characteristics. While DL models scale exceptionally well with larger sample sizes, they suffer notable performance drops in high-dimensional feature spaces. Additionally, we expose a structural vulnerability where heavy censoring artificially inflates absolute calibration metrics, creating overly conservative tests. Ultimately, our findings challenge the utility of global single-number performance summaries and advocate for survival analysis results that transparently report the evaluation setup. Our code is available at: https://github.com/sdening/surv-bench-study

URLs: https://github.com/sdening/surv-bench-study

new Measuring and Improving Transfer of Neural Surrogates for Vehicle Aerodynamics

Authors: Favour Nerrise, Julian Busch, Gurpreet Singh Hora, Martin Sipka, Danielle C. Maddix, Georgia Olympia Brikis

Abstract: Neural surrogates for external vehicle aerodynamics are fast alternatives to physics-based computational fluid dynamics (CFD) simulations. Their foundational limitation is a lack of zero-shot generalization across vehicle classes, which results in an expensive, custom training campaign for each new vehicle design. Similar to pretrained models in other fields, a pretrained surrogate should adapt to a new vehicle class using only a few simulations and stay accurate on its pretraining domain. No prior study has systematically tested this transfer behavior. We conduct a large-scale, controlled benchmarking study on five DrivAerML-pretrained surrogates across four independent target datasets. These datasets differ from the source in geometry, CFD solver, turbulence model, and flow conditions. We find that fine-tuning these surrogates outperforms training from scratch in fewer than half of the comparisons and lowers the accuracy of the surrogate on its source dataset. We introduce GeoReplay, a geometry- and gradient-aligned source replay that keeps source simulations resembling the target in training and applies their updates only when they agree with the target gradient. A risk bound separates geometric coverage from differences in the simulated physics, and we prove that every sufficiently small GeoReplay step decreases the target training objective. GeoReplay lowers the source error of fine-tuning in 77 of 80 settings and matches its target accuracy.

new Atomic MathReasoner: Instance-Level Composition of Atomic Reasoning Skills for Mathematical LLMs

Authors: Jiashu Li, Tianyu Gao, Qinglong Liu, Jinping Wang, Ye Wei, Zhiqiang Gao

Abstract: Most existing approaches enhance mathematical reasoning in large language models (LLMs) as a monolithic capability, despite the fact that different mathematical problems require different reasoning skills. Recent work has explored a more fine-grained perspective by decomposing mathematical reasoning into atomic skills and developing specialized modules for individual capabilities. However, how to effectively utilize these atomic abilities remains underexplored. To address this challenge, we propose Atomic MathReasoner (AMR), an instance-level framework for composing atomic reasoning skills. AMR first trains a library of capability-specific LoRA experts and performs gradient-free optimization over held-out validation subsets to discover a diverse set of low-loss composition prototypes. It evaluates the preference of each prototype using the token-level negative log-likelihood of reference solutions, filters ambiguous assignments through a confidence margin, and distills the resulting pseudo-labels into a lightweight router. During inference, the router selects an appropriate composition prototype for each problem, enabling problem-specific utilization of atomic skills without costly online optimization. Experiments on the AtomicMath benchmark across general and mathematics-specialized LLMs demonstrate consistent improvements. On Qwen2.5-Math-Instruct-7B, AMR increases average field accuracy from 64.4% to 68.1% at Level 1 and from 59.3% to 63.7% at Level 2, while improving average logical reasoning performance from 39.4% to 46.8%. These results demonstrate that effectively composing specialized atomic skills provides a promising approach to strengthening mathematical reasoning in LLMs.

new TangentPDE: Learning Local Operator Responses with Sparse Finite-Difference Supervision

Authors: Xinjian Xie, Ruxuan Chen, Jiashu Li, Tianyu Gao, Ye Wei

Abstract: Neural PDE operators are often optimized through state reconstruction, which largely constrains operator values at observed inputs but does not fully characterize their sensitivity to local perturbations. A more faithful approximation of operator behavior requires not only accurate state predictions, but also correct variations along local perturbation directions. We present TangentPDE, which uses finite differences to approximate directional derivatives and constructs task-relevant finite-step responses from the state outputs of a reference solver at sparse paired inputs. Its objective jointly constrains state fidelity, response-direction alignment, and response-magnitude calibration, thereby learning both operator values and their local variations. Because the response labels are constructed solely from state outputs at paired inputs, the method requires neither explicit derivative labels, linearized or adjoint PDE solves, nor full Jacobians, and remains applicable to nonlinear solvers without derivative interfaces. Building on this, we additionally propose a selectively executed adaptation mechanism to strengthen local operator representations under finite-difference supervision. Budget-matched and correspondence-perturbed controls isolate the effect of relational supervision from improvements attributable to additional state observations or increased data volume. Designed as a plug-and-play training framework, TangentPDE integrates with diverse neural-operator backbones. Across four PDE systems and 20 task-backbone settings, TangentPDE lowers the three-seed mean state error in 18 settings and the corresponding Rollout@20 error in 18, while intrinsic finite-step response gains are strongest on DiffReact and remain task- and backbone-dependent elsewhere.

new Auditing black-box clinical prediction models with a large language model through queries alone: a blinded test with planted flaws

Authors: Zhiyu Liu, Xinyu Yang

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are used to explain clinical prediction models, on the promise that they can say what a model computes, find its faults and even reveal new knowledge in it. We tested the three promises where the answers could be checked. From the International Stroke Trial we trained four kinds of model (logistic regression, explainable boosting machine, gradient-boosted trees, neural network) for two outcomes on patients randomised earlier, planted one flaw in each of 24 copies (an under-predicted subgroup, a reversed risk factor or an implausible step) and mixed them with the 8 clean models. GPT-6 Astra audited each model three times, blind, through a tool that only returned the model's predictions; the judging rules were committed before any run and applied by program. Its surrogate models, with a median of 128 terms, reproduced the audited models' log-odds with a mean $R^2$ of 0.976 (0.959 for an additive surrogate of 42 terms fitted by an analyst). It found the planted flaw in 69 of 72 runs (96%; 95% CI 80% to 99%); a script written for the purpose found all 24. It also reported many more problems: 60% of 1,089 checkable reports failed the pre-specified check against the model's average behaviour; post hoc, most of these were real but small steps, and 15% of the checkable reports were not found in the model. Of 601 unexpected relationships it read out of the clean models, 11% held in patients randomised later (random pairs 6%; a regression scan's shorter list of its strongest candidates, 28%); after a check on the training data, 33% against 30%. With query access alone, an LLM can describe a black-box model faithfully and find nearly every planted flaw, as a purpose-written script also did; its reports of problems must be checked on the model and its discoveries on data.

new Monoculture Robust Learning

Authors: Lee Cohen, Jon Kleinberg, Omer Reingold

Abstract: We introduce a notion of monoculture robustness in Probably Approximately Correct (PAC) learning, as a way to mitigate the problem of algorithmic monoculture (Kleinberg and Raghavan, 2021): the tendency of models sharing the same components (e.g., training data) to fail on the same inputs. Specifically, we ask whether a learner with access to a single dataset can output $k$ individually accurate hypotheses whose joint failure probability is comparable to training on $k$ independent datasets. We say that such a learning procedure achieves monoculture robustness, and we quantify this by bounding the conjunctive error that all $k$ hypotheses misclassify points in an individual sense. We design methods for achieving this type of guarantee by drawing a surprising connection between replicability (Impagliazzo et al., 2022) and monoculture robustness. Specifically, we show that if the base learner is $\rho$-replicable, then running it independently $k$ times on the same sample yields individual monoculture error that shrinks exponentially with $k$, controlled by the single-run error of the base learner at the point and $\rho$. We complement these positive results with a lower bound: for individual monoculture robustness, we prove a worst-case lower bound showing that, for learners, driving the individual conjunctive error down to $\gamma$ can require a sample-size multiplier of $t = \Omega(\log(1/\gamma))$.

new Cap-and-Track: Optimizing LLM Inference Request Dispatch Across Shifting Capacity

Authors: Benjamin Novotny, Doug Szeto, Chang Wang, Shicheng Chen, George Zhihua Yuan, Bob Kamma, Michael Ge, John Xin Zhou, Andrea Olgiati

Abstract: A fixed dispatch rate for LLM inference becomes stale as serving fleets resize and request sizes change. Generative output length is unknown at admission, and shared provider pools can lose capacity. We present Cap-and-Track, a design for asynchronous inference across provider capacity and internal GPUs. Meta's traffic shaper implements Cap-and-Track for post and live-video understanding. Refusals signal the backend's current admission ceiling. Durable priority queues hold excess work outside execution, including refused requests for retry. Cap is a fail-fast admission check at the saturated resource that refuses work before a long wait. Using only those refusals, the Track dispatcher probes upward and backs off. It follows the ceiling without output-length prediction or a fleet-level rate to retune. In a concurrent same-traffic production A/B, the shaper ran per-replica Cap plus Track. It served a classifier on 43% fewer H100s than its queued, fixed-rate precursor, though lower-priority work waited longer at backfill peaks. In steady-capacity real-engine replay, Track ran within 1% of the goodput of a fixed rate tuned above measured capacity, with about a quarter of that rate's refusal share. The shaper carries more than 300 million online requests and hundreds of terabytes of media a day. During a backfill, it handled 1.1 billion requests in its busiest 24 hours. Lessons include keeping queues inside inference engines just long enough to fill batches, steering dispatch by capacity refusals alone, keeping dispatch stateful, and sizing fleets by how long low-priority work can wait.

new On Evaluating User Simulators for Interactive Agents

Authors: Hengguang Zhou, I-Hung Hsu, Li Chen, Priyanshi Shrivastava, Anastasios Angelopoulos, Wei-Lin Chiang, Ion Stoica, Cho-Jui Hsieh

Abstract: User simulators offer a scalable way to evaluate interactive agents, but how to assess their quality remains underexplored. In particular, it is unclear whether existing user simulator evaluation frameworks reliably detect meaningful failures in simulated user behavior. This uncertainty makes it difficult to interpret simulator evaluation. To understand what a high benchmark score tells us about simulator quality, we (meta-)evaluate the metrics themselves. To instantiate this meta-evaluation, we deliberately perturb simulated replays to induce failures such as altered goals, omitted constraints, premature stopping, and acceptance of broken work, then test whether the metrics penalize these failures. Evaluation methods from three prior studies detect only 5-6 of the 10 perturbations; their combined scores even favor populations instructed to accept broken work over unmodified controls. Motivated by these gaps, we propose a new evaluation suite that combines utility of user simulators for evaluating agents and two measures of fidelity to real users: population-level fidelity assesses collective interaction patterns, while trace-level fidelity assesses the preservation of individual users' goals, constraints, and persona. Under the same detection criterion, our evaluation suite detects all 10 perturbations. Evaluating six user simulators on 351 real multi-turn sessions in which 348 distinct users across 23 languages and agents work together to create and refine artifacts such as full-stack web apps, games, design assets, and automated workflows, we find that population-level measures can miss when a simulated user pursues the wrong goal, ignores a constraint, or behaves out of character, highlighting the need to assess fidelity at comprehensive granularities.

new Equivariant Modeling with Polynomial Features via Minimal Homogeneous Generators

Authors: Michael Liaofan Liu, Joe Kileel

Abstract: We present a general framework for modeling and learning equivariant functions between finite dimensional semisimple real group representations, unifying constructions developed across many application areas. Our model is a linear combination of equivariant polynomial features whose coefficients are arbitrary learnable functions of invariant polynomial features. We take as features minimal homogeneous generators for the invariant algebra and the covariant module over the invariant algebra, up to prescribed degrees. We provide a Python package that automatically computes these features and fits the resulting models to training data. The algorithm for feature computation requires explicit isotypic decompositions of the input and output representations, along with explicit Clebsch-Gordan decompositions of the tensor product of irreducible representations, and achieves speedups over alternative methods in computational algebra. Numerical experiments in scientific modeling demonstrate the full learning pipeline and yield accurate predictors with few trainable parameters, using the model both for end-to-end prediction and within layers of larger architectures.

cross ConserveEdit: Source-Anchored Transactions for Drift-Bounded Iterative Image Editing

Authors: Shuyang Jiang, Fucheng Deng, Yuchuan Luo, Zhenyu Wu

Abstract: Instruction-driven image editors produce convincing local edits in one pass, but inside an iterative refinement loop every accepted output becomes the next input, so unintended changes outside the requested region accumulate. We present ConserveEdit, an editor-agnostic controller that treats each refinement step as a transaction against a contract compiled once from the source image. The contract separates what may change (the authorized region of each target, with a bounded halo for shadows and reflections) from what must survive (protected content with explicit tolerances). Candidates from a frozen editor are composed through a source-anchored mask and committed only if target progress, drift on protected content, and image quality pass as separate tests; otherwise the step is rolled back and repaired. For any editor, this keeps every pixel outside the authorized region bit-exact and bounds drift independently of the number of iterations, whereas no fixed scalar trade-off reproduces the separate tests. On all 1053 MagicBrush test edits with a frozen Qwen-Image-Edit, masking is necessary but not sufficient. A direct call already alters the rest of the image (12.1 dB PSNR outside the mask); composed through the source-anchored mask, it stays bit-exact there and its DINO similarity to the ground truth rises from 0.67 to 0.85, matching ConserveEdit, so this gain comes from the mask. Next to automatic masks, however, masked direct editing changes a protected face or text on 48% of turns, and masked scalar selection avoids this mostly by abstaining (23% constrained success). Because its drift test names the violated entity, ConserveEdit shrinks the support instead (2% violations, 53% constrained success); in a simulator with known ground truth it raises constrained success from 55.0% to 61.8%. Automatic grounding remains the main bottleneck. Code and logs will be released.

cross What Moves the Ceiling on Automated Audit: from General Code to Scientific Code, Results and Data

Authors: Zhaohe Dong

Abstract: Models increasingly check what other models write. The reading model (confounded with its sampling), one rulebook sentence and the decision rule each move that figure, and much of what is missed is, on a post hoc rating, behaviour the specification never determined. cross Measuring What Matters in the Age of AI: The Research Software Metrics Landscape

Authors: Addi Malviya-Thakur, Reed Milewicz, Gregory R. Watson, Audris Mockus

Abstract: Research software now serves as a primary instrument of science, but the methods the community uses to judge its quality, sustainability, reuse, and impact remain scattered across software engineering, open science, FAIR software, citation, and research-assessment literature. AI-assisted development sharpens the problem: code can be produced at rates that outpace human review capacity, provenance and attribution are harder to track, scholarly credit systems cannot keep pace, and existing metrics inherit assumptions about how research software gets developed that may no longer apply. To aid in navigating these challenges, we present a focused landscape synthesis of research software metrics based on 49 substantive references, organizing the recurring metrics into eight families described by data source, claimed construct, evidence of validity, automation potential, and susceptibility to misuse. Many widely cited research software metrics serve as partial signals but make weak standalone measures of scientific impact. The AI era opens or widens gaps around provenance, maintainability of generated code, reviewer effort, model and data dependencies, reuse and community-health signal inflation, and credit for human labor that AI does not perform. We argue for responsible, contextual, mixed-method use of research software metrics, and offer decision guidance for Research Software Engineers (RSEs), principal investigators, institutions, and funders selecting metrics that support better software, fairer credit, and more trustworthy science. All findings rest on the 49-reference focused review; a reproducible search-and-triage protocol and a 727-record triaged corpus accompany the paper as a transparency artifact for a future full systematic mapping study.

cross Error Analysis of Deep Learning

Authors: Yuling Jiao, Peiying Wu, Jerry Zhijian Yang, Pingwen Zhang

Abstract: This review examines deep learning theory through the lens of error decomposition, systematically addressing approximation, statistical, and optimization errors. We trace the development of error analyses from bounds on individual error components to frameworks that account for all three. Using nonparametric regression and the Deep Ritz Method as dual running examples, we demonstrate how these analyses apply across fundamentally different loss structures. We then review representative analyses in scientific machine learning, generative learning, reinforcement learning, representation learning, and large language models, showing how learning errors affect solutions, distributions, and predictions through problem-specific arguments. Throughout, we clarify which error components are controlled, how they are combined, and what assumptions are needed to obtain guarantees for trained networks.

cross HydroMind: A Checkable Agent for Autonomous Hydrological Research

Authors: Zheng Fang, Yiqun Sun, Simin Qu, Shijie Jiang

Abstract: General-purpose coding agents have written code for hydrological and Earth-system models. In these studies the researchers decided in advance how the model would be evaluated, for example the test period, the scoring function, and the standard the model had to meet. No study has tested what happens when the agent makes these decisions itself: how many of its claims are supported by its own data, code, and results, and whether readers can check them. Both may depend on the agent's harness, that is, the tools it can call and the written instructions it follows. We compared three harnesses: the agent's own harness; this harness plus the modeling tools of HydroMind, a hydrological modeling system we developed for general-purpose agents, which run every model through fixed commands and store its settings, trained model, and predictions; and both plus HydroMind's research procedure, under which the agent writes a research design before running any model and records every change to it. Under each harness, the same agent studied four open questions in hydrological model development and was given only the data. Under the three harnesses, the experiments submitted 23, 20, and 41 claims, each stating that one model or approach is better than, worse than, or similar to another. A claim does not hold if the difference recomputed from the experiment's own code and predictions disagrees with it, or if the code did not compare the two models or approaches the claim names. Under the three harnesses, 30%, 20%, and 5% of the claims did not hold. A statistical test, confidence interval, or decision rule that lets readers judge the stated difference was reported for 48%, 53%, and 83% of the claims. With the language model unchanged, changing only the harness reduced the share of claims that do not hold and increased the share that readers can check.

cross A Differential Theory of Quality-Resource Trade-offs for AI Model Selection: The Multiresource Frontier and Its Minimax Price

Authors: Dmitry Ignatov

Abstract: Comparing AI models across resource budgets yields quality-resource frontiers relating error to latency, parameter count, training compute, and energy. Offset power laws summarize such frontiers, but their fitted parameters reveal neither the price-independent geometry nor the manner in which trade-offs extend across several resource channels. This paper addresses both gaps by developing a differential theory in which this structure is made explicit. Each frontier admits a price-independent plane-curve representation relating the effective number of active resource channels, $N_{\mathrm{eff}} = s^2/\kappa$ (squared slope over curvature), to the remaining log-error depth, $\Delta S = \ln(\varepsilon/\varepsilon_{\infty})$. This representation separates intrinsic shape from the scale and origin of the resource axis. Complete monotonicity and divisibility yield differential inequalities and frontier bounds. When each of $N$ resources contributes one active channel, the theory gives the parameter-free operating rule $N_{\mathrm{eff}} = 1$ in delivered units and derives minimax resource prices $\lambda^{\star}_i = \alpha_i/(N+1)$ with an exact worst-case regret of $\ln(N+1)$ nats. The results make it possible to interpret measured frontiers and compare resource costs without hand-chosen exchange rates. The theory is evaluated on 117 published frontiers and implemented in the open-source package nn-rank, which derives prices from measured quality and cost, ranks candidates, and reports supported choices and minimax regret: https://github.com/ABrain-One/nn-rank

URLs: https://github.com/ABrain-One/nn-rank

cross Prioritize, Don't Decide: What We Learned Auditing a Deployed Human-in-the-Loop CV Sorter

Authors: Hadi Mohammadi, Jeroen Sparla, Alexandros Paliouras, Georg Krempl, Robert A. Bagheri

Abstract: A single academic vacancy can draw hundreds of applications, and the first screening round consumes most of a committee's time. We describe a CV Priority Sorter deployed at a national platform for academic vacancies and integrated with its vacancy database. The pool turned out to be sortable by document length alone, which makes precision-style metrics on it uninformative; regenerating it under a shared length budget removes that shortcut and leaves the reading-depth result standing. The four-level agreement score such systems report, ours included, is a binary probability cut at fixed thresholds, so it follows the classifier's confidence rather than its ranking and falls as labels accumulate. And uncertainty sampling, the obvious way to implement a "suggest the next CV" button, leaves model quality intact but raises the committee's total reading load above the no-label baseline, because the CVs it asks about are the ones that would otherwise be skipped. A fourth result shows where to look next: a hosted LLM embedding model lifts zero-label ordinal discrimination from 0.66 to 0.80 under length control, then stops mattering once the session model has two dozen labels; a local cross-encoder matches that cold-start gain without sending data out. cross A Survey on the Use of Machine Learning within Computer Algebra

Abstract: This survey explores the use of Machine Learning (ML) in the field of Computer Algebra (CA), both to optimise existing CA algorithms, and to perform symbolic computation directly. Traditional symbolic methods, while mathematically rigorous, often are computationally expensive thus limiting their use in real-world applications. Recent advances have shown that data-driven techniques can address these limitations by guiding heuristic decisions, selecting optimal algorithms, predicting structural properties of algebraic objects, or even making direct symbolic computations. We take a systematic literature review approach and uncover work in CA applications including cylindrical algebraic decomposition, Gr\"obner basis computation, symbolic integration, and many more. cross Why Sub-4-Bit Vision CNNs Collapse and Where Mixed-Precision Recovery Stops

Authors: Michael Schroeder

Abstract: Deep vision models operating in resource-constrained environments depend heavily on low-bit quantization, yet compressing weights below 4 bits frequently induces catastrophic accuracy collapse. While mixed-precision recovery strategies aim to rescue accuracy by selectively retaining high-precision parameters, the fundamental mechanics of where and why these models collapse remain poorly understood. In this study, we systematically analyze the anatomy of quantization collapse across standard Vision CNNs (including ResNet and EfficientNet backbones) evaluated on the full 50,000-image ImageNet-1k validation set. First, we map the exact progression of degradation, demonstrating that collapse is highly non-linear and concentrated in late-stage pointwise projections and depthwise layers. Second, we introduce a structured, multi-step recovery waterfall that evaluates selective integer precision (INT4/INT8) and selective floating-point scales. We show that keeping less than 5% of critical layer activations in 8-bit precision recovers up to 92% of the accuracy lost during aggressive sub-4-bit compression. Finally, we bridge the hardware reality gap by evaluating deployment performance on standard ARM and x86 CPU architectures. We show that while low-bit quantization reduces memory storage requirements, standard runtime software bit-unpacking introduces a 1.48x to 21.5x execution slowdown depending on batch size, highlighting a critical trade-off between model footprint and actual edge throughput. Our findings provide a practical blueprint for navigating the limits of sub-4-bit deployment without sacrificing hardware performance.

cross Where Global Context and Weight Sharing Work in Compact Vision Backbones

Authors: Michael Schroeder

Abstract: Compact convolutional backbones such as EfficientNet achieve strong parameter efficiency through local depthwise separable convolutions, yet two structural questions remain central to hybrid design: where does global self-attention improve recognition over pure convolution, and can repeated blocks share weights without losing representational capacity? cross GUARD: Stabilizing Recursive Self-Improvement with Regret Guidance

Authors: Minghe Zhang, Xuetao Kang, Yifei Ming, Yanfei Chen, Han Yu, Yufan Zhuang, Chen-Yu Lee

Abstract: Autonomous AI agents pair a frozen base model with an editable harness of prompts, skills, tool rules, and memory. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) adapts this harness from experience without weight updates, yet self-editing without external graders often backfires: reflection and scalar scores conflate what an edit newly broke with what remains unsolved, causing local regressions, skill forgetting, and memory bloat. We introduce GUARD (Grader-free Unsupervised Environment Design [UED] with Adaptive Rule Distillation), a closed-loop framework for reference-free continual harness evolution. Our key insight is to decompose each task into pre-execution self-checks and guide harness updates via directional regret. Each round grades trajectories against these checks, pinning mastered rules while prioritizing newly broken behaviors, and selects among parallel candidate edits by critic-free group-relative fitness. A Soft-HURT gate then promotes an edit only when gains outweigh regressions, scoping specialized fixes to prevent over-triggering and guaranteeing non-regression on its gated buffer. At test time, one frozen harness runs in a single pass. Across benchmarks on memory, skill evolution, continual task streams, and domain reasoning, GUARD consistently improves performance without catastrophic forgetting or memory pollution while substantially cutting deployment token usage.

cross SpecLook: Speculating Where to Look for Fast and Reliable Agentic MLLMs

Authors: Xi Ding, Jiawei Zhang

Abstract: Agentic multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that think with images interleave reasoning with zoom-in tool calls. However, this sequential loop is slow, and its answers carry no reliability score. Prior speculation of tokens, reasoning steps, or answers keeps the loop, and answer speculation also accepts confident errors. We find that the loop mostly adds one zoom-in step, with which a small model is ahead of the agent on most splits. Our key insight is to speculate the agent's next observation, which, unlike a drafted answer, can be verified, corrected, and used to test the answer. We propose SpecLook, a training-free speculate-and-verify framework with three levels. A small model drafts a glimpse and re-answers from it, and the question ends if the answer is unchanged and confident. Otherwise, the agent's large model verifies and corrects the glimpse without tools, and their answers are fused. Only questions that still lack evidence reach the agent, in one forward pass that SpecLook-Fast skips, so no question runs the loop. The trace scores every answer's reliability without labels. Extensive experiments on V*, HR-Bench, and POPE show that both are more accurate than every baseline on every split. SpecLook reaches 87.2% and 86.2% mean accuracy with 7.7x and 4.7x speedups over the DeepEyes-7B and Thyme-RL agents, and SpecLook-Fast reaches 9.9x and 6.3x.

cross JEPA for astronomy: multi-survey spectral alignment puts stellar parameters on the leading principal axes

Authors: Xiaosheng Zhao, Rosemary F. G. Wyse, Yuan-Sen Ting, Alexander S. Szalay

Abstract: Spectra from different surveys carry different systematics and are not calibrated against one another. Multi-survey foundation models for stellar spectra are then built by aligning embeddings of the same star observed across surveys. Most in astronomy follow the recipes established for language and vision, aligning with a contrastive loss that separates every star from the others. We show that legibility, the ease of reading the information out, is highest for a training objective that never pushes stars apart, and instead constrains only the overall distribution of each survey's embeddings (JEPA). cross Optimal Control of RKHS Diffeomorphic Flows for Classification and Generative Modeling

Authors: Christopher Chukwuemeka, Andreas Mang

Abstract: We develop a unified optimal-control framework that solves supervised classification and likelihood-based density estimation with a single diffeomorphic flow whose velocity lives in a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS); the two tasks share all structure except the objective. Variable projection eliminates the terminal model (a classifier, or a Gaussian-mixture (GMM) reference), leaving a reduced control problem whose gradient comes from an analytic discrete adjoint. The RKHS parameterization makes the flow's divergence exact and deterministic at the cost of one kernel matrix-vector product, avoiding the stochastic trace estimators and potential-flow restrictions of existing continuous normalizing flows. Because the objective and gradient are deterministic, matrix-free reduced Gauss-Newton (GN) and related second-order solvers are practical for both tasks, which we benchmark against a range of first- and second-order optimizers; the learned map is a diffeomorphism with explicitly controlled regularity. In experiments from dimension 2 to 256, the reduced GN solvers use the fewest iterations of any optimizer tested and, together with joint L-BFGS, the least work; a multiscale kernel improves the density fit, and a trainable GMM reference reduces the deformation needed for multimodal transport at comparable likelihood while lowering the likelihood on real data. The full-model solve is quadratic in the particle count and sub-linear in dimension; a control-point reduction makes the particle-count scaling linear at near-lossless accuracy.

cross Balancing Partition Forest for Causal Weighting

Authors: Felipe Angelim, Alessandro Leite

Abstract: Balancing weights are used in observational causal inference to construct a population in which the treatment is independent of observed pretreatment covariates. The ideal weight is the ratio between the product of the covariate and treatment marginals and their joint distribution, for binary and continuous treatments alike. We propose Balancing Partition Tree (BPT) and Balancing Partition Forest (BPF), which estimate this ratio on a learned rectangular partition of covariate--treatment space. Each split maximizes the gain in a Bregman divergence computed from joint and marginal counts. Two choices of divergence yield criteria that maximize the mutual information captured by the partition or reduce the squared error of the weights. We show that the population residual of either criterion bounds the bias of inverse-probability-weighted and doubly robust response estimators that use the population partition weights. Across 17 synthetic and semi-synthetic settings and three response estimators, BPF obtains the lowest mean RMSE rank among seven weight estimators, and a single tree fits in 0.14 seconds on 16,512 observations.

cross Can LLMs Understand What They Predict? Evaluating Latent Causal Structure Inference in Financial Markets

Authors: Yixiang Zheng, Yuechen Jiang, Zhiwei Liu, Jimin Huang, Yuyan Wang, Sophia Ananiadou

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly evaluated through their ability to predict future outcomes, implicitly assuming that predictive success reflects an understanding of the underlying system. However, accurate predictions can often be achieved without recovering the latent mechanisms that generate observable outcomes. To examine this assumption, we introduce LUCID, a benchmark that evaluates whether models can infer latent market structures from event descriptions and historical financial observations. Models generate event-induced causal graphs representing inferred market structure and are evaluated through a reconstruction-based framework. Our evaluation of 6 open-weight and proprietary LLMs shows that adding their generated graphs generally reduces reconstruction quality compared with using historical observations alone, although a few metrics improve. These findings suggest that inferring informative, event-specific market structures from observable evidence remains challenging for the evaluated LLMs.

cross Bounded-Order Non-Identifiability in Latent Structure Block Models: The Fiber in Closed Form

Authors: Sie Hendrata Dharmawan, Peter Chin

Abstract: A graphon is determined by all of its homomorphism densities, but motif-based methods use finitely many. We compute exactly what that truncation costs in a natural family: latent structure block models whose positions lie on $K$ rays, equivalently degree-corrected block models of rank two. For a given radial law, the configurations that agree on every graph with fewer than $2K-1$ vertices form a one-dimensional fiber, which we give in closed form with explicit endpoints; the friendship graph $F_{K-1}$ is its unique edge-minimal separator, so no fixed motif order serves every $K$. The separating signal is suppressed by a factor of at most $m_1^{2(K-1)}$, where $m_1$ is the mean squared slope, about $10^{-4}$ at $K=4$: detecting it from motif counts would take about $10^{15}$ nodes in a single graph, at any edge density. The information is nonetheless in the graph; the fiber belongs to the statistic, not the model. Common spectral pipelines, which count no motifs, still slide along the fiber by more than a third of its length at ordinary densities, with confidence regions that almost never contain the truth, while model-based fits of the same embedding do not. We give practitioners four checks: whether a fit is at risk, what to run instead, why a confidence interval will not warn, and how wide the remaining ambiguity is.

cross GRAFT: Graph-Regularized Prototype Fields for Realistic Test-Time Adaptation of VLMs

Authors: Maxx Richard Rahman, Wolfgang Maass

Abstract: Unlabeled test data can improve the zero-shot classifier of a vision-language model, yet transductive and test-time adaptation methods often fall below the zero-shot baseline when only a few vocabulary classes are present in a batch or when streams are correlated. We trace this failure to a shared design: every class owns independent parameters estimated from its own samples, so absent or rare classes are left at biased text anchors or fitted to foreign samples. We propose Graph-Regularized Anchor-displacement Field Transduction (GRAFT), which models the adapted prototypes as text anchors plus a displacement field that is partially pooled over a semantic class graph through a Gaussian Markov random field prior, together with an explicit class-presence model. Inference is closed-form and gradient-free on cached features. Anchor-only shrinkage and a single global shift are the two extreme settings of the prior; absent classes are provably harmonic extensions of the present ones, and the pooling strength can be chosen by empirical Bayes. Under the realistic batch and online protocols on eleven benchmarks and four ImageNet shifts, GRAFT improves over the zero-shot model in every setting and over anchor-based transduction where few classes are present.

cross Optical Neural Networks Should Be Evaluated by Function, Not Only Physics

Authors: Shi-Yuan Ma, Xingjian Bai, Ryan Hamerly, Mahmoud Jalali Mehrabad

Abstract: Optical neural networks (ONNs) are attracting significant attention as alternative platforms for machine learning (ML). In an ONN, an optical module often takes over part of the computation inside a digital ML pipeline. How much of the task does the optical module itself perform, and how much does it contribute to any claimed system-level advantages, such as throughput or energy? This paper offers a simple, function-oriented evaluation methodology. Whatever its internal physics, the optical module appears to the surrounding digital pipeline as a mapping between real-valued tensors. We therefore evaluate it in three steps: (i) define its boundary in the pipeline, as real-valued tensors in and out; (ii) replace it with standard digital modules to identify those that match the optical system's performance on the same task and metric; and (iii) state system-level claims, such as speed or energy, per inference against the performance-matched digital module. We summarize steps (i) and (ii) in an Expressivity Card and demonstrate its use on published ONN systems. This helps isolate the optical module's contribution and provides a concrete digital reference for assessing its claimed system-level advantages, without the need to understand the physics. While we focus on optical systems as the most prominent example, the same approach applies to any physical analog computing platform used in ML, wherever a physical module takes over part of a digital pipeline.

cross Isthmus: Bridging Supervision Islands Restores Uncertainty and Stability in One-Step Flows

Authors: Luming Yang, Siqing Li, Haoxian Liu, Zhenyu Bu, Jiazheng Zhu, Alper Yilmaz

Abstract: One-step flow maps are increasingly used as posterior samplers, where the point of sampling is the uncertainty that the samples carry. We find that the one-step sampler loses this uncertainty and becomes less stable. Compared with multi-step samples of the same network, its samples lose variance and decorrelate. Supervising the one-step jump directly makes this worse; the samples collapse further toward the conditional mean and break down when training is extended. We trace both failures to the locations where training places its pairs of start and end times. Only pairs on the diagonal of the time triangle receive targets from data, and the pair used for one-step sampling is either left unsupervised or supervised on an island, a set of pairs that the self-consistency identity does not connect to the diagonal, where the objective neither determines the one-step map nor anchors its training. We bridge the island with an isthmus, which places 5% of the training pairs along the whole edge of the triangle that joins the one-step pair to the diagonal. The error of the one-step map is then bounded by the self-consistency residual along this edge. With the network, the loss and the inference code unchanged and at no added cost, the isthmus improves one-step sampling across several flow-map objectives, in MRI reconstruction, face inpainting, and unconditional generation on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet 32x32. On brain MRI, it restores one-step calibration to 0.96 and the correlation with multi-step samples to 0.93; both vary far less across seeds, and the agreement holds when training is extended to three times its length.

cross How Uncertain Is My Action? Self-Correcting Visual Planning with Frozen World Models

Authors: Siqing Li, Luming Yang, Yijia Jiao, Junze Deng, Alper Yilmaz

Abstract: A frozen latent world model combined with sampling-based model predictive control is a visual planner. At deployment, the executed action can depart from the commanded one, and the frozen model has no way to notice. We show that the model can be run in reverse: for any hypothesized actuation shift, it predicts what it should have observed under that shift, and comparing this prediction with what was actually observed quantifies the shift and lets the planner correct itself. Unlike the residual energy, which mixes the shift with the model's own error, the score keeps only the part of the residual that a shift can produce. This shift score detects the shift with a locally optimal test, estimates it with Cramer-Rao error bars, names the dominant generator, and compensates the planner once an alarm fires. On PushT, TwoRoom and OGBench-Cube with a frozen latent world model, measured detection follows the prediction to within 0.05 AUROC. Self-correction raises success from 0.650 to 0.862 on PushT and from 0.378 to 0.432 on the harder Cube task, levels with an oracle that is told the true shift at the same block, better than five common test-time adaptation methods. On a real robotic arm, the score identifies a base-joint offset that no joint encoder registers and reduces the reach error from 36 mm to 3-5 mm, and under shifts drawn at random and sealed before the session, estimates recover 92-94% of the error reduction achieved with the true shift. The broader implication is that a frozen world model can act as a filter by predicting the future, testing action-interface uncertainty and validity, and finally correcting failures.

cross Two is better than one: optimizing binary rating scales for online platforms

Authors: Liren Shan, Alec Sun, Jingyan Wang

Abstract: Online platforms often struggle with inflated, uninformative user ratings. This issue can be mitigated by better design of the data collection mechanism, for example through changing the phrasing of user questions. Previous work optimizes a single rating scale to best distinguish the inherent qualities of items under binary feedback. In this paper, we demonstrate the power of utilizing different rating scales for different users. First, we show that a designer can always split a single rating scale into two to improve item rankings. Next, we formally characterize the optimal design of multiple scales as a series of 0-1 step functions whose threshold values are evenly spaced. Finally, we consider an adaptive model where subsequent rating scales can depend on the responses from previous users and items are ranked by their full sequence of responses, and show that adaptivity leads to exponentially greater distinguishing power.
