cross Option-GRPO: Hierarchical RL Against AI Slop in Code Generation for Unseen SDKs

Authors: Prashank Kadam

Abstract: Large language models increasingly produce AI slop in generated code: verbose, redundant, non-idiomatic implementations that pass functional checks but bloat codebases. The problem compounds on SDKs released after the model's training cutoff, where the model has neither idiomatic priors nor compositional understanding. We present Option-GRPO, a hierarchical reinforcement learning framework that adapts a code LLM to an unseen SDK from its source code alone, without human-written usage examples or test cases, and that explicitly optimizes for both functional correctness and code elegance. Our key insight is that SDK source code contains compositional structure that defines a hierarchy of reusable code patterns (code options) which simultaneously serve as the building blocks of a curriculum and the action space for efficient policy optimization. We show that code options induce a valid semi-Markov decision process, give conditional bounds under which shorter option description length implies more concise and, in expectation, more API-efficient programs, and analyze under stated assumptions how the option hierarchy improves credit assignment by a factor of $\sqrt{T/K}$. Using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with execution-based rewards at each curriculum level, Option-GRPO discovers SDK idioms, composes them into patterns, and assembles patterns into complete programs. Experiments on 12 unseen SDKs with a 32B-parameter base model demonstrate 61.4% pass@1, outperforming flat GRPO by 8.7 points, ExploraCoder by 11.2 points, and ReCode by 6.3 points. Option-GRPO produces solutions that use 34.7% fewer API calls and are 27% shorter than flat-GRPO outputs while maintaining higher correctness, directly attacking the slop problem.

cross When Do LLMs Model the Right Target? Problem Formulation in High-Stakes Risk Prediction

Authors: Jennifer Zhang, Amanda Coston

Abstract: Generative AI is increasingly used to assist with data science tasks, yet fundamental questions remain about its ability to correctly formulate analytical problems. We investigate how well large language models (LLMs) can formulate prediction problems when developing models for high-stakes decision making. We focus on one particular challenge to validity: decision dependency, which arises when historical decisions affect observed outcomes. We evaluate this aspect of problem formulation in LLM-generated code and explanations across child-welfare, healthcare, and employment settings, using 11 models from OpenAI and Anthropic and seven prompt conditions. We assess whether the LLM-generated code implements a procedure appropriate for estimating the intended outcome under the study's assumptions and whether the explanation acknowledges decision dependency. We find that LLMs do not reliably address decision dependency without guidance in the prompt. When prompts specify the target, the assumption needed to estimate it, and the estimation procedure, target-aligned implementation reaches 99-100%. Among frontier models, a simple prompt description of how decisions affect outcomes appears sufficient to elicit target-aligned implementation. Some responses also acknowledge decision dependency without addressing it in their code. Our findings suggest that analysts should not rely on LLMs to correctly formulate prediction problems without explicit methodological guidance and careful review. We propose validity-centered guidelines for LLM-assisted predictive modeling, covering prompt formulation, validation of the target and estimation procedure, and identification of key assumptions. Establishing validity is a necessary early step in evaluating whether to deploy a predictive model in a high-stakes setting, but deployment requires weighing broader ethical, institutional, and practical considerations.

cross Same Model, Different People: Numerical Execution Contracts and the Reproducibility of Fairness Audits

Authors: Erzheng Zhang, Agnes Chu

Abstract: Fairness audits treat a trained model as a fixed decision function, but the arithmetic that evaluates it is part of that function. This study holds the model, records, threshold and audit code fixed and varies the numerical execution contract in two tiers. Tier 2 runs reduced-precision deployment configurations (BF16 compute, FP8 and 8-bit weights), which can also round weights and change engines and kernels, so its effects belong to the configuration. At the reference's selection count FP8 failed demographic parity, and no reading gave its equalized-odds change a paired interval excluding zero. The declared audit depends on the configuration; FP8 is not shown to be fairer. At the frozen thresholds, every pass-fail verdict a configuration changed had a sampling interval containing its criterion. It proposes an audit stability protocol in which the auditor declares contracts, groups, weights and tests before scoring and records each verdict's range over them apart from its sampling uncertainty.

cross CratesBump: A Version-Bump Dataset for the Rust Ecosystem

Authors: Muhammad Hassnain

Abstract: We release CratesBump, the first public dataset for predicting version bumps in the Rust ecosystem, together with semver-extract, the tool that builds it. cross A Free Lunch: Gradient Averaging Achieves Optimal Statistical Efficiency in Newton Sketch

Authors: Haoxuan Wang, Jianliang Ye, Sen Na

Abstract: We study statistical inference for online sketched Newton methods, in which each Newton system is approximately solved by an inner randomized sketching solver. Existing work reveals a computational--statistical trade-off: the sketching solver (and its accelerated variants) reduces the per-step cost of Newton methods from $O(d^3)$ to $O(d^2)$, but the randomness introduced by sketching also inflates the limiting covariance of both the last and the averaged Newton iterates. In this work, we address this trade-off via gradient averaging. We show that simply replacing the stochastic gradient with a moving average at each step can mitigate, and in some cases eliminate, the trade-off in Newton sketch. Importantly, gradient averaging requires no additional oracle evaluations and leaves the contraction bound of the inner sketching solver unchanged. In particular, we prove that the last sketched Newton iterate with gradient averaging attains a limiting covariance no larger than that without gradient averaging, while the averaged iterate even attains the minimax optimal limiting covariance, matching that of the exact Newton method. To our knowledge, this is the first analysis showing that randomness introduced to improve computational efficiency need not compromise statistical efficiency as long as proper gradient estimates are used. The key analytical challenge is that gradient averaging destroys the crucial martingale difference structure underlying central limit theorem arguments. We overcome this challenge by analyzing the coupled two-timescale recursions governing the iterate and the gradient estimator. Numerical experiments on linear and logistic regression support our theoretical covariance comparisons.

cross Finetuning VLAs for Dexterous Manipulation: The Right Checkpoint Is All You Need

Authors: Artur Kuramshin, Charlotte Cloutier, Michael Przystupa, Kirsty Ellis, Mohammad S. Akhter, Glen Berseth

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action models have demonstrated strong manipulation capabilities. In this work, we study how to best adapt VLAs to dexterous hands by pairing supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with diffusion-steering reinforcement learning (DSRL). Using $\pi_{0.5}$ and a LEAP hand, we evaluate five fine-tuning recipes across two simulation tasks using only a handful of demonstrations. cross Evaluating Governed LLM-Driven ML Experimentation: Lifecycle, Provenance, and Failure Metrics

Authors: Dowling Wong

Abstract: Autonomous agents that run ML experiments are usually judged by the best score they reach. That score says nothing about how it was reached: whether the agent stayed within the files it was allowed to edit, whether failed trials were kept or silently dropped, and whether each keep or discard decision can be traced to its evidence. cross Measuring the Conservatism of Latent Safety Filters for World Models

Authors: Johannes Mootz, Mani Amani, Reza Akhavian

Abstract: A latent safety filter decides from the latent of a world model whether a planned action is safe. Conformal calibration sets the threshold of the filter so that it accepts an unsafe action only with a small probability. However, the filter can still be conservative and refuse many safe actions, depending on the latent. To measure the conservatism that a latent forces on the filter, we group similar latents and actions into cells. A filter that gives one decision per cell can refuse the unsafe transitions of a cell only by refusing the safe ones in it as well. We call the share of safe transitions that lie in such cells the overlap. We prove that the overlap is the minimum share of safe transitions that such a filter has to refuse in order to accept no unsafe transition in the data. Both the overlap and the conformal calibration can be applied to any latent. We use this to compare three existing designs, in which the safety labels train a classifier on top of the world model, the world model itself, or a separate encoder beside it. In a visual navigation task, the separate encoder has the lowest overlap, and at the same conformal level its filter refuses the fewest safe actions and lets the planner reach the most goals of the three.

Abstract: The rise of the agentic web has been accompanied by growing demand for on-device Vision-Language Models (VLMs) that perform diverse cross-modal web tasks with enhanced privacy and reduced latency. cross Improved Leverage Score Sampling for Constrained Active Linear Regression

Authors: Aarshvi Gajjar, Syamantak Kumar, Aditya Makkar, Christopher Musco, Joey Zhou

Abstract: In this paper we study active learning for constrained linear regression. Given a design matrix $A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}$, a constraint set $C\subseteq\mathbb{R}^d$, and query access to $b\in\mathbb{R}^n$, we seek an approximate solution to $\min_{x\in C}\|Ax-b\|_2^2$ using as few queries as possible. This problem arises in many scientific and engineering applications, and randomized leverage score sampling provides a powerful method to solve it. However, existing leverage score methods ignore the constraint or encode it through ridge regularization, suffering unfavorable query complexity for anisotropic constraint sets. We propose ellipsoid-regularized leverage scores, where we approximate the constraint set by an outer ellipsoid and use its shape matrix to bias sampling toward directions that matter under the constraint geometry. We prove that the query complexity is governed by an anisotropic effective dimension $d_{\text{eff}}(C)\le d$. With appropriate parameters, $O(d_{\text{eff}}(C)\log d/\varepsilon^2)$ queries suffice to return a point in $C$ with objective value at most $(1+\varepsilon)$ times the optimum. This bound can substantially improve on the standard leverage-score bound, since anisotropic constraints can make $d_{\text{eff}}(C)$ much smaller than $d$. For convex constraints, we improve the dependence on $\varepsilon$ from $1/\varepsilon^2$ to $1/\varepsilon$. We also prove two lower bounds: first, the $d/\varepsilon^2$ dependence is unavoidable without convexity assumptions on $C$, and second, in some regimes, the effective dimension dependence $d_{\mathrm{eff}}(C)/\varepsilon$ is necessary for ellipsoidal constraints. Experiments on synthetic regression problems and a parametric heat conduction problem show lower regression error than standard baselines for similar query budget.

cross Sightline: Long-Window Speculative Decoding for Fast SVG Generation

Authors: Yanchao Li, Ben Gao, Jiaqing Xie, Yusu Zhang, Beilun Wang, Yanbo Wang, Xiaohua Xu, Nanqing Dong, Tianfan Fu, Zhongyu Huang

Abstract: Scalable Vector Graphics (SVG) generation models write a graphic as code with one forward pass per token, so drawing is slow. Speculative decoding speeds them up losslessly, and block drafters guess many tokens per pass. On SVG, many of their rounds accept the whole draft and end only at the verification window. We find that their misses are rare and clumped, and that they stay accurate far deeper into a block than on natural language. A run-length identity and three propositions show that a longer window pays once the candidates are wide, up to the drafter's horizon. We propose Sightline, which pairs a window as long as the drafter's horizon with a draft tree wide enough to fill it. Experiments cover text-to-SVG and image-to-SVG with three models on six benchmarks from MMSVG, MMSVG-2M and SArena. In every setting, Sightline is the fastest of the compared lossless decoders. On SArena, it stays the fastest when every decoder shares one loop control. It also leads in almost every setting of six models from four other families, which write SVG code, compressed SVG tokens or Lottie JSON. Analyses trace the lead to rounds that shorter windows cut off, both in loop-free content and in loops. Speculative decoding also saves time for choosing samples by rendering, which beats choosing by likelihood. These results suggest that speculative decoding on vector graphics should verify as far as the drafter sees. Code and data are available at https://github.com/LiZaiyuan0619/Sightline.

Authors: Jinbae Seo, Jungin Park, Hyunsung Jang, Namkoo Ha, Jiyoung Lee, Kwanghoon Sohn

Abstract: Multimodal agents largely rely on generic tools such as cropping, search, and code execution, leaving specialist vision models outside their tool space. This work proposes COTEX, continual tool-space extension learning, that adds new tools at efficient adapter-level cost. cross NarrowSpec: Draft Hidden Width Need Not Match the Target in Speculative Decoding

Authors: Alagappan Valliappan, Ankit More, Dheevatsa Mudigere, Jatin Mitra

Abstract: Speculative decoding is widely used to speed up generation in large language models (LLMs), with a small draft model proposing tokens that one forward pass of the target model verifies. Draft models that read the target's hidden states, such as DFlash and EAGLE-3, typically inherit the target's hidden width. We show that the draft's whole residual stream can run at half or a quarter of that width while largely preserving acceptance. For DFlash, which borrows the target's vocabulary projection, the trained output adapter folds into it, giving one narrower projection with logits unchanged up to floating-point rounding. We test two drafter families, DFlash and EAGLE-3, on two targets, Qwen3-8B (NVFP4) and GPT-OSS-20B (MXFP4), in SGLang on a B200 and a DGX Spark (GB10). At batch size 1 and temperature 0, narrowing the draft to half or a quarter width speeds up end-to-end decoding by 1.06-1.12x on B200 and 1.13-1.44x on GB10. The speedup holds at larger batch sizes. From batch 32 to the largest batch each GPU fits, narrowing reaches up to 1.21x on both GPUs, and no narrow draft is slower than full width.

cross RePlan-VLA: Progress-Aware Replanning for Long-Horizon Vision-Language-Action Models

Authors: Apoorva Vashisth, Manav Kulshrestha, Damon Conover, Zachary Kingston, Aniket Bera

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have emerged as a promising paradigm for general-purpose robotic manipulation, yet their performance often degrades on long-horizon tasks that require multiple interdependent manipulations. Instead of burdening the VLA policies with long-horizon reasoning, memory, action generation, progress tracking, and error recovery, we argue for leveraging their short-horizon manipulation capabilities within an execution-aware framework. cross A Fast, Programmable GPU-to-CPU Invocation Layer for GPU-Initiated Communication

Authors: Jiakun Yan, Shiksha Patel, David Sidler, Gengyu Rao, Brandon Potter, Bradford Beckmann

Abstract: Modern GPU workloads increasingly rely on device-initiated communication to let GPU kernels communicate directly without synchronizing with the host at kernel boundaries. Existing libraries commonly favor placing transport control on GPU threads, an approach known as GPUDirect Async (GDA), and treat reverse offload to host proxy threads as a slower fallback. We argue that reverse offload can outperform GDA and should be a first-class communication path. We further propose exposing reverse offload as a programming interface that lets users define workload-specific communication operations. We present KIWI, a programmable reverse-offload framework that achieves sub-microsecond one-way device-to-host latency and hundreds of millions of operations per second. More importantly, KIWI-EP redesigns low-latency mixture-of-experts dispatch and combine kernels around workload-defined operations, lowering expert-parallel communication latency by up to 2.3x over the best existing backend on AMD GPUs and time per output token in vLLM by up to 29%. cross Neural Memory Orchestration

Authors: Michael Zizov, Boris Morose

Abstract: Modern HPC workloads increasingly stall on the memory hierarchy rather than on compute, and page faults from mmap'd files, swap, and tiered memory are a growing fraction of wall time. Heuristic prefetchers in the kernel handle stride-like and temporally clustered patterns well but degrade sharply on the irregular, data-dependent access streams typical of these workloads. Learned prefetchers have been shown to capture such patterns in simulation but have not been taken to a deployed pipeline running against live workloads on real hardware. We present Overseer, an end-to-end learned page-prefetching system. An eBPF collector observes page faults on the target host, a transformer predictor (OverNet) runs on network-attached inference hardware and emits future page addresses, and a kernel-resident enforcer materializes those pages into the target's address space before they are demanded. OverNet encodes fault sequences through per-instance ordinal ranks over deltas, which lets a single trained model serve any target process without retraining. On two page-fault-heavy workloads, Overseer reduces runtime by roughly 35% on Hilbert-curve traversal and by roughly an order of magnitude on dense matrix multiplication, with corresponding reductions in major-fault counts.

cross LAGR: Language-Aware Grasp Re-Ranking for Task Oriented Grasping

Abstract: Grasping is a fundamental capability for robotic manipulation, with the appropriate grasp depending on the downstream task. For example, a hammer should be held by its handle for hammering but by its head when it is handed over. Selecting such a grasp requires consideration of two criteria: physical feasibility on the object and suitability for the task. Developing such an approach requires dense grasp labels with both feasibility and task scores on real observations, but existing task-oriented grasping datasets are small, synthetic, or costly to annotate. To address this gap, we construct a dataset of 2.68 million scored 5-DoF grasp annotations over 100,911 image-task pairs from $17$ object categories. Additionally, we introduce LAGR, a task-oriented grasp detection method that explicitly addresses both these challenges. Given vision-language inputs, LAGR first generates task-independent grasp proposals along with a physical feasibility score, and then scores the generated grasps with a language-conditioned re-ranker that reasons over all proposals jointly. Experiments show that LAGR outperforms the next best method by 9.3 percentage points. We further validate LAGR on real objects by executing task-oriented grasps with a Franka Research 3 robot.

cross In Situ Training of Optical Neural Networks by Coding Agents

Authors: Ilker Oguz, Colin Christian, Christophe Moser, Demetri Psaltis, Gordon Wetzstein

Abstract: Optical neural networks promise fast, energy-efficient computing, but every device departs from its design and must be trained with the hardware. In situ training relies on an expert-built calibrated digital model of each device or on gradient-free trial and error. Coding agents, large language models that write and run programs, have shown an emergent ability to understand and edit the weights of digital neural networks. Here we introduce optics as code, a method in which a coding agent trains an optical neural network from measurements alone, through a harness that returns camera images of its probes and requires a hypothesis per request. We discovered that the agent forms a physical understanding of the optics and reparametrizes the network with interpretable optical elements tuned on the hardware. It outperforms gradient-free methods in classification and image encoding, approaching backpropagation through a calibrated model at a fraction of the cost. Needing neither per-device calibration nor gradients through the hardware, and improving with more capable models, optics as code offers a practical route to large, scalable optical neural networks and a general tool for calibrating and optimizing optical experiments.

cross Infinity-Wasserstein Distributionally Robust Principal Component Analysis: Exact Reformulation, Finite-Sample Guarantee, and Efficient Algorithm

Authors: Zhouxing Luo, Lei Wang, Xin Liu

Abstract: Principal component analysis (PCA) is a fundamental and ubiquitous technique for dimension reduction and representation learning, yet its empirical formulation remains vulnerable to distributional uncertainty. While distributionally robust optimization (DRO) offers a natural remedy, existing order-2 Wasserstein models of PCA either preserve the nominal PCA subspace or lack an exact reformulation. This paper demonstrates how the infinity-Wasserstein distance can reconcile these two desirable properties. For the resulting DRO model of PCA, we derive an exact finite-dimensional reformulation and explicitly identify a worst-case distribution attaining the inner maximum. Crucially, the reformulated objective retains samplewise residual information beyond the empirical covariance matrix, revealing a mechanism through which distributional robustness can reshape the learned principal subspace. Under the mild assumption that the true distribution has a finite fourth moment, we further establish a uniform finite-sample performance guarantee for the proposed model. An iteratively reweighted majorization-minimization algorithm is also developed to solve the resulting nonconvex and nonsmooth optimization problem, with a rigorous analysis of its convergence properties. Numerical experiments substantiate the computational efficiency of the proposed algorithm and the practical effectiveness of the infinity-Wasserstein model in downstream applications.

cross Accelerating Distributed Digital Ising Machines

Abstract: Ising machines, dedicated accelerators built around the dynamics of Ising-like spin models, have emerged as a leading platform for combinatorial optimization and probabilistic sampling. The problems that motivate them require millions of spins, far beyond the on-chip capacity of any single device, so a practical Ising machine must distribute its computation across many devices. cross CoSlice: Coordinated Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for 5G Network Slicing Allocation

Authors: Pengxin Wang, Nakjung Choi, Jingdi Chen

Abstract: Radio Access Network (RAN) slicing is a shared resource allocation problem: each slice seeks to satisfy its Service Level Agreement (SLA) with minimal radio-resource consumption, while all slices compete for a limited resource budget. This creates both an intra-slice trade-off between SLA assurance and resource efficiency and an inter-slice conflict over shared resources, calling for specialized yet coordinated control. In this work, we present CoSlice, a coordinated multi-agent reinforcement learning framework for inter-slice physical resource block allocation in Open Radio Access Network (O-RAN). CoSlice uses preference-conditioned slice agents for fast resource allocation and an LLM-based coordinator to adapt their objectives using global network observations at a slower timescale. We build a network slicing evaluation environment using ORANSlice and its radio-frequency simulator (RFsim), supporting realistic protocol-stack execution and application traffic across multiple slices. Experimental results show that CoSlice reduces both average and tail SLA violations compared with other centralized and multi-agent baselines under diverse slice workloads.

cross How Complete Must an Environment Be to Train a Capable GUI Agent?

Authors: Jingxing Wang, Teng Wang, Xingyu Lou, Zihan Guo, Jun Wang, Weiwen Liu, Weinan Zhang, Jianghao Lin

Abstract: Graphical user interface (GUI) agents are widely trained in executable GUI environments. The gain reverses when each episode is weighted equally and vanishes when only episodes on the pruned tasks are balanced. Without pruning, length weighting there reinforces long detour successes that erode skills on other tasks. Following these findings, we build MobileGym-Lite, which keeps reward and data coverage complete and prunes unneeded features. It outperforms Full in the full environment (55.48% versus 51.61% success) with lower construction cost.

Abstract: Stability under input perturbations is necessary for deep neural network estimators in many applications involving noisy covariates or estimated features. cross A Mechanistic Analysis of In-Context Learning and Out-of-Context Hallucination

Authors: Tianxiang Chen, Jingyuan Zhou, Guoyao Yang, Kaidi Yang

Abstract: Large language models can infer and follow specific rules through in-context learning (ICL), yet may fail to remain faithful to provided evidence, giving rise to out-of-context (OOC) hallucination. Existing work has largely investigated the underlying mechanisms of ICL and OOC hallucination along separate lines. However, this separation obscures the mechanistic basis for these seemingly opposing outcomes. In this work, we study this contrast through the unified view of functional roles in dynamic context. The key observation is that role information can be internally represented without reliably constraining output. We derive a first-order decomposition to characterize the role effect, showing that its substantial influence depends on two necessary conditions, namely sufficient contribution magnitude and coherent signed aggregation. Causal analysis further shows that role span contributions affect key token choices involving response structure, content selection, and factual values. We further show that role-consistent choices depend on sufficient role span support at these key decisions, while failing choices can be reversed when this support overcomes the deficit. These findings support a common mechanistic view of ICL and OOC hallucination: each functional role defines the pattern of output decisions over which role span contributions must remain effective, and differences in this organization account for the seemingly opposing outcomes.

cross NOTREKS: Marginal Independence in Score-based Structure Learning

Authors: Fabian Bleile, Sarah Lumpp, Mathias Drton

Abstract: Score-based structure learning estimates causal graphs by optimizing a data-dependent objective over structural equation models. We study how prior knowledge that selected pairs of variables are marginally independent can be incorporated into such causal discovery methods. Under faithfulness, marginally independent pairs cannot be connected by a trek, so the knowledge becomes a graphical no-trek constraint, which we call NOTREKS information. We introduce a power-series trek (PSTrek) penalty for gradient-based optimization and a trek constraint for order-based search, and we analyze why such information helps in finite samples and how it restricts the search space. Authors: Yunhe Gao, Ashwin Kumar, Jiaming Liu, Chong Wang, Maya Varma, Fuying Wang, Stefania L. Moroianu, Louisa Fay, Sergios Gatidis, Akshay S. Chaudhari, Curtis Langlotz

Abstract: CLIP-style contrastive image-text pretraining is widely used in radiology data, where each image is paired with one report and every other report in the batch is treated as a negative. cross Recurrent Domain Incremental Adaptation of Time-Series Foundation Models

Authors: Sachith Abeywickrama, Peiliang Gong, Emadeldeen Eldele, Min Wu, Chau Yuen

Abstract: Pretrained Time-Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) provide strong representations for downstream classification tasks. Yet adapting a TSFM under changing operating conditions remains challenging. A model must improve on each newly observed condition without sacrificing conditions it has already learned, often from only a small labeled calibration set and without knowing the operating condition at inference time. We find that this difficulty has an important geometric structure. Under an operating-condition shift, the embedding space of a TSFM does not move uniformly: some local regions change substantially, while others remain nearly unchanged. Consequently, a global adaptation can unnecessarily move embeddings that were already correctly classified. To address this, we propose the novel PACT (Piecewise Adaptation via Chart Transport). With only 5% labeled calibration data per arriving condition and no condition identity at test time, PACT maintains an average macro-F1 above 0.95 across a four-condition bearing-fault stream. cross Meaning Reaches Object Choice but Not Completion: Where Is a VLA Vulnerable to Landmark Synonyms?

Abstract: VLA policies are fragile to rewording. Success rates alone cannot say whether a policy fails to choose the object or to complete the task, nor where in the policy to intervene. After the connector, pasting at the landmark, the remaining instruction, or the image tokens alone rarely moves the choice, but any two do. cross Quantum Annealing with a Quantum Neural Network: Width of Annealing Matters

Authors: Mojtaba Asadollahi, Danial Khajehzadeh, Abolfazl Ramezanpour

Abstract: Quantum annealing is a promising quantum algorithm for dealing with computationally hard optimization problems. Currently available hardware can implement the algorithm for a significant class of Hamiltonian problems. However, its performance is especially limited in discontinuous quantum phase transitions, where the energy gap shrinks exponentially with system size. In this paper, we introduce an extension of quantum annealing to a quantum neural network and study its performance in a number of prototypical optimization problems. In particular, we observe that already a random quantum annealing network of width W = 2 can exponentially reduce the number of evolution layers or depth of the annealing process in the quantum search problem. cross Nearly Tight Almost Sure Convergence Rates for the Last iterate of SGD

Abstract: We study the almost sure convergence of the last-iterate optimization error of Stochastic (sub)Gradient Descent (SGD) for convex objectives. cross Rare-Tail Statistics For Learning Biased Gaussian Halfspaces with Label Noise

Authors: Arya Mazumdar, Prateeti Mukherjee, Harsh Vardhan

Abstract: We revisit the classical problem of learning general Gaussian halfspaces in the presence of label noise, with particular focus on the large-bias regime, where information-computation gaps are conjectured in several standard label-noise models. Concretely, we present and analyze the rare-tail mean estimator, an extremely simple one-shot statistic that averages only the observed minority class. In the large-bias regime, classical Averaging (Servedio, 1999) and related global first-moment statistics are provably suboptimal, while the rare-tail mean estimator recovers the target halfspace direction at the information-theoretic sample rate (modulo log factors) under a broad class of label noise models, including probit noise and margin-dependent noise with very slow decay away from the boundary. Notably, our guarantees allow labels arbitrarily close to the decision boundary to be nearly random, while still giving strong directional recovery from a small number of samples. The estimator also cleanly interpolates between the noiseless and noisy regimes: without noise, it attains the same sample rate as classical separability-based methods for realizable halfspaces, and under low noise, or noise that decays away from the boundary, it keeps this rate even after linear separability has been destroyed. As an application, we consider a two-stage curriculum in which source samples, whose noise may be arbitrarily close to $1/2$ near the boundary and decay very slowly away from it, precede target samples, whose noise flips every label at the same rate, i.e., random classification noise (RCN). Since both stages share the same Bayes optimal halfspace, our estimator supplies the directional warm start needed by existing refinement routines, and together they learn the RCN target at the information-theoretic sample rate in the large-bias regime, up to log factors.

Abstract: Neural decoding of visual experience from electroencephalography (EEG) has achieved strong performance in intra-subject settings, but current methods fail to generalize across individuals: on the standard 200-way benchmark THINGS-EEG-2, top-1 accuracy drops from above 80% within subjects to about 32% for an unseen subject. Authors: Florian Hettstedt, Andreas Erbsl\"oh, Gregor Schiele

Abstract: Integrating gesture sensing into furniture offers a way to interact with digital applications and physical devices through familiar surfaces. We present TableControl, an interactive demonstration of continuous surface-vibration-based gesture recognition with the complete processing pipeline running on a microcontroller. Using four concealed piezoelectric sensors, the system combines signal acquisition and preprocessing with neural event detection and gesture classification. The resulting labels for knock, swipe-up, and swipe-down gestures are transmitted to a host computer without streaming the underlying sensor signals. Visitors explore two example applications through hands-on interaction: an endless runner game and a physical table lamp with on/off and brightness control. A dashboard displays recognized gestures, class probabilities, and processing times.

cross Functional Principal Component Analysis: Minimax Optimality from Sparse to Dense Designs and Beyond

Authors: Shunxing Yan, Fang Yao

Abstract: Functional principal component analysis (FPCA) plays a central role as a dimension-reduction and representation tool in functional data analysis, but its minimax theory for diverging spectral indices under noisy discrete observation has remained open for over two decades. To fill this gap, we consider a sieve-based FPCA framework that pools discrete measurements across subjects and accommodates sampling frequencies ranging from sparse to dense under random designs. We begin by establishing new minimax rates for covariance estimation over general bivariate function classes beyond tensor-product low-complexity structures, thereby identifying the intrinsically two-dimensional optimal rate. Our central theoretical contribution is to establish minimax optimal rates for both individually labelled eigenfunctions with diverging indices and leading principal subspaces of increasing dimensions, under explicit growth and regularity conditions. To establish these results, we develop a general framework for sharp minimax analysis of FPCA under discrete observation, separating spectral approximation from stochastic perturbations and exploiting their distinct structures, thereby offering a new approach to a broad class of nonparametric spectral estimation problems. For both spectral targets, the optimal rates reveal two distinct statistical mechanisms, spectral identification and nonparametric reconstruction, whose interplay gives rise to either four or two sampling regimes. This yields, for the first time, a precise characterization of the impact of discrete sampling across the full range from sparse to dense designs. Finally, as a downstream application, we analyze FPCA-based functional linear regression and show that the FPCA-based estimator attains the minimax-optimal rates.

cross ViLFlow: Parallel Vision-Language Generation with Continuous Latent Flows

Authors: Kevin Zhai, Bian Sun, Zhenyi Wang, Song Wang

Abstract: Vision-language generation has predominantly relied on autoregressive and discrete diffusion models. Autoregressive models generate tokens sequentially, while discrete diffusion models typically predict tokens independently within each denoising step. We propose ViLFlow (Vision-Language Flow), a continuous-flow vision-language model that combines joint modeling of output embeddings with parallel token decoding. ViLFlow adapts pretrained continuous language generation to visual question answering and image captioning through learned image guidance and prediction-aware token decoding. A visually adapted teacher provides image-guided predictions as additional denoising supervision. The denoiser learns to reproduce these predictions directly, retaining one image-conditioned prediction per generation step. We train the token decoder on denoiser predictions and paired reference text to improve the conversion of continuous outputs into words. The decoder can be adapted independently, improving answering and captioning without further denoiser training. For captioning, it also supplies text feedback during late denoising, allowing decoded words to inform subsequent refinement. ViLFlow outperforms autoregressive and masked-diffusion baselines by up to 8.46 percentage points on VQAv2 and 4.50 points on GQA without GQA training. On COCO Karpathy test, it improves CIDEr by 16.25 over masked diffusion and 17.81 over sampled autoregression.

cross Stability-compatible kernels: beyond Gaussianity for impulse response estimation

Authors: Gianluigi Pillonetto, Mauro Bisiacco

Abstract: Kernel-based regularization for linear system identification uses positive semidefinite kernels both to define reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHSs) and as covariance models for stochastic impulse responses. Sharp kernel conditions are known for RKHS stability, while almost-sure BIBO stability under Gaussian priors requires an even more restrictive covariance condition. These restrictions, however, need not persist beyond Gaussianity, where the covariance no longer determines the full probability law. We introduce the notion of a stability-compatible kernel, namely a covariance kernel admitting at least one stochastic realization with almost-surely absolutely summable sample paths. We show that stability-compatibility holds if and only if the stable elements are dense in the associated RKHS, a condition strictly weaker than RKHS stability, and derive practical sufficient conditions, including a spectral criterion for stationary kernels. We then construct non-Gaussian priors that preserve a prescribed covariance while enforcing almost-sure stability. In particular, this yields a stable prior based on the stationary radial-basis-function (RBF) kernel, separating covariance structure from the mechanism enforcing stability. Numerical experiments show substantial estimation gains for several important classes of impulse responses, with particularly pronounced advantages when relevant dynamics occur at late lags or in separated temporal regions.

cross LoopCache: Accelerating Long-Context Self-Speculative Decoding for Looped Transformers

Authors: Zizhuo Fu, Haochen Huang, Shengxuan Qiu, Linye Wei, Ziyu Tang, Runsheng Wang, Meng Li

Abstract: Looped Transformers achieve large effective depth with compact models by repeatedly applying a weight-shared block across R recurrent steps, each maintaining a loop-specific key-value (KV) cache. To accelerate sequential loop execution, recent wave-style self-speculative methods like WaveFront Decoding (WFD) and LoopSpec pipeline draft and target tokens across loops in a single step. However, they face two critical limitations. First, they draft only at fixed loops and ignore intermediate prediction changes, wasting computation on stale trajectories. Second, batching tokens from different loops loads up to Rx the KV cache per step, severely amplifying memory traffic in long-context scenarios. To address these challenges, we propose LoopCache, an efficient self-speculative decoding framework for long-context Looped Transformers. Based on the correlation between prediction switches and output confidence, LoopCache introduces confidence-guided dynamic draft updating to refine drafts in flight across all intermediate loops. Furthermore, LoopCache employs a cyclic loop KV cache strategy that rotates through loop-specific KV caches, restricting per-step KV traffic to a single loop while batching tokens at the nearest matching cycle for lossless verification. By also selectively retaining independent KV caches for a few loop-sensitive shallow layers, LoopCache improves acceptance rates with negligible overhead. Experiments show that LoopCache achieves average 10.25x and 14.56x speedup over WFD and LoopSpec.

cross AutoPI: Taxonomy-Guided Adaptive Red Teaming for Prompt Injection in LLM Agents

Authors: Stavros Damianakis, Konstantinos Psounis

Abstract: Prompt injection is the dominant unsolved security risk for LLM agents, and the community now agrees that robustness must be measured against adaptive attackers rather than fixed benchmark injections. Many adaptive red teamers exist, and most are built to maximize attack success rate (ASR). Yet ASR counts how often a target breaks, not in how many different ways. A red teamer can achieve a high ASR by reusing a few techniques that work, leaving the others untested and absent from the attacks used to harden models. We present AutoPI, an adaptive red teamer designed to find many different ways to break a target, not just to break it often. AutoPI uses PIAtlas, a taxonomy of prompt-injection techniques, both to guide its search and to measure coverage. For each scenario, an attacker LLM writes an injection for every sub-technique in the taxonomy and then reworks the ones that fail using feedback from an LLM judge. The method is black-box and training-free, it plugs into existing benchmarks in place of their fixed attack templates, and it stays up to date as the taxonomy evolves. We evaluate AutoPI on AgentDojo and IPI Arena against open-weight and proprietary target models. Alongside ASR, we report technique coverage, the fraction of taxonomy classes, techniques, and sub-techniques that yield at least one successful injection. On AgentDojo, AutoPI finds a successful injection against Qwen3.6-35B in all six classes of the taxonomy and for more than 90% of its techniques. Against Claude Haiku 5.5, the most robust target we evaluate, it achieves between 20% and 50% higher ASR and between 25% and 400% higher class-level coverage than recent state-of-the-art adaptive red teamers, while requiring no training. We release our code and the generated attacks to support reproducible, coverage-aware evaluation.

cross Can Weight-Space Programming Alter Learned Ethical Priorities?

Authors: Kai Yi Ji

Abstract: We investigate whether a weight edit can alter how a model trades off honoring a commitment against benefiting more people. In a concept-supervised text model, we alter fitted choice weights so that the benefit required to override a formal pledge is larger than for an ordinary promise. cross MoE-Context: Show the Model Only What It Needs

Authors: Vedant Navale

Abstract: AI agents read their whole history at every step. People do not work like that: we keep what the moment needs and hold the rest as a rough note we can recall. MoE-Context applies this to language models. The context is split into blocks. Before each step a fast yes or no pass decides which blocks the step needs; the rest stay as one-line notes that can be brought back, and nothing is deleted. In the version we build, one model makes this decision and then answers, over a single key-value cache where hidden blocks are masked rather than removed. On a laptop with Qwen3-1.7B and a synthetic lookup task, showing only the needed block raises accuracy from 93 to 119 of 120, and a masked block is as hidden as a deleted one. The model's own picking is the weak part. A supervised linear probe written into spare output rows lifts its accuracy from 80 to 110 of 120, but simple keyword search picks perfectly on this task, and the probe does not transfer to a second task. We report these results openly, along with two cheap usage patterns that block switching enables: undoing a failed attempt and running subagents without a new context.

cross Every Query Routes Itself: Accelerating Video Diffusion Transformers with Per-Query Routed Sparse Attention

Authors: Zhen Huang, Shuwei Li, Ruizhe Yao, Xinrui Chen, Danyi Liu, Haoyuan Liang, Zhiyu Ye, Qiuyan Sun, Bingwei Chen, Yiyuan Li, Xu Zhou, Haohuan Fu

Abstract: Self-attention over long token sequences dominates the inference cost of video diffusion transformers. Clustering-based sparse attention clusters both queries and keys and lets all queries in a cluster share one selection of key clusters. We show that this sharing limits accuracy: with key clusters and density fixed, attention grows more accurate as the query clusters are refined, and is most accurate when each query selects on its own, which needs no query clustering at all. We therefore propose Per-Query Routed Sparse Attention (PQRS), a training-free method that clusters only keys, lets each query select its own key clusters, and compensates for the unselected ones with their centroids. Per-query selections leave no query tile with a shared key list, so PQRS traverses key clusters instead of queries, buckets queries with identical selections into dense tasks, and merges their partial results in one pass under a fixed softmax offset carried from the previous denoising step. On four text-to-video and image-to-video models from the Wan and HunyuanVideo families, PQRS is the fastest of all the sparse attention methods we evaluate and the closest to the dense output, reaching $28.9$ dB PSNR on Wan2.1-T2V and $35.4$ dB on HunyuanVideo at $1.66$--$2.10\times$ end-to-end speedup.

cross Sparse designs via quantization of the uniform measure on high-dimensional hypercubes

Authors: Luc Pronzato, Anatoly Zhigljavsky

Abstract: While the notions of sphere packing and sphere covering underlie the most widely used criteria for space-filling design in computer experiments, namely, the maximin and minimax approaches, they suffer from major drawbacks in high dimensions. First, when the dimension $d$ is very large, deploying a space-filling design requires an extremely large number of design points, making the very notion of a space-filling design questionable. Second, these two design characteristics are highly sensitive to boundaries, particularly when the design domain is a hypercube (the case considered in this paper), and especially to its vertices. This sensitivity is particularly acute when the design size is less than $2^d$. Instead of considering the maximin or minimax criterion, we consider here the quantization error of the uniform measure on the hypercube. This error has three key advantages: it accounts for the entire distribution of distances from a random point $U$ to the design, is less sensitive to boundary effects than the maximin and minimax criteria, and, thanks to Zador's celebrated theorem, enables a smooth and natural transition to uniform designs as the design size tends to infinity. Building on recent results on the construction of optimal random quantizers for spherically symmetric distributions (arXiv:2610.11772) we propose a method to generate random designs of a given target size $n$ with small $L_s$-mean quantization error. The support of the resulting distribution is then used to construct designs, possibly incrementally, with sizes up to $n$. We demonstrate that, for moderate $n$, designs supported on a fractional factorial subset of the vertices of a small hypercube perform exceptionally well---and significantly better than designs uniformly distributed over the entire hypercube. We also explain how to account for projections onto lower-dimensional subspaces.

cross Taming the Tails of Stochastic Gradient Descent in the Interpolation Regime

Authors: Leonardo Galli, Daniela Angela Parletta, Andrea Paudice

Abstract: In this paper, we study stochastic gradient descent in the smooth interpolation regime, where the population objective shares a common minimizer with the sample losses. In contrast to prior work, which has largely focused on in-expectation analysis, we obtain high-probability convergence guarantees under the same assumptions. In the convex case, we prove a fast rate of order $\log(\beta^{-1}) \cdot n^{-1}$ after $n$ iterations, with failure probability at most $\beta$. In the strongly convex case, we derive a family of moment-based bounds of order $\beta^{-p} \cdot \exp(-\mathcal{O}(p \cdot n))$ for $p \in (0,1]$, and complement them with a matching lower bound showing that the corresponding deterioration in the exponential rate is essentially unavoidable. In both settings, our theoretical results are unified across a broad class of step-size schedules. Building on this analysis, we extend our results to a low-noise regime and to nonconvex objectives satisfying the Polyak-Lojasiewicz condition.

Authors: Ivan Lee, Xingyu Xu, Yuejie Chi

Abstract: Diffusion models have transformed generative modeling through their ability to capture and generate samples from complex data distributions. This success has spurred substantial interest in using pretrained diffusion models as expressive priors for highly ill-posed inverse problems in imaging and sensing. Despite strong empirical performance, theoretical understanding of this approach remains limited: existing guarantees are often asymptotic or entail prohibitive computational costs in the worst case. In this paper, we address this gap by studying sparse recovery from a small number of linear measurements, a classical problem in compressed sensing. Abstract: As large language models (LLMs) and LLM-based agents are increasingly deployed, monitoring their behavior becomes important for detecting unsafe or unintended actions. cross When Does Continual Pre-Training Help? A Controlled Study of Dialectal Adaptation for Qu\'ebec French

Authors: Eeham Khan, Richard Khoury, Leila Kosseim

Abstract: Continual pre-training (CPT) is widely used to adapt pretrained language models to dialectal and non-standard language varieties, yet it remains unclear when this additional training is necessary, what drives its benefits, and whether aggregate evaluation scores adequately capture its effects. In this paper, we present a controlled study of CPT for Qu\'ebec French language understanding across nine LLMs spanning different families and scales. Using a budget-matched protocol of 30M tokens per experiment, our results show that the benefit of CPT depends strongly on the starting model's existing competence in the target variety. When CPT is beneficial, the improvements are driven more by corpus composition than by data volume, with literary text accounting for most of the gain. Paired Qu\'ebec and standard French evaluations further show that CPT primarily improves expression comprehension rather than grammatical competence, and that corpus register determines which regional expressions are acquired. These gains also involve capability-specific trade-offs, including declines in reading comprehension that aggregate scores can conceal. Overall, these findings show that CPT for dialectal adaptation should not be treated as a uniformly beneficial default. Instead, adaptation strategies should begin with a careful assessment of the base model's existing competence, accompanied by source-level data analysis and task-level evaluation.

cross Surrogate Modeling and Reduced-Order Methods in Gravitational-Wave Physics and Numerical Relativity: From Spectral Representations to Adaptive Compression

Authors: Manuel Tiglio

Abstract: The rapid expansion of gravitational-wave astronomy and high-precision numerical relativity has created an acute computational bottleneck: direct numerical integration of the Einstein field equations and high-dimensional Bayesian parameter estimation require millions of waveform evaluations that cannot be sustained by traditional solvers alone. Reduced-order modeling (ROM) and surrogate techniques have emerged as foundational tools to bridge this gap, compressing high-fidelity solution manifolds into compact, rapidly evaluable representations without sacrificing physical accuracy. This perspective reviews the mathematical pillars of projection-based reduced-order modeling---including Proper Orthogonal Decomposition, greedy basis selection, and the Empirical Interpolation Method---and traces their operational impact in gravitational-wave data analysis through surrogate waveform families and Reduced-Order Quadratures. We then examine the fundamental approximation barriers posed by slow Kolmogorov n-width decay in non-stationary, transient, and strongly nonlinear regimes, showing how adaptive hp-greedy domain partitioning and multi-scale spectral compression overcome these limits. Finally, we discuss how combining certified reduced-order structures with modern scientific machine learning and sensitivity-aware quantization provides a rigorous blueprint for next-generation detectors such as LISA, Einstein Telescope, and Cosmic Explorer.

cross Sex Differences in the Detection of Parkinson's Disease from Speech

Authors: Hiba Akhaddar, Peter Plantinga, Mirco Ravanelli, Tristan Glatard

Abstract: Parkinson's disease (PD) is the second most common neurodegenerative disease in the world, and 70\% to 75\% of PD patients suffer from speech disorders at some stage. Since PD affects men more frequently than women, previous publications tend to rely on unbalanced datasets that potentially introduce sex biases into trained models. This study investigates sex differences in PD detection from speech using deep learning and traditional machine learning approaches. We evaluated speech-based PD detection using embeddings from three pre-trained speech models, combined with handcrafted acoustic features and different classifiers. Sex-specific experiments were conducted on balanced subsets of four sustained phonation datasets, controlling for sex, disease status, and, where possible, age. Sex-specific results show that when the models are trained on one sex, they fail to generalize to the other one. cross Cross-Vehicle Transferability of Lateral Velocity Estimation on Production Vehicles through Input Selection and Data Augmentation

Authors: Hendrik Sch\"afke, Simon F. G. Ehlers, Thomas Seel, Askar Vagapov, Christoph Schweers, Ahmed Trabelsi, Daniel O. M. Weber

Abstract: Lateral velocity is safety-critical for vehicle-dynamics control but cannot be measured with production sensors. Model-based estimators such as Kalman filters are established, whereas data-driven estimators are more accurate within the training regime but suspected to lose accuracy beyond it. Their transfer to a structurally different vehicle has rarely been tested. We record real-world test drives of three series-production vehicles, train a neural-network estimator on production CAN signals of a compact car, and evaluate it zero-shot on an SUV and a van against a GNSS-aided inertial reference. On the unseen vehicles, the network's error exceeds that on the training vehicle by an order of magnitude, a gap not quantified on production vehicles before. A systematic comparison of input sets from ten production CAN signals identifies a five-signal set that transfers most robustly. To narrow this gap, we introduce three physically motivated augmentations of training data. Two improve generalization, namely exploiting the vehicle's left-right symmetry and emulating accelerometer bias. Combined, they reduce the error on the validation maneuvers of the SUV and van by 13% and 15%. Contrary to common expectation, on the SUV the data-driven estimator trained with both augmentations matches a model-based estimator tuned on the same training data.

Abstract: We extend our previous search for the modular data of topological quantum field theories in 2+1 dimensions from 22 to 42 anyons, identifying 150,679 new modular data. cross Training-free motion statistics match self-supervised behavior encoders

cross Kaer: Security-Aware Replanning for Multi-Agent Systems

Authors: Tushin Mallick, Evan Rose, Matthew Laws, Alina Oprea, Cristina Nita-Rotaru

Abstract: One popular architecture for multi-agent systems (MAS) relies on an orchestrator that generates an initial plan, coordinates its execution, and dynamically revises the plan based on agent feedback when tasks cannot be fully specified in advance. This dynamic replanning introduces a critical vulnerability: malicious agents can embed instructions within their feedback to hijack the replanning process. Existing defenses focus exclusively on per-tool access control or static, pre-execution policy enforcement, leaving the replanning phase unsecured. We introduce Kaer, a multi-agent framework featuring security-aware replanning. Kaer shields the planner from untrusted agent feedback by conveying diagnostics to the replanning procedure strictly through abstract templates. Plan revisions are restricted to a set of deterministic repair operators, and all modified plans must satisfy preset policies. We create Cintra, a benchmark of simulated user tasks requiring replanning paired with adversarial objectives targeting the replanning phase. Using Cintra, we evaluate both task completion rates and system vulnerability to replanning exploits by malicious agents. Evaluating Kaer on Cintra against Magentic-One (an undefended MAS) and ControlValve (the state-of-the-art defense) demonstrates that Kaer achieves higher robustness than both systems while maintaining comparable utility to ControlValve. Although abstract failure templates slightly constrain utility on select tasks, Kaer enables MAS to perform secure, dynamic plan revisions without exposing the orchestrator to untrusted agent feedback.

cross Does the Action Matter? Probing Action-Conditioned Semantic Forecasting as a World Model for Autonomous Driving

Authors: Binay Kumar Singh, Niels Da Vitoria Lobo

Abstract: World models are increasingly used in autonomous driving to predict how a scene evolves under the ego vehicle's actions, supporting counterfactual reasoning and model-based planning. However, existing evaluations focus on prediction fidelity and planning performance, and rarely verify that the predictions depend on the conditioning action. In this paper, we study action dependence in action-conditioned semantic forecasting, which predicts future semantic segmentation from past segmentations and the ego action. cross Decomposing SGD Dynamics in Neural Networks: Teacher-Induced Spikes and Variance Inflation

Authors: Samet Demir, Zafer Dogan

Abstract: We study two-layer neural networks trained by stochastic gradient descent (SGD) on a multi-index teacher model, focusing on how first-layer parameters orthogonal to the teacher subspace affect generalization. We decompose the first-layer weights into a signal component aligned with the teacher and an orthogonal complement, and analyze their joint SGD dynamics together with the second layer in the proportional limit. We show that although SGD updates to the complement are initially isotropic, the training dynamics induce teacher-dependent spectral spikes in the complement Gram matrix. This emergent anisotropy provides a concrete mechanism by which representation variance is amplified, and generalization degrades. To isolate this effect, we introduce Decomposed Dynamics, an analytically tractable surrogate whose asymptotic test-error dynamics coincide with those of SGD. In simulations with a ReLU student, freezing the directions of the orthogonal complement improves generalization relative to training them. Finally, we show that the signal component rapidly aligns with the second-layer weights, revealing strong cross-layer coupling even in this shallow setting. Together, our results provide a dynamical explanation for how feature learning outside the teacher subspace harms generalization.

cross Knowing What You Know: A Trait Theory of Reviewer Value in Human-AI Cognitive Systems

Authors: Priyanka Sinha, Sugam Pandey

Abstract: When a capable model and a human reviewer cooperate on the same decision, which human still adds value? We argue this is a question about a single cognitive trait, metacognitive calibration: the alignment between a reviewer's behavioral confidence and the actual correctness of their judgments. We develop a trait theory of reviewer value to make it precise. The theory makes five claims: value is governed by calibration rather than by activity, speed, agreement, or tenure; calibration leaves observable traces in behavioral telemetry; those traces are non-diagnostic on their own, because the fast, low-revision signature of a calibrated expert is indistinguishable from that of a confident novice until it is gated by outcomes; human calibration and a model's self-knowledge are the same construct, so the joint system should defer to whichever agent is better calibrated on each item; and individual calibration is causally identifiable only above the level of the single judgment. We ground each claim in a six-month production window of an AI-assisted accounting system (541621 reviewer events, 483 reviewers), and find that a purely behavioral expertise profile inverts the ground truth, scoring an overconfident cohort (5.2 percent correction survival) higher than genuine experts (99.2 percent); that reviewer quality is flat across tenure, so the trait is not learned on the job; and that the same calibration logic that separates good from bad reviewers, applied to the model, concentrates 92.5 percent of human effort on genuine errors. We close with the boundary the theory must respect: under majority-vote aggregation, individual causal value is unidentifiable.

replace Loss-Controlling Calibration with Asymptotic Guarantees

Authors: Di Wang, Yujin Li, Junzhi Shi, Pingping Wang, Shuo Zhuang, Hongyue Li

Abstract: We propose a learning framework for calibrating predictive models to perform loss-controlling prediction, which extends our proposed conformal loss-controlling prediction framework to more general settings. The approach is a natural extension of conformal loss-controlling prediction, since it reduces to the latter when the predictors are set-valued with the nesting property and the losses are monotone. replace Survival of the Fittest Perturbations: Uncertainty-Aware Evolution of Adversarial Distributions for Robust Generalization

Authors: Junhao Dong, Zicheng Yu, Xinghua Qu, Z. Jane Wang, Yew-Soon Ong

Abstract: Despite deep learning's broad successes across diverse scenarios, its vulnerability to adversarial examples impedes deployment, making adversarial training a leading approach to improve robustness. However, pointwise adversarial training maps each clean sample to a single adversary, overlooking surrounding adversarial variability and the associated shifts in feature statistics. To address these issues, we propose Evolutionary Uncertainty-Aware Distributional Adversarial Training (EvoUAD-AT). Specifically, we design an evolutionary adversarial uncertainty modeling module that maintains an instance-wise search distribution over adversarial feature statistics around an adversarially generated anchor. Candidates are evaluated under the current model, selected by their threat to a benign reference, and used to adapt the distribution; strategy parameters are retained across epochs. To avoid anchoring on misclassified clean samples, we refine the alignment reference and match benign and adversarial distributions. replace Bigger Isn't Always Memorizing: Early Stopping Overparameterized Diffusion Models

Authors: Alessandro Favero, Antonio Sclocchi, Matthieu Wyart

Abstract: Diffusion probabilistic models have become a cornerstone of modern generative AI, yet the mechanisms underlying their generalization remain poorly understood. In fact, if these models were perfectly minimizing their training loss, they would just generate data belonging to their training set, i.e., memorize, as empirically found in the overparameterized regime. We revisit this view by showing that, in highly overparameterized diffusion models, generalization in natural data domains is progressively achieved during training before the onset of memorization. Our results, ranging from image to language diffusion models, systematically support the empirical law that memorization time is proportional to the dataset size, consistent with a kernel-regression bound on the time required to fit the empirical score at low noise. Generalization vs. memorization is then best understood as a competition between time scales. We show that this phenomenology is recovered in diffusion models learning a simple probabilistic context-free grammar with random rules, where generalization corresponds to the hierarchical acquisition of deeper grammar rules as training time grows, and the generalization cost of early stopping can be characterized. We summarize these results in a phase diagram. Overall, our results support that a principled early-stopping criterion - scaling with dataset size - can effectively optimize generalization while avoiding memorization, with direct implications for hyperparameter transfer and privacy-sensitive applications.

replace Hyperparameter Optimization via Budget Allocation in Lipschitz Spaces

Authors: MohammadHossein Bateni, Hossein Esfandiari, Samira HosseinGhorban, Alireza Mirrokni, Radin Shahdaei

Abstract: Hyperparameter optimization and model selection often require allocating a limited evaluation budget across many candidate configurations, where performance improves with additional training or resources. We study this setting when each configuration has an unknown performance curve over budget. Similarity between configurations induces similarity between their curves, capturing geometric structure in the search space. We formalize the resulting decision problem as Unknown Value Probing and propose budget allocation strategies that combine principled exploration of the configuration space with value-informed concentration on regions supported by observed evidence. We prove worst-case approximation guarantees for our approach and show these guarantees are near-optimal by matching corresponding hardness barriers. Extensive experiments on large benchmark suites spanning more than 250 experimental settings demonstrate consistent gains over strong baselines in both anytime behavior and final incumbent performance.

replace Assessing the robustness of heterogeneous treatment effects in survival analysis under informative censoring

Authors: Yuxin Wang, Dennis Frauen, Jonas Schweisthal, Maresa Schr\"oder, Stefan Feuerriegel

Abstract: Dropout is common in clinical studies, with up to half of patients leaving early due to side effects or other reasons. When dropout is informative (i.e., dependent on survival time), it introduces censoring bias, because of which treatment effect estimates are also biased. In this paper, we propose an assumption-lean framework to assess the robustness of conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimates in survival analysis when facing censoring bias. Unlike existing works that rely on strong assumptions, such as non-informative censoring, to obtain point estimation, we use partial identification to derive informative bounds on the CATE. replace Consecutive Preferential Bayesian Optimization

Authors: Aras Erarslan, Carlos Sevilla Salcedo, Ville Tanskanen, Anni Nisov, Eero P\"aiv\"akumpu, Heikki Aisala, Kaisu Honkap\"a\"a, Arto Klami, Petrus Mikkola

Abstract: Preferential Bayesian optimization allows optimization of objectives that are either expensive or difficult to measure directly, by relying on a minimal number of comparative evaluations done by a human expert. Generating candidate solutions for evaluation is also often expensive, but existing preferential optimization methods do not account for this production cost. We introduce Consecutive Preferential Bayesian Optimization (CPBO) for the consecutive setting, where production cost is reduced by reusing the previous candidate in each comparison. replace Meta-RL with Bayesian Linear Task Models

Authors: Jingyang You, Hanna Kurniawati

Abstract: Deep Bayesian reinforcement learning adapts to unseen tasks by inferring latent transition and reward models, but existing methods typically rely on variational posteriors and evidence lower bounds, introducing approximation error and unstable task representations. We introduce GLiBRL, a deep Bayesian RL framework that combines generalised linear task models with learnable non-linear basis functions. GLiBRL features conjugate Bayesian inference, yielding exact, sequential posterior updates over task parameters and model noise, together with a closed-form marginal likelihood that eliminates variational inference. The update is naturally permutation-invariant, allowing GLiBRL to integrate with both off- and on-policy algorithms. GLiBRL also learns task representation admitting an exact kernel identity, relating distances between task representations to kernel discrepancies over the task contexts. Compared against eight representative or recent meta reinforcement learning methods, GLiBRL achieves the highest aggregate zero-shot test performance on both the MuJoCo locomotion and MetaWorld manipulation benchmarks.

replace CoreQ: Mismatch-Corrected Successive Rounding for LLM Quantization

Authors: Seohyeon Cha, Huancheng Chen, Dongjun Kim, Haoran Zhang, Kevin Chan, Gustavo de Veciana, Haris Vikalo

Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) enables efficient deployment of large language models by mapping pretrained weights to low-bit formats without retraining, typically using a small calibration set to minimize a layer-wise calibration objective. However, this sequential procedure induces a mismatch: errors from earlier quantized layers alter the inputs received by later layers, causing the activations to deviate from those of the full-precision model. Recent approaches introduce mismatch-aware calibration objectives to compensate for this effect, but leave open how much of the observed mismatch should shift each layer's calibration target. We show that fully applying the empirical mismatch correction can overfit finite calibration data, while using a fixed correction coefficient ignores variation in mismatch estimates across layers. To address these limitations, we propose CoreQ, a learning-free PTQ framework that uses a geometric decomposition of the mismatch to compute a closed-form, layer-adaptive correction coefficient. This coefficient controls how much of the observed mismatch enters each layer's calibration target, reducing overfitting to limited calibration data without coefficient tuning. CoreQ then applies efficient greedy successive rounding to the resulting triangular least-squares objective. Its bounded beam-search extension, K-CoreQ, trades modest additional compute for improved performance. Across multiple LLM families, scales, bit-widths, and quantization settings, CoreQ and K-CoreQ achieve lower perplexity and higher downstream accuracy than strong PTQ baselines. Code is available at GitHub.

replace Pragmatic Curiosity: A Unified Framework for Hybrid Learning and Optimization via Active Inference

Authors: Yingke Li, Anjali Parashar, Enlu Zhou, Chuchu Fan

Abstract: Many engineering and scientific workflows rely on expensive black-box evaluations, requiring sequential decisions that must both improve task performance and reduce uncertainty. Bayesian optimization (BO) and Bayesian experimental design (BED) provide powerful but largely separate treatments of goal-directed optimization and information-seeking experimentation, leaving limited guidance for hybrid settings in which learning and optimization are intrinsically coupled. We propose Pragmatic Curiosity (PraC), a unified framework for hybrid learning and optimization via active inference. PraC evaluates candidate queries by trading information gain about a task-relevant latent symbol against an expected regret-based potential over outcomes. This formulation foregrounds three operational design choices: which latent quantity should be clarified, how task value is encoded as regret, and how strongly information gain should be exchanged against pragmatic regret. We instantiate PraC across three regimes of increasing complexity: decision-oriented monitoring with fixed global symbols and known downstream losses, targeted active search with induced local symbols and evolving coverage goals, and composite Bayesian optimization with hierarchical regret learning under unknown preferences. Across these regimes, PraC reduces downstream decision risk, improves coverage of critical outcome regions, and jointly learns predictive and preference structures without relying on task-specific staging rules.

replace Towards Generalizable Reasoning: Group Causal Counterfactual Policy Optimization for LLM Reasoning

Authors: Jingyao Wang, Xingyu Zhang, Peizheng Guo, Jiahuan Zhou, Zhenghao Zhang, Changwen Zheng, Chuxiong Sun, Wenwen Qiang

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) excel at complex tasks with advances in reasoning capabilities. However, existing reward mechanisms remain tightly coupled to final correctness and pay little attention to the underlying reasoning process: trajectories with sound reasoning but wrong answers receive low credit, while lucky guesses with flawed logic may be highly rewarded, affecting reasoning generalization. From a causal perspective, we interpret multi-candidate reasoning for a fixed question as a family of counterfactual experiments. Building on this, we propose Group Causal Counterfactual Policy Optimization to explicitly train LLMs to learn generalizable reasoning patterns. It proposes an episodic causal counterfactual reward that jointly captures (i) robustness, encouraging the answer distribution induced by a reasoning step to remain stable under counterfactual perturbations; and (ii) effectiveness, enforcing sufficient variability so that the learned reasoning strategy can transfer across questions. We then construct token-level advantages from this reward and optimize the policy, encouraging LLMs to favor reasoning patterns that are process-valid and counterfactually robust. Extensive experiments on diverse benchmarks demonstrate its advantages.

replace Signature-Kernel Based Evaluation Metrics for Robust Probabilistic and Tail-Event Forecasting

Authors: Benjamin R. Redhead, Thomas L. Lee, Peng Gu, V\'ictor Elvira, Amos Storkey

Abstract: Standard sample-based metrics like Continuous Ranked Probability Score (CRPS) and Quantile Loss (QL) are frequently used in the evaluation of probabilistic time-series forecasting. However, these metrics fail to capture multivariate correlations or assess the ability to capture distribution tails. These global metrics are insensitive to errors on high-utility events in distributional tails due to over-representation of the distribution's body. To accurately measure distributional fidelity on tail-regions and regions of high utility while still having a proper scoring rule, we introduce a family of censored signature kernel metrics. Our proposed metrics concentrate evaluation of forecasts to a focus region, representing high-utility or distributional tails, by collapsing the body of the distribution to a single pivot. To encourage the use of the proposed metrics we open-source the efficient signature kernel (ESK) library. This library facilitates batch computation, with custom triton kernels, achieving a speed-up of up to 3.58$\times$ compared to implementations via the popular SigKernel library. Authors: Cl\'audio Correia, Alberto E. A. Ferreira, Lucas Martins, Miguel P. Bento, Sofia Guerreiro, Ricardo Ribeiro Pereira, Ana Sofia Gomes, Jacopo Bono, Hugo Ferreira, Pedro Bizarro

Abstract: In binary classification systems, decision thresholds translate model scores into actions. Choosing suitable thresholds relies on the specific distribution of the underlying model scores but also on the specific business decisions of each client using that model. However, retraining models inevitably shifts score distributions, invalidating existing thresholds. In multi-tenant Score-as-a-Service environments, where decision boundaries reside in client-managed infrastructure, this creates a severe bottleneck: recalibration requires coordinating threshold updates across hundreds of clients, consuming excessive human hours and leading to model stagnation. Deployed at scale by Feedzai, MUSE processes over 4 thousand events per second, and over 55 billion events in the last 12 months, across several dozens of tenants, while maintaining high-availability and low-latency guarantees. replace Unlocking Continuous Diffusion Language Models via Discrete Stochastic Localization

Authors: Yunshu Wu, Jiayi Cheng, Longxuan Yu, Partha Thakuria, Rob Brekelmans, Evangelos E. Papalexakis, Greg Ver Steeg

Abstract: Continuous diffusion is a natural framework for non-autoregressive generation but has generally lagged behind masked discrete diffusion models (MDMs) on discrete sequence generation. We argue that continuous diffusion can effectively model discrete sequences through the joint design of noise parameterization and token representation geometry. We introduce \emph{Discrete Stochastic Localization} (DSL), a continuous-state framework that combines a stochastic localization channel with unit-sphere token embeddings, resulting in a Bayes-optimal denoiser that depends only on the noisy state, without additional timestep conditioning.
