The same checkpoint supports random-order autoregressive sampling, and a hybrid continuous-then-discrete sampler using as few as $T=48$ total steps, without distillation or further training.

replace Counterfactual Feature Regularization for Expansion-Based Class-Incremental Learning

Authors: Zhen Zhang, Jielei Chu, Jiangtao Hu, Bin Liu, Jie Wang, Ya Liu, Fengmao Lv, Tianrui Li

Abstract: Current expansion-based methods for Class Incremental Learning (CIL) effectively mitigate catastrophic forgetting by freezing old features. However, such task-specific features learned from the new task may collide with the old features. From a causal perspective, spurious feature correlations are the main cause of this collision, manifesting in two scopes: (i) guided by empirical risk minimization (ERM), intra-task spurious correlations cause task-specific features to rely on shortcut features. These non-robust features are vulnerable to interference, inevitably drifting into the feature space of other tasks; (ii) inter-task spurious correlations induce semantic confusion between visually similar classes across tasks. To address this, we propose a Probability of Necessity and Sufficiency (PNS)-based regularization method to guide feature expansion in CIL. Specifically, we first extend the definition of PNS to expansion-based CIL, termed CPNS, which quantifies both the causal completeness of intra-task representations and the separability of inter-task representations. We then introduce a dual-scope counterfactual generator based on twin networks to ensure the measurement of CPNS, which simultaneously generates: (i) intra-task counterfactual features to minimize intra-task PNS risk and ensure causal completeness of task-specific features, and (ii) inter-task interfering features to minimize inter-task PNS risk, ensuring the separability of inter-task representations. Theoretical analyses confirm its reliability. The regularization is a plug-and-play method for expansion-based CIL to mitigate feature collision. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.

replace Auditing Path-Specific Fairness Across Causal Discovery Algorithms: A Heart Failure Case Study

Authors: Nitish Nagesh, Elahe Khatibi, Thomas Hughes, Mahdi Bagheri, Pratik Gajane, Amir M. Rahmani

Abstract: Causal discovery is increasingly applied to healthcare data, where the recovered relationships can encode biased pathways that propagate to downstream prediction. Path-specific fairness analysis, through direct, indirect, and spurious effect decompositions along with fairness-utility ratio assessment allows bias quantification and associated prediction cost. However, existing evaluations of causal discovery under fairness constraints have largely used non-clinical datasets and have not separated the contribution of confounders from that of mediators, leaving open how discovery-algorithm choice propagates through path-specific fairness to the utility trade-off in clinical settings. This work addresses that gap by establishing an expert-elicited causal graph benchmark for the heart failure clinical records dataset and tracing discovery algorithms through composite, per-variable fairness decompositions and fairness-utility ratio. ICA-LiNGAM achieves the lowest structural Hamming distance and highest recall, while GES achieves the highest precision. For path-specific effects, there was a 1.2 percentage-point difference in the mediated effect on ejection fraction on overall causal fairness between the original and discovered graphs. This ultimately led to variations in the fairness-utility ratio highlighting need for fine-grained evaluation. These results indicate that discovery-aware, path-specific fairness evaluation is consequential in clinical settings and warrants inclusion in routine auditing of clinical risk models.

replace Shapley meets Rawls: an integrated framework for measuring and explaining unfairness

Authors: Fadoua Amri-Jouidel, Emmanuel Kemel, St\'ephane Mussard

Abstract: Explainability and fairness have mainly been considered separately, with recent exceptions trying the explain the sources of unfairness. This paper shows that the Shapley value can be used to both define and explain unfairness, under standard group fairness criteria. This offers an integrated framework to estimate and derive inference on unfairness as-well-as the features that contribute to it. Our framework can also be extended from Shapley values to the family of Efficient-Symmetric-Linear (ESL) values, some of which offer more robust definitions of fairness, and shorter computation times. An illustration is run on the Census Income dataset from the UCI Machine Learning Repository. Our approach shows that ``Age", ``Number of hours" and ``Marital status" generate gender unfairness, using shorter computation time than traditional Bootstrap tests.

replace The multiplicative Lipschitz bound as an inductive bias

Authors: Simon Kuang, Yuezhu Xu, S. Sivaranjani, Xinfan Lin

Abstract: We study the problem of training a neural network to minimize a loss function given a Lipschitz constraint. While the most obvious way to estimate the Lipschitz constant of a neural network is to multiply the Lipschitz constants of its layers, this bound, which we term the _trivial bound_, is often considered impractically loose. We show that, on the contrary, it can be more effective than state-of-the-art methods (Sandwich, Almost-Orthogonal Layers, Convex Potential Layers, and SDP-based Lipschitz Layers) for Lipschitz-constrained learning. Our method combines the trivial bound with a novel activation function (the quadrature nonlinearity) to achieve lower cross-entropy over most of the tested classification bounds. For autoencoders constrained to be nonexpansive in a learned norm, it achieves the lowest pixel reconstruction error at every tested bottleneck width, improving on PCA by 12--24%. Our architecture, consisting of concatenated cosine and sine activations, is supported by a semi-rigorous theoretical analysis of Lipschitz bound looseness that may prove valuable in its own right.

replace Optimistic Actor-Critic with Parametric Policies for Linear Markov Decision Processes

Authors: Max Qiushi Lin, Reza Asad, Kevin Tan, Haque Ishfaq, Csaba Szepesvari, Sharan Vaswani

Abstract: Although actor-critic methods have been successful in practice, their theoretical analyses have several limitations. Specifically, existing theoretical work either sidesteps the exploration problem by making strong assumptions or analyzes impractical methods with complicated algorithmic modifications. Moreover, the actor-critic methods analyzed for linear MDPs often employ natural policy gradient and define "implicit" policies through the accumulated critic estimates, rather than learning an explicitly parameterized actor. Such policies can be computationally expensive to sample from, making the environment interactions inefficient. To that end, we focus on the finite-horizon linear MDPs and propose an optimistic actor-critic framework that uses parametric log-linear policies. In particular, we introduce a tractable logit-matching regression objective for the actor. For the critic, we use approximate Thompson sampling via Langevin Monte Carlo to obtain optimistic value estimates. We prove that, when the policy features are sufficiently expressive, the resulting algorithm achieves $\widetilde{O}(\epsilon^{-4})$ and $\widetilde{O}(\epsilon^{-2})$ sample complexity in the on-policy and off-policy settings, respectively. Our results match the state-of-the-art dependence on $\epsilon$ in prior theoretical work, while our algorithm is more aligned with practice.

replace Training-Free Dynamic Upcycling of Expert Language Models

Authors: Eros Fan\`i, O\u{g}uzhan Ersoy

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable performance on a wide range of specialized tasks, exhibiting strong problem-solving capabilities. However, training these models is prohibitively expensive, and they often lack domain-specific expertise because they rely on general knowledge datasets. Expertise finetuning can address this issue; however, it often leads to overspecialization, and developing a single multi-domain expert remains difficult due to diverging objectives. Furthermore, multitask training is challenging due to interference and catastrophic forgetting. Existing work proposes combining the expertise of dense models within a Mixture of Experts (MoE) architecture, although this approach still requires multitask finetuning. To address these issues, we introduce Dynamic Upcycling MoE (DUME), a novel approach that reuses dense experts trained on different domains to construct a unified MoE model. Our method builds a single multitask model that preserves the capabilities of the original dense experts without requiring additional training. DUME is both cost-efficient and scalable: by leveraging the closed-form solution of ridge regression, it eliminates the need for further optimization and enables experts to be added or removed dynamically. Our extensive experiments on different tasks (in causal language modeling and reasoning settings) with several model sizes (between 115M and 9B parameters) and architectures demonstrate that DUME consistently outperforms baseline approaches. In particular, in a reasoning setting, DUME can also surpass the dense expert model's performance in its domain, achieving 104% of the dense expert's performance on average. Finally, we also show that the DUME model can be fine-tuned to further improve performance in complex reasoning scenarios. Our code is available at: github.com/gensyn-ai/dume.

replace Reading Human Preference from Looped Transformer Internal States

Authors: Jan Kirin

Abstract: Show a frozen language model two replies to the same request, one of which a person preferred. Can a small probe on the model's hidden states tell which? On Ouro-2.6B-Thinking, a model that runs its layers four times per token, and Anthropic's HH-RLHF data, the answer is yes, but only faintly. A linear probe picks the preferred reply 61.5% of the time, a small nonlinear network that scores each reply separately 64.9%, and the evaluator trained for version 1 reaches 64.7% on the test split: clearly better than simple rules such as picking the longer reply, but weak. Whether preference is stored as a comparison between the two replies or as a property of each is still open. A linear probe cannot tell, and a network that reads both replies together has an estimated edge of only 0.7 points (95% CI 0.0 to 1.4) over one that scores each reply separately. These numbers hold under three controls: scoring every pair in both orders, splitting the data by pair, and, for the probes, keeping the end of each conversation, where the two replies differ. Version 1's 95.2% and 84.5% were obtained without the first two and do not hold; version 2's corrected relational-probe accuracy, obtained without the third, was four to five points too low. This version replaces both.

replace Distinct mechanisms underlying in-context learning in transformers

Authors: Cole Gibson, Wenping Cui, Gautam Reddy

Abstract: Modern distributed networks, notably transformers, acquire a remarkable ability (termed `in-context learning') to adapt their computation to input statistics, such that a fixed network can be applied to data from a broad range of systems. Here, we provide a mechanistic characterization of this behavior in transformers trained on a finite set $S$ of discrete Markov chains. The transformer displays four algorithmic phases, characterized by whether the network memorizes or generalizes, and whether it uses 1-point or 2-point statistics. We show that the four phases are implemented by multi-layer subcircuits that exemplify two qualitatively distinct mechanisms for implementing context-adaptive computations. Minimal models isolate the key features of both motifs. Memorization and generalization phases are delineated by two boundaries that depend on data diversity, $K = |S|$. The first ($K_1^\ast$) is set by a kinetic competition between subcircuits and the second ($K_2^\ast$) is set by a representational bottleneck. A symmetry-constrained theory of a transformer's training dynamics explains the sharp transition from 1-point to 2-point generalization and identifies key features of the loss landscape that allow the network to generalize. Put together, we show that transformers develop distinct subcircuits to implement in-context learning and identify conditions that favor certain mechanisms over others.

replace Observable Performance Does Not Fully Reflect Adaptive System Organization: A Multi-Level Analysis of Gait Dynamics Under Occlusal Constraint

Authors: Jacques Raynal, Pierre Slangen, Elsa Raynal, Jacques Margerit

Abstract: In biomechanical systems, observable performance is often used as a proxy for underlying organization, although similar outputs may arise from different adaptive configurations. This study considers the vertical dimension of occlusion (VDO) as a constraint applied to an adaptive neuromechanical system. A single-case design in a patient with Parkinson's disease enabled repeated intra-individual gait observations under six occlusal probes. Three complementary analytical levels were examined: (i) an aggregated scalar score of observable performance, (ii) a conceptual dynamical systems framework, and (iii) an exploratory UMAP representation of 55 standardized biomechanical variables from 270 M1 observations. The revised Level 1 analysis showed that the relative ranking of OC2.5 and OC3 depended on score construction, while their scalar distributions remained close. The Level 3 embedding showed substantial overlap among all six probes and did not identify independently separated condition-specific clusters. OC2.5 and OC3 displayed limited centroid displacement but broad observation-level overlap. The principal result is therefore representational non-identifiability: neither the aggregated score nor the selected low-dimensional embedding uniquely identifies an occlusal-condition-specific system state. VDO is interpreted as a constraint parameter rather than a causal determinant. The findings are exploratory, model dependent, and non causal. They do not establish distinct physiological states, an optimal VDO, clinical thresholds, or diagnostic, predictive, mechanistic, or prescriptive validity.

replace TraXion: Rethinking Pre-training Frameworks for Mobility and Beyond

Authors: Shang-Ling Hsu, Mark Tenzer, Cyrus Shahabi, Khurram Shafique

Abstract: Human mobility differs from text and from generic time series in three structural ways: visits are tuple-valued events whose meaning depends on the joint distribution over location, time, and activity; users carry persistent signatures across trajectories; and visits are not independent across users, since co-location at shared places is a primary signal. Existing pre-training recipes for mobility import objectives from language modeling, treating trajectories as sentences and visits as tokens, an analogy that fails against each of the three properties above. These properties define a broader class, multi-entity spatiotemporal event streams (MESES), spanning enterprise authentication logs, electronic health records, and other event-stream domains where entities share infrastructure, schedules, or contexts. We make the properties precise as three axioms that any pre-training framework for MESES should satisfy, and introduce TraXion, whose objectives and architecture are jointly designed to meet them. A single TraXion checkpoint per dataset leads task-specific baselines on every location-ranking and detection metric across six public mobility datasets covering anomaly detection, next-POI recommendation, next-visit prediction, and social-link prediction. The same recipe, applied unchanged to enterprise authentication logs and ICU mortality prediction, matches or exceeds prior work on both, showing that event streams from domains as different as mobility, security, and healthcare can be modeled under a single framework.

replace StreamPhy: Streaming Inference of High-Dimensional Physical Dynamics via State Space Models

Authors: Panqi Chen, Yifan Sun, Shikai Fang, Xiao Fu, Lei Cheng

Abstract: Inferring the evolution of high-dimensional and multi-modal (e.g., spatio-temporal) physical fields from irregular sparse measurements in real time is a fundamental challenge in science and engineering. Existing approaches, including diffusion-based generative models and functional tensor methods, typically operate in offline settings, depend on full temporal observations, or incur substantial inference cost. We propose StreamPhy, an end-to-end framework that enables efficient and accurate streaming inference of full-field physical dynamics from incoming irregular sparse measurements. The framework integrates a data-adaptive observation encoder that is robust to arbitrary observation patterns, a structured state-space model that supports memory-efficient online updates across irregular time intervals, and an expressive Functional Tensor Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FT-FiLM) decoder for continuous-field generation. We prove that FT-FiLM is more expressive than the functional Tucker model, admitting a richer function class for handling complex dynamics. Experiments on three representative physical systems under challenging sampling patterns show that StreamPhy consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, with at least 48\% improvement in accuracy and up to 20--100X faster inference than diffusion-based methods.

replace Sparse Layers are Critical to Scaling Looped Language Models

Authors: Ryan Lee, Jacob Biloki, Edward J. Hu, Jonathan May

Abstract: Looped language models repeat a set of transformer layers through depth, reducing memory costs and providing natural early-exit points at loop boundaries. However, looped models do not scale as favorably as standard transformers with unique layers. We compare standard and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) transformers, with and without looping, and find two main results. First, we find Looped-MoE models scale better than the standard baseline while dense looped models do not. We trace this to routing divergence between loops: in Looped-MoE models, different experts are activated on each pass through the same shared layers, recovering expressivity without additional parameters. Our second finding is that looped models have better compute-quality trade-offs with early exits than standard models. Because each loop ends with the same layers that produce the final output, loop boundaries are superior exit points, as confirmed by earlier output convergence at these points. In sum, we provide a clear direction for scaling looped models: a Looped-MoE model with early exits can not only beat standard transformers at scale, but also enable significant memory and inference savings with minimal degradation in quality.

replace Inverse Design for Conditional Distribution Matching

Authors: Ori Meidler, Shaul Tolkowsky, Or Zuk

Abstract: Generative models are powerful tools for sampling from a learned distribution $\mathcal{P}(Y \mid X)$, and inverse-design methods invert this map to find an input $x$ that produces a desired point output $y^*$. However, many design goals are naturally distributional rather than pointwise, incorporating the inherent uncertainty of $Y$ and targeting a specific form for it, a task not addressed by standard inverse design. To address this issue we introduce Conditional Distribution Matching (CDM), a new inverse-design problem class in generative modeling: given a joint distribution $\mathcal{P}(X, Y)$ and a target distribution $\mathcal{G}(Y)$, find an input $x^*$ whose induced conditional distribution $\mathcal{P}(Y \mid X = x^*)$ matches $\mathcal{G}$. We formally define two variants: Conditional Distribution Matching Sampling (CDMS) and Conditional Distribution Matching Optimization (CDMO). To solve these problems, we propose MLGD-F (Matching-Loss Guided Diffusion with a Fast inner sampler), a plug-and-play inference-time algorithm that combines a pretrained score-based diffusion model with a pretrained fast conditional sampler, requiring no additional training or fine-tuning. By leveraging single-step conditional sampling, MLGD-F enables tractable gradient computation, making the estimation of $\mathcal{P}(Y \mid X)$ both memory-efficient and computationally lightweight. We validate MLGD-F on synthetic benchmarks, structured image transformations, and generative editing optimization, demonstrating reliable recovery of inputs whose conditional distributions match diverse user-specified targets, including discrete mixtures and continuous low-rank supports.

replace Signature Approach for Contextual Bandits with Nonlinear and Path-dependent Rewards: Regret Bound and Statistical Inference

Authors: Xin Guo, Grace He, Xinyu Li

Abstract: We study contextual bandits with nonlinear and path-dependent rewards through a novel signature-transform-based approach. Leveraging the universal nonlinearity property of signatures, we approximate continuous path-dependent reward functionals by linear functionals in the signature space. This representation enables the use of efficient linear contextual bandit methods while preserving expressive sequential structure. Building on this framework, we propose \texttt{DisSigUCB}, a signature-based disjoint upper confidence bound (UCB) algorithm. Under boundedness and non-degeneracy assumptions, we establish a high-probability regret bound of order \(\tilde\mathcal{O}\bigl(d_{\text{Sig}} \sqrt{KT} + \ varepsilon_N T \sqrt{d_\Sig}\bigr)\) with \(K\) being the number of actions, \(d_{\text{Sig}} \) the signature feature dimension, and \(\varepsilon_N\) the finite-depth approximation error. We further introduce $\zeta$-\texttt{DisSigUCB}, a randomized variant that ensures persistent exploration for statistical inference. Under additional margin and long-run design conditions, we prove consistency and asymptotic normality of the action-specific ridge estimators. Both the synthetic experiments and the numerical studies on temperature sensor monitoring, sleep-stage classification, and hospital nurse staffing demonstrate the effectiveness of \texttt{DisSigUCB} over linear and kernelized contextual bandit baselines in nonlinear and path-dependent settings.

replace Minimum Neural Weight Norm = Kolmogorov Complexity

Authors: Tiberiu Musat

Abstract: Why do neural networks generalize better when their parameter norm is small? We prove that, in looped neural networks with constant-precision weights, the smallest weight norm of a network computing a function is asymptotically equivalent to the Kolmogorov complexity of that function, divided by its logarithm. The proof has two reductions: looped neural networks can encode information at a rate of logarithmically many bits per non-zero parameter encoded via the sparsity pattern, while any fixed-precision network can be described by enumerating its non-zero parameters with logarithmic addressing cost. Both bounds have the same leading constant factors, giving an asymptotic equivalence. More broadly, by establishing a theoretical link between norm minimization and algorithmic simplicity, we provide an avenue towards understanding generalization in modern deep learning.

replace MIST: Reliable Streaming Decision Trees for Online Class-Incremental Learning via McDiarmid Bound

Authors: Phu-Hoa Pham, Chi-Nguyen Tran, Nguyen Lam Phu Quy, Dao Sy Duy Minh, Huynh Trung Kiet, Long Tran-Thanh

Abstract: Streaming decision trees are natural candidates for open-world continual learning, as they perform local updates, enjoy bounded memory, and static decision boundaries. Despite these, they still fail in online class-incremental learning due to two coupled miscalibrations: (i) their split criterion grows unreliable as the class count K expands, and (ii) the absence of knowledge transfer at split time. Both failures share a common root: the range of Information Gain intrinsically scales with log2 K. Consequently, any Hoeffding-style confidence radius derived from it must inevitably grow with the class count, making a K-independent split criterion structurally impossible, taking away the potential benefits of applying streaming decision trees to continual learning. To fix this issue, we present MIST (McDiarmid Incremental Streaming Tree), which resolves both failures through three integrated components: (i) a tight, K-independent McDiarmid confidence radius for Gini splitting that acts as a structural regulariser; (ii) a Bayesian inheritance protocol that projects parent statistics to child nodes via truncated-Gaussian moments, with variance reduction guarantees strongest precisely when splitting is most conservative; and (iii) per-leaf KLL quantile sketches that support both continuous threshold evaluation and geometry-adaptive leaf prediction from a single data structure. On standard and stress-test tabular streams, MIST is competitive with global parametric methods on near-Gaussian benchmarks and uniquely robust on non-Gaussian geometry where SOTA benchmarks collapse.

replace Learning to Decide with AI Assistance under Human-Alignment

Authors: Nina Corvelo Benz, Eleni Straitouri, Manuel Gomez-Rodriguez

Abstract: It is widely agreed that when AI models assist decision-makers in high-stakes domains by predicting an outcome of interest, they should communicate the confidence of their predictions. However, empirical evidence suggests that decision-makers often struggle to determine when to trust a prediction based solely on this communicated confidence. In this context, recent theoretical and empirical work suggests a positive correlation between the utility of AI-assisted decision-making and the degree of alignment between the AI confidence and the decision-makers' confidence in their own predictions. Crucially, these findings do not yet elucidate the extent to which this alignment influences the complexity of learning to make optimal decisions through repeated interactions. In this paper, we address this question in the canonical case of binary predictions and binary decisions. We first show that this problem is equivalent to a two-armed online contextual learning problem with full feedback, and establish a lower bound of $\Omega (\sqrt{|H| \cdot |B| \cdot T} )$ on the expected regret any learner can attain, where $H$ and $B$ denote the sets of human and AI confidence values. We then demonstrate that, under perfect alignment between AI and human confidence, a learner can attain an expected regret of $O(\sqrt{|H| \cdot T\log T})$ and, when $\sqrt{|H|} = O(\log T)$ and $B$ is countable, a non-trivial generalization of the Dvoretzky-Kiefer-Wolfowitz inequality improves the regret bound to $O(\sqrt{T\log T})$. Taken together, these results reveal that alignment can reduce the complexity of learning to make decisions with AI assistance. Experiments on real data from two different human-subject studies where participants solve simple decision-making tasks assisted by AI models show that our theoretical results are robust to violations of perfect alignment.

replace REALM: Retrospective Encoder Alignment for LFP Modeling

Authors: Peicheng Wu, Zhenyu Bu, Runze Ma, Lin Du

Abstract: Spike activity has been the dominant neural signal for behavior decoding because its high spatiotemporal resolution supports accurate decoding. However, as intracortical brain-computer interfaces (iBCIs) move toward higher channel counts and wireless operation, the high sampling rates required to record spikes create substantial power and bandwidth demands. Local field potentials (LFPs) offer complementary advantages, including greater long-term stability, lower energy consumption, and lower bandwidth requirements. However, LFP-based decoders often achieve lower accuracy and rely on non-causal architectures that cannot be used directly for real-time deployment. We propose REALM, a retrospective knowledge distillation (RKD) framework for causal LFP behavior decoding. Inspired by offline-to-online distillation in speech recognition, REALM transfers non-causal representational knowledge from a pretrained, multi-session bidirectional LFP teacher to a causal student model. We first pretrain a bidirectional Mamba-2 teacher across multiple recording sessions using continuous masked autoencoding (CMAE), and then distill its representation into a compact causal student using a combined objective of representation alignment and autoencoding. REALM achieves the highest mean accuracy among the compared decoders in both label-free and fine-tuned pipelines, with statistically significant improvements over each baseline, including the state-of-the-art CrossModalDistill. It does so using LFPs alone throughout pretraining, distillation, and decoding, with less than half the parameters of CrossModalDistill's published student and one-tenth of its pretraining time. These results show that a causal LFP-only model can achieve decoding accuracy competitive with a non-causal multi-modal model, offering a practical and scalable approach for next-generation wireless and implantable iBCIs.

replace What competing constraints reveal about representation learning

Authors: Gwenol\'e Quellec

Abstract: Learning latent states from temporal, multimodal, and partially observed data requires decisions about what information representations should retain, discard, and organize. I argue that six complementary properties help clarify these decisions: predictive sufficiency, minimality, temporal coherence, observation compatibility, nuisance invariance, and structural constraints. Drawing on established approaches, I distinguish desired representation properties from the objectives used to encourage them and the diagnostics used to assess them, since neither a training objective nor an evaluation metric guarantees that a property has been achieved. Controlled synthetic experiments examine how training objectives influence predictive performance, nuisance decodability, and latent organization. The results reveal discrepancies between seemingly favorable diagnostics and the information retained in learned representations, as well as latent-coordinate ambiguities under equivalent predictive performance, illustrating how complementary evaluations can improve interpretation and guide design choices. Together, these analyses provide a basis for designing, comparing, and evaluating learned latent states.

replace KVBuffer: IO-aware Serving for Linear Attention

Authors: Longwei Zou, Lin Zhong

Abstract: Linear attention has recently gained significant attention for long-context inference due to its constant decoding cost with respect to context length. However, existing serving systems typically serve linear attention by recurrently computing and updating a large linear attention state in every decoding step. Since the state is much larger than the per-token key and value, recurrent decoding incurs substantial memory access and becomes inefficient for serving linear attention. In this paper, we propose KVBuffer, an IO-aware serving mechanism for linear attention. By buffering recent keys and values, KVBuffer enables serving systems to compute linear attention outputs in more flexible and memory-efficient ways. For decoding, KVBuffer enables chunkwise computation, which reduces average memory access and decoding latency by deferring state updates and applying them in batch. For speculative decoding, KVBuffer verifies draft tokens in parallel and avoids storing temporary states. For short contexts, KVBuffer computes attention outputs directly from buffered keys and values, without creating or updating the linear attention state. We implement KVBuffer in SGLang for Qwen3.5 and Nemotron-3 series models. Our evaluations show that KVBuffer can speedup batch decoding by up to 1.47x and increase the maximum number of serving requests by 5x for speculative decoding when verifying four draft tokens.

replace The Silent Hyperparameter: Quantifying the Impact of Inference Backends on LLM Reproducibility

Authors: David Pape, Jonathan Evertz, Lea Sch\"onherr

Abstract: Progress in LLMs is increasingly measured through standardized benchmarks, where state-of-the-art improvements are often separated by fractions of a percentage point. At the same time, the computational cost of evaluating modern LLMs has driven widespread adoption of specialized inference backends, software systems that execute trained models efficiently at inference time. While critical for scalability, system-level optimizations, such as custom CUDA kernels and reduced-precision arithmetic, can alter token probabilities and introduce non-determinism, possibly cascading into divergent generation. In this work, we first survey the inference landscape, identifying $200$ distinct engines, and analyze $35,000$ ML publications, finding that the specific inference stack is rarely reported despite this widespread diversity. We then present a systematic empirical study of how inference backends affect LLM benchmark results. Holding model weights, decoding parameters, and hardware constant, we evaluate five widely used inference engines, including vLLM, SGLang, and llama$.$cpp, across multiple open-weight models spanning 4B-30B parameters and established benchmarks. We show that the choice of backend alone can shift benchmark scores by up to $16.6$ percentage points and induce high rates of output disagreement. By isolating backend optimizations and tracing the execution pipeline, we find this divergence is driven by system-level optimizations such as prefix caching and CUDA graphs, custom kernels, and engine-specific defaults in logit processing. Our findings identify the inference backend as a previously unreported yet consequential hyperparameter in LLM evaluations and advocate standardized reporting of inference stacks to improve the reproducibility and interpretability of benchmark comparisons.

replace Measuring What Matters: Synthetic Benchmarks for Concept Bottleneck Models

Authors: Julian Skirzynski, Harry Cheon, Shreyas Kadekodi, Meredith Stewart, Berk Ustun

Abstract: Concept bottleneck models predict using high-level concepts detected in inputs. Although these models provide a simple way to reap benefits from interpretability, they are hard to train and evaluate because very few real-world datasets include concept labels. In this paper, we develop synthetic benchmarks for concept bottleneck models. With our benchmarks, researchers can generate labeled datasets and control properties that affect performance, such as data modality, the choice of concepts, or annotation quality. They can thus find problems that suit these models and isolate the factors behind their failures. We demonstrate how our benchmarks can be used to evaluate major use cases for concept bottleneck models: decision support, i.e., helping human experts make better decisions; and automation, i.e., handling routine tasks without supervision. replace Dirichlet-Guided Group Forecasting for Alleviating Over-smoothing in Time Series Forecasting

Authors: Xingyu Zhang, Jingyao Wang, Xin Yu, Zeen Song, Jianqi Zhang, Changwen Zheng, Wenwen Qiang

Abstract: Time series forecasting often suffers from over-smoothing, especially when future dynamics are multi-modal. Forecasts may follow the coarse trend of the observed future, but fail to preserve sharp changes, oscillations, turning points, and regime transitions that define plausible dynamic evolution. In this work, we revisit over-smoothing from the perspective of latent dynamical mode compression: under partial observation and single-realization supervision, multiple plausible future modes can be weakened, merged, or averaged during forecasting. Based on this view, we propose Dirichlet-Guided Group Forecasting (DGF), a mode-preserving forecasting framework that explicitly models multiple mode-conditioned predictive distributions and uncertainty over their selection probabilities. Extensive experiments on real-world forecasting benchmarks show that DGF reduces over-smoothing while improving forecasting accuracy, diversity, and dynamical consistency. replace FedRAN: Accurate and Efficient Analytic Federated Continual Learning

Authors: Jebacyril Arockiaraj, Dhruv Parikh, Jayashree Adivarahan, Rajgopal Kannan, Viktor Prasanna

Abstract: Federated continual learning (FCL) enables distributed clients to learn from evolving data streams while retaining knowledge from previously observed tasks. Existing optimization-based FCL methods often rely on repeated local training, incurring communication overhead and feature drift across clients. Analytic alternatives avoid iterative training, but high-dimensional random features used to improve accuracy require communicating a Gram matrix whose cost grows quadratically with feature dimension. Communication-efficient alternatives reduce this cost by statistically reconstructing the Gram matrix from first-order summaries. We propose FedRAN, a low-rank analytic FCL framework that instead compresses the required second-order structure directly through spectral summaries that are merged across clients and tasks. The retained rank explicitly controls the tradeoff between approximation fidelity and communication, reducing second-order communication from quadratic to linear in feature dimension for a fixed rank. FedRAN further supports label scarcity through prototype-based pseudo-labeling. Across CIFAR-100, ImageNet-R, and VTAB, FedRAN improves average accuracy by up to 4.8 percentage points over the strongest baseline, uses 30.6-121.8x less per-client communication, and is 190.3x faster than optimization-based baselines.

replace Rational Sparse Autoencoder

Authors: Naiyu Yin, Maosen Tang, Alex Townsend, Yue Yu

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are standard tools for mechanistic interpretability, but current SAE families are constrained by fixed encoder nonlinearities such as ReLU, JumpReLU, and TopK. This hard-codes a particular sparsity mechanism into the model and can distort the reconstruction-versus-sparsity trade-off. We introduce the Rational Sparse Autoencoder (RSAE), which replaces the fixed encoder activation with a trainable rational function. The design is motivated by a reconstruction advantage that we prove for RSAE at matched expected sparsity, with guaranteed non-inferiority to ReLU and, under stated assumptions, provable improvements over JumpReLU and TopK. The model is then implemented through a two-stage pipeline: an initialization procedure that copies the pre-trained baseline SAE weights and calibrates the scale parameters along with the rational coefficients; followed by a fine-tuning step under a sparsity-regularized reconstruction objective that holds $\ell_0$ at the teacher's value. Consistent with this theory, on residual-stream activations of three open-weight language models and across all three baseline activation families, the RSAE improves reconstruction fidelity after the fine-tuning step at the same level of sparsity. These gains are consistent across host language models, across baseline activation families, and across the full range of baseline sparsity we tested, while the upgrade itself adds only 20 scalar parameters per autoencoder and one training run takes between about 4 and 25 minutes on a single consumer GPU.

replace Learning through Internalization

Authors: Nikolaos Tsilivis, Nirmit Joshi, Marko Medvedev, Julia Kempe, Nathan Srebro

Abstract: We study internalization processes, by which neural-network-based systems absorb an explicit computational procedure into their own weights, and how they facilitate learning. We investigate how transformers internalize the simulation of semiautomata by internalizing chain-of-thought (CoT) tokens, which classes of semiautomata are harder to internalize, and expose the flip side of internalization, that is, a progressive degradation of out-of-distribution performance. We then provide the first provable analysis of successful internalization: for the task of learning parities, we show that a simplified one-layer transformer provably first learns the target with explicit CoT supervision and then internalizes the autoregressive generation as CoT tokens are progressively removed, learning to directly compute the parity. This task is computationally hard to learn from data without CoT supervision. Finally, we discuss how learning through internalization relates to the \textit{Positive Distribution Shift} phenomenon recently introduced by~\citet{Med+26}.

replace TROPT: An Open Framework for Unifying and Advancing Discrete Text Optimization

Authors: Matan Ben-Tov, Mahmood Sharif

Abstract: Discrete text-trigger optimization -- searching for text sequences that, when ingested by a model, steer it toward a specified objective -- underpins model red-teaming (e.g., LLM jailbreaks), as well as auditing and interpretability. However, the current state of discrete optimizers hinders their adoption and progress. First, existing optimizers, when open-sourced at all, are scattered across research codebases tied to specific models, objectives, and problem domains. Second, optimizers proliferate, each requiring engineering overhead to use or extend, and remaining hard to compare head-to-head. Together, these raise the bar for adopting optimizers in existing or new domains, and for advancing them via new strategies. We address these gaps with TROPT, the first open-source framework that unifies discrete optimizers' execution and standardizes their development under a single interface. TROPT makes it easy to customize end-to-end optimization recipes by swapping any component -- models, objectives, and optimizers -- extending its reach across domains and new applications. TROPT currently ships with 40+ optimization recipes -- covering applications such as jailbreaking and probing model internals -- built from 15+ optimizers (spanning white-box to black-box access) and 15+ losses, from foundational to state-of-the-art methods. Demonstrating its utility, we leverage TROPT in several studies: (i) controlled, large-scale experiments comparing and enhancing optimization strategies for LLM jailbreaks, revealing potent-yet-underadopted techniques; and (ii) porting optimizers across applications and domains (e.g., from LLM jailbreaks to corpus poisoning). In all, TROPT significantly lowers the barrier to adopting and advancing discrete text optimization.

replace Embedding Foundation Model Predictions in Discrete-Choice Models with Structural Guarantees

Authors: Yingshuo Wang, Xian Sun, Yanhang Li, Zhichao Fan, Zexin Zhuang

Abstract: Tabular foundation models achieve strong accuracy on choice prediction tasks, but their predictions often violate the economic logic those tasks require: raising a price can increase predicted demand, implied estimates of willingness to pay are frequently negative or implausible, and unavailable alternatives receive nonzero probability. We propose TabFM-Logit, an adapter that takes a foundation model's predicted choice probabilities as a precomputed feature and embeds them inside a multinomial logit's utility. In Stage 1, we fit the multinomial logit's structural coefficients by maximum likelihood with sign constraints. In Stage 2, we freeze those coefficients and fit a small neural correction operating on the foundation model's predictions. We prove that this composition exactly preserves the multinomial logit's marginal rate of substitution, so the analytically computable value of time becomes a mathematical guarantee rather than an empirical accident, and that training the two stages jointly breaks identifiability of the structural coefficients. Counterfactual derivatives and response signs are therefore the multinomial logit's by construction, and the foundation model contributes accuracy on observed choices. On respondents and households unseen in training, the adapter gains 5.7-6.8 percentage points (pp) of test accuracy on Swissmetro, 1.5 pp on LPMC, and 3.3-3.6 pp on IoT-Wearables over the multinomial logit (4.2 pp on average), stays within 1.0 pp of the raw foundation model, maintains 100% cost monotonicity, and keeps the multinomial logit's values of time exactly. The gain is largest where the multinomial logit is weakest and smallest where it is already strong. In every case it comes with the multinomial logit's guarantees, which the raw foundation models lack.

replace SharQ: Bridging Activation Sparsity and FP4 Quantization for LLM Inference

Authors: Haoqian Meng, Yilun Luo, Yafei Zhao, Wenyuan Liu, Huaqing Zheng, Xindian Ma, Peng Zhang

Abstract: Low-bit floating-point formats and semi-structured sparsity are increasingly supported by modern accelerators, yet combining them for LLM activation compression remains challenging: activations contain input-dependent outliers that dominate block scales in FP4 quantization, and directly applying N:M sparsity masks discards moderate values, coupling sparsification loss with quantization error. We introduce SharQ, a training-free inference method that bridges activation sparsity and FP4 quantization through an online sparse--dense decomposition. For each activation tensor, SharQ generates an input-adaptive N:M mask to extract an outlier-dominated sparse backbone, quantizes it to FP4, and defines a dense residual relative to the quantized sparse backbone rather than the unquantized sparse values. A sparse FP4 GEMM processes the backbone while a dense FP4 GEMM compensates for both mask-induced activation loss and sparse-path quantization error. The two paths share a single FP4 weight payload with path-specific scale views, and a fused preparation kernel absorbs mask generation, residual construction, and layer normalization into one operator. SharQ requires no calibration data, retraining, or model-specific tuning. Evaluated on Llama-3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen3-30B-A3B, and Qwen3-VL-8B, SharQ recovers 43--63% of the NVFP4-to-FP16 accuracy gap across language and vision-language tasks, and generalizes across NVFP4, HiF4, and MXFP4 formats. On an RTX 5090, SharQ delivers 2.2--2.4$\times$ latency reduction over FP16 and 1.2--1.4$\times$ throughput improvement over FP8 in language model serving, and up to 1.58$\times$ speedup on Wan2.2-T2V-A14B video generation when combined with SageAttention. Our code is available at https://github.com/actypedef/SharQ.

URLs: https://github.com/actypedef/SharQ.

replace FlexMoE: Learning One-for-All Multi-Budget Expert Pruning for Flexible MoE Deployment

Authors: Fan Mo, Yuxuan Han, Geng Zhang, Wangbo Zhao, Yang You

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language models scale model capacity through sparse expert activation, yet deployment still requires storing and serving all experts. Moreover, deployment budgets can vary substantially across devices, users, and workloads. Existing MoE compression methods typically optimize a separate compressed model for each target budget. We introduce FlexMoE, a post-training one-for-all framework that converts a large pretrained MoE model into a multi-budget family of smaller deployable subnetworks. FlexMoE first ranks expert FFN channels by importance to construct a sliceable weight space shared across budgets, and then learns expert-wise discrete retention actions under gradually increasing cost pressure, allowing a single action-training run to produce reliable pruning masks across budgets. Moreover, only a single mid-budget fine-tune can further recover degraded performance and yields a shared recovered model that transfers to other unseen budgets, avoiding repeated per-budget recovery and separated checkpoint storage. Across tested MoE LLMs with diverse architectures and scales, FlexMoE consistently outperforms strong MoE compression baselines. In particular, on Qwen2-57B-A14B, it retains ~99.8% of base performance while pruning 50% of routed-expert parameters even without recovery fine-tuning. For deployment, pruned subnetworks deliver real memory reduction and end-to-end throughput gains, while our kernel co-design enables runtime budget switching without checkpoint reloading and delivers substantial inference acceleration.

replace When Do Foundation Models Pay Off? A Break-Even Analysis of Pretrained Time Series Forecasters

Authors: Nicholas Tan Jerome, Frank Simon

Abstract: Deploying a time series foundation model (FM) requires GPU infrastructure and engineering overhead, with no guarantee of improvement over classical forecasting methods. We provide the first systematic break-even analysis of when this investment pays off. Across 30 benchmark datasets, we compare zero-shot and LoRA fine-tuned FMs (Chronos-Bolt, Moirai, Lag-Llama) against classical baselines (Naive, ETS, ARIMA, XGBoost) at six training set sizes from 2% to 100% of available data. FMs outperform classical methods at every evaluated training fraction on 16 of 30 datasets. On 6 datasets, classical methods surpass zero-shot FMs with at most 5% of the training data (21--6,324 samples); on the remaining 8, break-even ranges from 24 to 22,177 samples. No classical method dominates: ARIMA, Naive, ETS and XGBoost are each best on 6--11 datasets. One robust rule requires no model training: if n_train < 700 and seasonality is non-negligible, FM zero-shot is the right low-data choice -- below 20% of the history, classical methods either lose to it (4 of these 10 datasets) or cannot be fitted at all (6) -- and it stays best at full history on 6 of them; fine-tuning should be skipped. Contrary to common practice, LoRA fine-tuning rarely helps and degrades short series by up to 52%. We operationalise these findings as a two-step decision framework -- compute dataset length and seasonality strength, run a brief 5--10% pilot only if needed. Cheap dataset features do not predict the break-even category at this benchmark scale, so outside the rule a pilot remains necessary. Code and benchmark: https://github.com/nicolaisi/fm_breakeven.

URLs: https://github.com/nicolaisi/fm_breakeven.

replace Uncertainty-aware damage identification in short-span bridges via physics-informed variational autoencoder

Authors: Ana Fernandez-Navamuel, A. Javier Omella, Diego Zamora-Sanchez, David Pardo

Abstract: Vibration-based damage identification in civil infrastructure is a challenging, ill-posed inverse problem due to measurement noise, sparse sensor arrays, and modeling errors. While deep learning is powerful for system identification, deterministic approaches lack reliable uncertainty quantification and can yield physically inconsistent results. This work proposes a probabilistic Scientific Machine Learning framework for structural health monitoring: a physics-informed Gaussian copula variational autoencoder. The methodology introduces two main contributions. First, a numerical eigenvalue solver is embedded directly into the variational autoencoder as its decoder, which replaces data-driven surrogates, ensures that every posterior sample satisfies the governing equations of structural dynamics, and reduces the number of trainable parameters. Second, a Gaussian copula describes the posterior distribution of the stiffness factors, capturing cross-correlations between components with fewer parameters than Gaussian mixtures in real time. We validate the method numerically on a short-span bridge supported on elastic bearings, whose response is dominated by vertical bending, accounting for modeling errors, measurement noise, and a finer finite element discretization for testing. The method estimates the posterior distribution of the damage condition, i.e., the stiffness reduction factors of the deck zones and bearings, and these posteriors closely match the exact ones computed with sequential Monte Carlo sampling. The results reveal that the proposed methodology offers a robust and interpretable decision-making tool, able not only to indicate the presence and location of damage, but also to inform on the uncertainty associated with the diagnosis, for instance, when light damage is masked by the effect of noise or when the measured modal properties present limited sensitivity to damage.

replace Predicting Lost Updates Is Not Enough to Choose a Rounding Policy

Authors: Zekai Shang

Abstract: In low-precision training, a weight can round back to its previous value after a small update. We test whether predicting how often this happens helps choose a rounding rule. In Pythia-410M continuations from a common checkpoint, a higher-precision pilot accurately predicted the fraction of matrix weights left unchanged at each of the first 64 updates. Across three schedules with optimizer state retained or reset, nearest rounding gave lower validation loss at 256 updates than stochastic rounding or retaining 32-bit weights. The same ordering held at 512 updates with retained state. Accurately forecasting the unchanged fraction did not identify a benefit from switching policies in these runs.

replace Operational Proto-Introspection in Looped Language Models: Reading Correctness Before the Answer

Authors: Jan Kirin

Abstract: Can one tell, while a language model reasons, whether its answer will be right? Looped models are a good place to ask: they run the same layers several times per token, leaving one hidden state per loop for a probe to read. I probe Ouro-RLTT, a 2.6B-parameter looped model, for what these states reveal about how its reasoning will end. I call such information operational proto-introspection: operational because it is defined by what a probe can read, and proto because nothing here shows that the model itself uses it. In part, yes. Reading the first 48 tokens of reasoning, from answers that state no answer yet, a probe on the hidden states reaches an AUROC of 0.758 on a synthetic logic task, against 0.515 for token confidence. Question difficulty is a rival explanation: on 1,164 further questions, token confidence plus simple features of the question predict about as well. It is not only difficulty: between two answers to the same question, the probe scores the correct one higher 74% of the time. A fine-tuned language model reading the same tokens does about as well. On GSM8K and on a second checkpoint this within-question test is unresolved. A probe that ranks finished candidate answers is weak at layer 8 on the first loop, much better on the second, and as accurate as the last layers by the third, on four Ouro checkpoints. The direction it reads is largely shared across loops: a probe trained on the last loop reads the first nearly as well as a probe trained there. The signal helps decide what to do with finished outputs. It improves abstention over surface features and picks correct answers among candidates better than chance, though surface features pick about as well. Once several samples are finished, a baseline that knows their agreement leaves the answer-free probe nothing measurable to add. None of my attempts to use it to make the model generate better answers worked.

replace MOLT: A Hugging Face-Native, Minimal, and Scalable Framework for Agentic Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Jian Hu, Huiying Li, Hao Zhang, Binfeng Xu, Yifan Zhang, Shaokun Zhang, Hemil Desai, Michael Demoret, Pavlo Molchanov, Jan Kautz, Yi Dong

Abstract: Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) research iterates quickly on agents, rewards, and learning algorithms, while policies have grown to hundreds of billions of parameters and beyond. Existing open frameworks force a choice: those that are easy to modify do not scale to the largest models, and those that scale rely on Megatron, which needs dedicated bridge code for every model family and several times more framework code. We present MOLT, an open-source framework built for research that is Hugging Face-native, minimal, and scalable. Its code is easy to modify: the entire RL path is approximately 9.2K lines of Python with one training backend and one rollout engine, so a researcher or an AI coding agent can read it in full and edit the code that actually runs. A Hugging Face checkpoint is loaded directly into a PyTorch FSDP2 trainer with tensor, expert, and context parallelism, so a new model family needs no bridge, and the same code scales from a 4B model to a 753B one. Existing OpenAI- and Anthropic-SDK agents are trained by redirecting their client, with automatic handling of agents that rewrite their own context; training is fully asynchronous; and trajectory data stay in a distributed object store. With MOLT, a 30B mixture-of-experts tool agent trains stably for 1,464 asynchronous updates, a visual computer-use agent improves on OSWorld, asynchronous training is 2.40 times faster than synchronous training, and pipeline throughput reaches 92% of a Megatron-based stack. Its trainer, NeMo AutoModel, trains models of one to 2.8 trillion parameters.

replace QAdapt: Noise-Adaptive Neural Pre-Decoding for Surface-Code Quantum Error Correction

Authors: Ran Miao, Rui Luo, Xiaohan Shan, Xiaoming Sun

Abstract: Fault-tolerant quantum computing relies on quantum error correction to protect logical information, requiring classical decoders that keep pace with syndrome generation. Heterogeneous, changing hardware noise and the distribution shift from simulation to experiment challenge the accuracy and robustness of fixed neural decoders. We present QAdapt, a noise-adaptive neural pre-decoding framework for surface-code quantum error correction. QAdapt learns local spatiotemporal correlations, continually adapts across noise environments, and passes the residual syndrome to a conventional global decoder. Online elastic weight consolidation limits forgetting as training moves between measurement, idle, CNOT, and Pauli-bias noise regimes. At distance nine, the HTNet pre-decoder reduces equivalent per-round logical error by 15.64% relative to Ising-Fast, with 28.75% fewer parameters and 9.07% less matching-backend time. Online EWC reduces average forgetting by 76.52% relative to sequential fine-tuning. With weights frozen, it reduces equivalent per-round logical error by 5.66% relative to continued base-environment training on a fixed valid domain of 331 unseen compound-noise conditions; whole-sequence failure probability falls by 2.54% over all 396 conditions. On native Willow records, the corresponding per-round reductions are 19.44%, 17.47%, and 17.95% at 10, 30, and 50 rounds, without target-domain fine-tuning. These results show how adaptive neural pre-decoding can improve logical accuracy, preserve performance across noise regimes, and reduce residual matching workload within an established decoding pipeline.

replace Graph-of-Thoughts for LLM Causal Discovery with a Fairness-Pathway Case Study

Authors: Nitish Nagesh, Elahe Khatibi, Thomas Dean Hughes, Mahdi Bagheri, Pratik Gajane, Amir M. Rahmani

Abstract: Methods that use large language models (LLMs) for causal discovery reason locally, deciding one variable pair or one node at a time, so they never compare alternative whole graphs. We introduce GoT-CD, a Graph-of-Thoughts method that asks an LLM for several complete candidate graphs from variable metadata alone, ranks them with a deterministic validity score, merges them under a hard union constraint, refines the merge, and projects the result to a DAG. We evaluate on Asia, Child, and an Alzheimer's disease graph with 100 samples and GPT-4o-mini. Over five seeds on Alzheimer's and Child, GoT-CD gives the highest mean F1 score and the lowest mean structural Hamming distance of the LLM methods, with 95% intervals of the difference excluding zero in seven of eight comparisons, and it returns a DAG in every run while using three LLM requests per graph. Replaying the logged runs without individual components attributes most of the gain to generating and merging several complete candidates. A case study on the effect of Sex on MOCA Score in the Alzheimer's graph shows the limits of structural scores: the fairness verdict drawn from the discovered graph disagrees with the reference verdict in twelve of fifteen LLM runs, including all five GoT-CD runs, which mostly drop the edge out of Sex. Graphs meant for a specific downstream analysis should therefore also be checked on the pathways that analysis depends on.

replace CommitKV: Commit-Aligned KV Cache Compression for Multimodal Agents

Authors: Weizhong Huang, Jinchao Zhang, Xiawu Zheng

Abstract: Multimodal agents gather visual evidence through reasoning and tool use, extending their ability to solve tasks beyond the initial input. However, accumulating observations increases KV cache memory and inference costs. Token eviction reduces these costs by retaining useful cached tokens. Existing methods use attention, redundancy, or stage-specific queries to rank tokens, without explicitly comparing the same history across tool interactions. When observations shift attention, they may mistake temporarily reduced influence for lost usefulness and evict information needed later. We propose CommitKV, a training-free KV cache compression method that accounts for this changing evidence use. Commit-aligned measurement compares the deletion effects of the same page before a tool call completes and after its observation returns. Lifecycle-aware compression retires pages with low influence in both windows and temporarily protects pages influential before the call. Joint scoring combines attention importance, key redundancy, and token-level deletion effects to fill the remaining budget. Extensive experiments cover four open-weight multimodal models and eight benchmarks. CommitKV achieves the highest average accuracy among the compared compression methods on all four models. On Qwen3-VL-8B, it achieves 69.85% at a 512-token budget, only 0.91% below FullKV, and exceeds the strongest compression baseline by 8.44% at 256 tokens. Deploying the Qwen3-VL-8B agent with SGLang and vLLM at a 256-token budget reduces peak allocated GPU memory by 54.37%-55.23% and accelerates end-to-end inference by 2.12-2.17$\times$ over FullKV on the matched workload.

replace How do features act in an individual prediction? Separating standalone and interaction effects with a case-based taxonomy

Authors: Sejong Oh

Abstract: Local explanation methods such as SHAP and LIME summarize each feature with a single attribution score, and interaction indices quantify how strongly features interact. Neither tells a user how a feature acts in a given prediction: whether its effect is the same alone and in context, or whether the other features amplify it, make it unnecessary, switch it off or reverse it. We extend case-based feature importance (CBFI), originally a global method, to individual predictions. Evaluating the model in four hybrid states per background sample decomposes the in-context importance of a feature into a standalone and an interaction effect, and a case taxonomy labels each feature of an instance as inactive, standalone, amplified, redundant, gated or reversed. We compare the method with k-SII, TreeSHAP interaction values and the H-statistic on synthetic functions, and study eight real tabular datasets with six model families. On the true functions, the decomposition recovered interactions exactly (correlation 0.99 with the analytic local interaction). On fitted models, pair recovery was slightly below that of dedicated indices (local precision@k 0.86-0.91 versus 0.91-0.95). Interaction values alone could not distinguish substitution from gating, whereas the taxonomy identified both without knowing the partner (accuracy 0.74-0.76); applied to Shapley interaction values, it performed comparably. On real data, 45-57% of feature pairs with nearly identical SHAP values received different labels, and in a medical-insurance model the method showed that the interaction component of smoking changes sign at a body-mass index of 30. Localized CBFI does not detect interactions more accurately than existing indices; what it adds is a decomposition into standalone and interaction effects and a label stating how each feature acts.

replace XMerge: Cross-Axis Selection and Reconstructive Layer Merging for LLM Depth Compression

Authors: Jundong Hu, Shekar Ramachandran

Abstract: Removing complete transformer layers preserves a standard serving architecture, but existing depth-compression methods can lose substantial quality, and the loss varies unpredictably across models. We introduce XMerge, a post-training method with two components. Cross-axis selection identifies a block with low relative-magnitude and angular hidden-state change, and local boundary reconstruction re-fits the adjacent surviving block to match the original two-block output. XMerge uses no task labels or end-to-end fine-tuning, and it introduces neither architectural changes nor additional inference-time parameters. Across seven Llama and Qwen backbones (0.5B-8B), five published baselines, and three layer-reduction levels, its advantage over baselines is largest at the most aggressive removal: at k=4 it ranks first on six of seven backbones on CORE (a 22-task aggregate) and, separately, on six of seven on MMLU (five of seven on both at once), while avoiding the large perplexity increases of several competing operators. In a task-level bootstrap, the 95% confidence intervals for the three largest CORE margins exclude zero; the remaining margins are consistent with ties. Across the 14 (model, regime) cells it is also the only evaluated operator that never collapses, ranking top-2 in both zero-shot and in-context regimes; on a first calibration probe (one backbone) it is the best-calibrated operator. Ablations show that local reconstruction provides most of the gain, while cross-axis fusion helps when the two selection axes disagree. The additional construction cost is recovered through per-token decode savings after roughly tens of thousands of requests.

replace Two Principles for Autoregressive Language Modeling

Authors: Runlin Shi, Bojian Yin, Guoqi Li

Abstract: We investigate the functional differentiation learned by RoPE Transformers to derive principles for autoregressive language modeling. We introduce two intervention-based metrics: RoPE Frequency Importance Score (RFIS) measures how removing a frequency contribution changes a head's full attention distribution, and RoPE Positional Dependence (RPD) isolates the effect of rotary modulation. In Qwen3 and Llama3.1, these metrics distinguish retrieval from positional modeling, while individual heads can exhibit both functions. A mid-low-frequency Global Positional Band (GPBand) separates their frequency dependencies and tracks the training sequence length in controlled experiments, suggesting that global positional fitting can hinder zero-shot length extrapolation. These findings motivate two principles. (1) Functional Organization: retrieval and positional functions should be assigned separately and mixed within layers with layer-specific allocation; and (2) Positional Dependence: positional modeling should operate only locally, while global access should rely on position-independent retrieval. Matched pretraining from scratch and targeted architectural ablations at 380M and 1.4B parameters support both principles and highlight the importance of mechanism choice in realizing each function. Together, these findings suggest a promising route toward architectural innovation grounded in the functional requirements of autoregressive language modeling.

replace TabBench-Bio: A Living Benchmark for Machine Learning on High-Dimensional Biomedical Tables

Authors: Jules Kreuer, Sofiane Ouaari, Julia Hellmig, Julius Braitinger, Nico Pfeifer

Abstract: Biomedical tables often combine thousands of measured variables with only tens or hundreds of labelled samples, a regime that is poorly represented in general-purpose tabular benchmarks. We introduce TabBench-Bio, a living and interactive benchmark of 43 biomedical datasets spanning multiple domains. Under a shared cross-validation protocol, we compare classical estimators, neural networks, and tabular foundation models across 28 feature-by-sample operating points.
