replace Perplexity Ranks Activation Quantisers but Understates What They Remove

Authors: Anish Sathyanarayanan, Min Xu

Abstract: Four-bit activation quantisation of language models is usually tuned and compared by perplexity. We ask what that misses, and find three things. First, perplexity gets the order of quantisers right but the size of their damage wrong. Across four models it ranks 33 of 34 pairs of settings correctly by closed-book QA loss, yet a 6-16% rise in perplexity comes with a 10-23% drop in QA accuracy, and the lost facts are mostly rare ones. Second, the ranking only holds while the quantisation error acts like random noise. Without rotation, rounding small values to zero deletes part of the signal, and this hurts in-context retrieval far more than noise of the same size would. The crest factor of the quantiser's input (its peak-to-average ratio) helps tell the two cases apart. Third, rotating in blocks, the usual workaround when a layer's width has no ready-made Hadamard matrix, can land a model in between and leave outliers behind; 10 of 42 popular open models have such a width. A full-width Hadamard exists for every such width we found, costs about the same, and on Qwen3-4B halves the closed-book TriviaQA loss (0.112 to 0.060, over three rotation seeds). We recommend reporting a knowledge-heavy task alongside perplexity, and rotating at full width wherever blocks leave an input outlier-heavy.

replace When Does Reallocating a Sampling Budget Pay? Instance-wise Allocation for Neural Routing Solvers

Authors: Jinhyung Bae

Abstract: Neural routing solvers sample many solutions per instance and keep the best, with the same number of samples for every instance. We measure how much a non-uniform split of the same total budget is worth and which property of a workload decides it. Best-of-k curves estimated by averaging random orderings of stored samples overstate the gain, and deciding on one half of the samples while evaluating on the other understates it; we use exact best-of-k curves, a U-statistic over all orderings. For three pretrained solvers (POMO, the Attention Model and Sym-NCO) on the traveling salesman problem, reallocation reduces the total optimality gap by at most 2.5% on the training distribution, so uniform allocation is close to optimal there. On workloads that mix in out-of-distribution instances it reduces the gap by 11.8-13.3% (1.1-1.8% of tour length) for the two solvers that degrade under the shift, and almost nothing for the one that does not. Across two heterogeneity mechanisms, three solvers, two confirmation collections and budgets of 25 to 200 samples per instance, the gain is predicted by the spread of difficulty within a workload, not its average. Allocating on a power-law curve fitted to twenty charged probe samples per instance recovers 88-93% of the uncharged optimum; successive halving does worse. A 2-opt local search, however, removes most of the gap at negligible cost, after which reallocation adds a few hundredths of a percent of tour length.

replace Elastic Selective Spectral Hybrids for Train-Once, Export-Many Budgeted Inference

Authors: Dachuan Song, Chuchu Chen, Xuan Wang

Abstract: Deploying a language model under different computing and latency budgets calls for compact models with different quality-cost trade-offs. Towards this end, elastic spectral state space models provide ordered and temporally decomposed channels that can be truncated, but they are associated with linear time-invariant filters that cannot selectively preserve relevant past information or forget irrelevant information as the context evolves. To address this, we introduce the Elastic Selective Spectral Hybrid (ESSH), which realizes each Hankel spectral channel as an independent recurrent unit using fitted damped rotation modes. It also features an input-dependent decay and write/read gates that make temporal retention and state update input-dependent while preserving channel-wise truncation and a structured recurrence for efficient execution. ESSH combines these selective spectral mixers with sliding-window attention and jointly trains multiple capacities by reducing spectral-channel count and feed-forward width at different rates through a two-rate capacity map with full-model distillation. The resulting models support chunked parallel training and fused recurrent decoding while avoiding computation for discarded channels. At full capacity, ESSH achieves language-modeling quality comparable to similarly sized independently trained models, while smaller exports exhibit a smooth quality-cost trade-off. We validate the effectiveness of the proposed framework using language understanding, retrieval, cross-domain text, and DNA experiments, by assessing quality retention and the trade-off against independently trained and elastic baselines. At the 1.53B model configuration, fused batch-one decoding takes 1.37 ms per token on a B300, providing a 2.14-2.80x speedup over the tested Mamba-2 and Mamba-3 implementations and 3.03x over Transformer++ at matched parameter counts.

replace SANTA++: Sampling Attention through Representative Keys

Authors: Kyle Lee, Christian Z. Pratt, Ruoyu Fang, Heekyung Lee, Avinash Lohitsa, Ryan Modafe, Kerem Y. Camsari

Abstract: Attention often concentrates on a small subset of tokens in the context, but which subset matters changes from one query to the next. To exploit this changing structure, we introduce SANTA++, a training-free stochastic attention method that uses representative keys for memory-efficient selection without scanning the entire key-value (KV) cache. Cached keys are organized into teams, and the query scores one representative from each team to decide which teams to sample. We compute exact attention scores within the sampled teams and reweight each team's contribution by the inverse of its inclusion probability. This importance sampling correction estimates attention over the full cache, with a sampling budget that lets us trade memory reads for accuracy. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct at 32K context, contiguous SANTA++ at S=1024 retains 95-98% of dense aggregate scores on LongBench v2, HELMET RAG, and RULER using 28-31% logical KV access. On RULER, SANTA++ outperforms Quest across the tested accuracy-access sweep, using 48.7% fewer KV reads at approximately 79% accuracy. It also outperforms MagicPIG at low access. At S=1024, our Triton implementation gives a 1.38x attention-operator speedup over the Flash backend of PyTorch scaled dot-product attention (Flash SDPA) in FP16. At batch eight, full-model decoding is 1.23x faster than dense Flash SDPA with matched INT4 model weights and FP16 KV-cache precision on an RTX 5090 Laptop GPU. Our kernels are available at https://github.com/OPUSLab/santapp-kernel-demo.

URLs: https://github.com/OPUSLab/santapp-kernel-demo.

replace IQS-BO: In-Context Query Selection for Bayesian Optimisation

Authors: Luca Geminiani, Nadja Klein

Abstract: Bayesian Optimisation (BO) is a powerful framework for optimising expensive black-box functions, but typically requires refitting a surrogate and maximising an acquisition function at every evaluation. In-context approaches based on Prior-data Fitted Networks (PFNs) amortise part of this cost by pre-training transformers on functions drawn from synthetic priors. PFNS4BO amortises the surrogate but still relies on a numerically maximised acquisition function, while FIBO performs BO fully in-context by sampling optimiser locations from a learned density, which fixes the decision rule and admits no surrogate. Learned acquisition functions score a finite candidate set with a network, but, lacking a label for the query, learn the score by reinforcement learning. We propose IQS-BO, a PFN that learns the query decision by supervised learning on synthetic priors. In a single forward pass, IQS-BO predicts the probability that each candidate is the best in the set, and we show that training minimises its expected KL divergence to the posterior probability of this event. The model can be pre-trained without a surrogate for fully in-context BO, or take the predictions of a fixed probabilistic surrogate as additional input, amortising only the decision step. Our method proposes queries at a fraction of the cost of acquisition-based methods, while matching or outperforming BO with Gaussian processes (GPs) and available in-context methods in sequential and batch optimisation, in low and high dimensions. Finally, we propose a mixture prior for pre-training PFNs which combines samples from GPs with functions exhibiting warped inputs, narrow optima, or plateaus that are poorly modelled by stationary GP kernels. We show that pre-training on this prior can improve performance and help the network generalise to a broader range of optimisation problems.

replace Think Before You Distill: Generalization Recovery in Quantized VLAs via Selective Policy Distillation

Authors: Shen Ruan, Wenchang Gao, Jin Wang, Siao Liu, Zhaxizhuoma, Dongchun Ren, Xin Zheng

Abstract: Post-training quantization has been shown to preserve VLA performance under standard evaluation conditions, but whether it preserves the full-precision model's robustness and generalization remains underexplored. In this study, we systematically study the robustness and generalization of post-quantized VLA policies under environmental disturbances. Empirical results show that quantized policies can become fragile to subtle environmental variations despite retaining comparable in-distribution performance. We further observe that action discrepancies are concentrated in a small subset of the rollout states, while teacher guidance has opposite effects depending on discrepancy: it improves performance at high-discrepancy states but can degrade it at low-discrepancy states. These findings reveal that effective post-quantization recovery requires selective intervention on vulnerable states rather than globally distilling the student. We therefore propose Policy-Induced Vulnerability-Oriented Tuning (PIVOT-Q), a vulnerability-aware On-Policy Distillation (OPD) framework that selectively corrects vulnerable states encountered during quantized-student rollouts using the frozen full-precision policy as a teacher. PIVOT-Q identifies vulnerable states using discounted accumulated discrepancies over a short horizon, applies phase-balanced sparse supervision, and uses a Behavioral Anchor to prevent unintended changes. Experiments under seven LIBERO-Plus environmental variations demonstrate consistent recovery across multiple VLA backbones and quantization methods. Notably, PIVOT-Q outperforms full-state distillation with smaller variance across evaluated settings while using only 20\% of its state-level distillation budget. Our code is available at https://github.com/ruanruan-andy/PIVOT-Q.

URLs: https://github.com/ruanruan-andy/PIVOT-Q.

replace Considering Context: When World Models Need Context Encoders

Authors: Oleg Smirnov, Sofiane Ennadir, John Pertoft, Bjartur Hjaltason, Sara Karimi

Abstract: Methods for generalization in model-based reinforcement learning typically assume that an agent cannot recover the latent context governing the environment dynamics from its own experience, and therefore supplies it externally. We formalize and test this assumption with \emph{predictive sufficiency}, which quantifies what access to the context adds to next-step prediction under the visitation distribution an agent induces, and separates that quantity into a history-recoverable part and a residual requiring the true context, both distinct from a finite model's deficit. We classify context-aware algorithms by the predictive risk their conditioning set can target and demonstrate across environments of increasing identification difficulty that the headroom does not follow the MDP class. The same task under different priors leaves predictive headroom in one setting and nothing distinguishable from zero in another, where the agent's behavior implicitly identifies the context and any benefit of such a mechanism cannot be attributed to missing one-step predictive information. Where headroom persists, the learned state exposes it only partially, and adding the true context still lowers the risk. Our contribution is an analytical framework, instantiated by a practical criterion for matching contextual mechanisms to the predictive information available to them, estimated from the ordinary trained agent.

replace LoongSage: A Production-Grade, High-Efficiency Agentic RL Framework for Frontier LLMs with Validated Recipes

Authors: LoongSage Team, Bowei Song, Caiwei Zhao, Chuwen Liu, Dechuang Gu, Dong Fang, Feiyu Xie, Huaiyu Li, Hui Zhang, Jian Tian, Jian Wang, Jie Zhao, Liguang Li, Maoyun Wang, Meiyan Yang, Minghan Zhu, Pai Liu, Pandeng Yao, Renbao Tian, Ru Yang, Shuai Jia, Shuai Tan, Shuailong Li, Shuliang Li, Teng Jin, Tianze Yang, Wenbo Cui, Wenjing Bai, Wenkang Wu, Xiaowei Jiang, Xiaoyi Dong, Yanpeng Wang, Yanwen Wang, Yaohui Ma, Yawen Wang, Yihang Zhang, Yingjie Yan, Zhanbiao Lian, Zhenyu Lu, Zhida Hu, Zhihui Hao, Zhihui Hu, Zhiwen Xiao, Zhiyi Gao, Zhongwei Qi, Zhuangzhuang Wu, Ziqian Liu, Ziyue Zheng

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a critical phase in the post-training pipeline of large language models (LLMs). As the frontier of LLM capabilities shifts from static reasoning to agentic tasks--characterized by external tool invocation, multi-turn interactions, and long-horizon decision-making--existing mainstream RL frameworks have grown increasingly unwieldy, raising the overhead of adoption, usage, and debugging. To address these challenges, we present LoongSage, an agent-native, fully asynchronous RL framework with a lightweight core of roughly 14K lines of code. Built upon Megatron for distributed training and SGLang for high-throughput inference, LoongSage centralizes orchestration within a single controller process and encapsulates all GPU-resident components as Ray actors. It introduces AgentFlow, a unified execution paradigm that orchestrates both white-box and black-box agents. For asynchronous execution, it integrates sliding-window strategies that bound trajectory staleness, and employs TransferMesh, a topology-aware synchronization mechanism that routes weights after each step over the fastest transport available to each receiver. Beyond agentic RL, LoongSage incorporates a memory-efficient Teacher Manager that decouples the scaling of teacher models from GPU memory constraints, natively supporting Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation. Extensive evaluations across four complete training runs, spanning complex reinforcement learning and multi-teacher distillation, demonstrate that task rewards improve steadily while the train-inference gap is kept under continuous control, and the fully asynchronous pipeline removes synchronous idle bubbles, sustaining hardware utilization.

replace One Checkpoint, Many Runs: Recipe Cards for Fine-Tuned Models

Authors: Jaewon Choi, Jihye Kang, Namhyuk Ahn

Abstract: Fine-tuned language models are shared with model cards that describe the training setup in detail, often down to the default random seed, but report the score of a single released checkpoint. Such setups are retrained routinely, by fine-tuning services for each customer, by organizations at each update, and by users on their own data, so the model that is deployed comes from a new run of the setup. Some runs fail, and a failed run can go unnoticed and reach the people the model serves. We call such a setup a model recipe and argue that its documentation should state how often a run of the recipe misses a required performance bar. In a survey of the model cards of 300 fine-tuned language models on Hugging Face, none reports this failure probability, and only three report results from more than one training run. Across 2,124 fine-tuning runs, we find that reading the mean, the median, or a bootstrap interval as an approval often approves recipes whose failure rate exceeds a stated failure tolerance, because these summaries do not measure how often a run fails. Model cards do not report how often the runs of a recipe fail, although whoever runs the training is best placed to count them, while those who rely on a retrained model bear the cost of a failed run. To report this failure probability directly, we propose recipe cards, an addendum to model cards that documents the model recipe behind a checkpoint. A recipe card states the bar and the tolerance and who chose them, reports how many runs are made and how many of them miss the bar, gives an upper bound on the failure probability, and warns when this bound exceeds the tolerance. Recipe cards record who set and who accepted this risk, and let deployers and regulators state acceptance criteria for retrained models.

replace Interaction-as-a-Judge

Authors: Wayne Chi, I-Hung Hsu, Li Chen, Wei-Lin Chiang, Ion Stoica, Anastasios Nikolas Angelopoulos, Ameet Talwalkar, Chris Donahue, Cho-Jui Hsieh

Abstract: Coding agents are tasked with producing sophisticated interactive applications such as websites, desktop applications, and games. While humans assess these applications interactively, current automated LLM evaluations primarily focus on code analysis and unit tests. This mismatch can cause LLM judges to miss failures that are immediately apparent to human users. We study Interaction-as-a-Judge, an evaluation paradigm where computer-use agents (CUAs) directly interact with an application. We demonstrate that Interaction-as-a-Judge can improve both evaluation and generation of interactive applications. To this end, we collect and release two complementary datasets: $6,858$ pairs of applications with human preferences and corresponding agent traces, and $222$ applications with human-verified requirement labels. Relative to standard non-interactive approaches, we find that Interaction-as-a-Judge improves evaluation in terms of predicting human preferences ($65.6\% \to 69.1\%$) and requirement verification ($77.0\% \to 81.6\%$). We also observe scaling benefits: across both preference alignment and requirement verification, higher-quality interactions improve performance. Finally, through both inference- and training-time experiments, we demonstrate that Interaction-as-a-Judge can also improve the quality of generated artifacts. At inference time, Interaction-as-a-Judge repairs $75.5\%$ of broken requirements within five attempts, compared to $55.4\%$ for code-only self-feedback. At training time, using Interaction-as-a-Judge as a reward signal improves win-rate over the base model by $6.7$pp while reducing both generated code length by $35.7\%$ and build failure rates by $7.2$pp compared to a code-only reward signal.

replace Reinforcement Learning for Large-Scale Lifelong Multi-Agent Path Finding with Shared Spatial Fields

Authors: Jean Seong Bjorn Choe

Abstract: Large-scale applications of lifelong multi-agent path finding (LMAPF) call for computationally inexpensive movement decisions under changing traffic conditions and across varied layouts. We propose a navigation policy in which a convolutional network computes a shared spatial field over the map once per timestep and a compact goal-conditioned operator derives agents' movement preferences. The complete policy is trained end-to-end using average-reward reinforcement learning with throughput as the reward, without planner demonstrations or a separate online path planner. For structured and unstructured layouts, we train one policy per class on pools of small maps and deploy each without retraining on larger, unseen layouts and agent populations. Across six layouts with 10,000 agents, our policies achieve throughput comparable to or higher than the strongest evaluated baselines. The proposed method has an average execution time below 10 ms per step, more than an order of magnitude faster than several established planners.

replace Typed Decision Models under Prevalence Shift and Label Budgets: Decision Training, Calibration, and Implicit Priors

Authors: Abdul Rahman Iqbal, Muhammad Fawad Nasim, Amir Saleem

Abstract: Typed decision models (TDMs) return probabilities over caller-defined options, and Jev's vendor describes it as calibrated. We ask whether vendor decision training improves a model over its backbone, whether a TDM's calibration and thresholds withstand prevalence (label) shift like a fine-tuned classifier's, and how many labels a trained model needs to overtake a TDM. We compare three TDMs (Jev, Tev1-4B, Nimble-9B), their base models and trained classifiers on identical candidate-set decisions from five public datasets (on intent datasets, within the gold label's intent group) under simulated shift, with 35 paired tests frozen before the confirmatory analysis (earlier test-data checks disclosed), and estimate label-budget curves. First, decision training is not always beneficial: Nimble-9B, which differs from Qwen3.5-9B only by a LoRA adapter, is better calibrated on intent tasks but 12 Macro-F1 points worse on financial sentiment, a loss that persists across prompt formats; Tev1-4B's largest gain over its backbone is a prompt-format effect. Second, on 50 fixed moderate shifts, Jev's top-label calibration error and threshold risk grow significantly more than a fine-tuned DeBERTa's only on financial sentiment, sensitive to the prior set; averaged over 2,000 shifts the gap is at most one point. Third, label-free EM correction with the model's implicit prior lowers Jev's shifted calibration error where it is highest, but labelled recalibration lowers it far more. Fourth, DeBERTa needs 100-250 labels to overtake Jev on hate speech and does not clearly overtake it on CLINC150 at any budget; a QLoRA adapter on Qwen3.5-4B overtakes Jev with at most 2,500 labels on every dataset, on CLINC150 only marginally and not under in-budget selection. The TDMs tested are neither uniformly better than their backbones nor uniquely fragile under shift; their lead over trained models depends on the task.

replace The Effectiveness and Durability of Pretraining Data Filtering in Protein Language Models

Authors: Aaron Maiwald, Roderick McNeill, Jaeyoung Lee, Matthew A. Clarke, Lennart Justen, Alejandro Acelas, Ivo Andrews, Giles Harper-Donnelly, Adam Moore, Sophie Rose, Oliver M. Crook, Felix Jackson

Abstract: Pretraining filtering is one of the most common safeguards for open-weight language models. Here we study its effectiveness, durability and scalability using the ESM-2 family of protein language models. We find that filtering viral proteins from the pretraining data meaningfully degrades masked language modelling, mutation effect prediction and long-range contact prediction on viral proteins, while largely preserving performance on non-viral proteins. These effects are concentrated among proteins from human-infecting viruses, and grow as we scale models from 8M to 3B parameters. As models increase in size, we find that stringent filtering becomes more effective. However, fine-tuning on sequences from the removed viral families restores the lost performance. Although the cost to recover performance increases with model scale, even for the largest models it costs about 80 USD of H100 time. This work establishes pretraining filtering as moderately effective at selectively degrading performance on viral proteins, but not durable against fine-tuning with modest computational resources and access to relevant data.

replace Geometric state space, memory representation and architectures

Authors: Shang Xu

Abstract: We introduce a geometric framework for deriving attention architectures. A sequence forms a memory $m\in M_n$, and an attention pairing $\rho_n\colon M_n\to\operatorname{Hom}(A,A)$ determines how that memory acts on each token state. Different representations of this action recover familiar architectures: softmax attention reads individual key--value pairs, linear attention accumulates their contributions in matrix statistics, and Intention fits a regression map from keys to values. The framework also suggests new architectures by changing the geometry of the query--key pairing, the function carried by each value, or the space representing the memory. ImageNet-1K experiments illustrate these constructions through geometric CAFormer variants, a ViT with query-dependent values, and a linear ViT with a wider memory representation.

replace Shared Context, Separate Engines: Stateful Streaming Inference for Multimodal LLMs on Edge SoCs

Authors: Yilong Li, Chengpo Yan, Yijing Zeng, Hanlei Wu, Mingjun Song, Suman Banerjee

Abstract: Streaming multimodal large language models (MLLMs) must take in new video frames while answering questions about what they have already seen. On an edge SoC, if one engine does both, ingestion and answering compete for its time while the other engines sit idle. To take over a query, another engine must first import or rebuild the accumulated history, because each runtime keeps it as KV state in its own format. \name lets one engine answer from history that another engine built. Each query reads the history as it stood when the query was asked, so one engine can keep ingesting while another answers. The CPU and GPU can also decode answers to different queries simultaneously. Phase-level state continuation lets an engine prefill a query, pass its KV state to another engine for decoding, and return to ingestion. State-Driven Execution Planning (SDEP) chooses where each query is prefilled and decoded, based on the state each engine holds and the work queued on it. Across M4 Max, RK3588, and QCS6490, \name outperforms single-engine execution. On a live M4 Max stream, SDEP cuts p95 time to first token by $76\%$, and every query receives its first token within one second. Under backlog, it raises request throughput by up to $35\%$. For two concurrent video streams on RK3588, SDEP delivers $19.6\%$ higher request throughput than single-engine execution and $27.2\%$ more than running both engines in parallel without shared state. \name delivers up to $2.48\times$ the throughput of the same engines run serially. Every answer matches serial execution token for token.

replace FOCUS: Active Critic Learning for Long-Horizon Language Model Agents

Authors: Zhengping Jiang, William Jurayj, Liaoyaqi Wang, Chunsheng Zuo, Anqi Liu, Benjamin Van Durme

Abstract: Sparse outcome rewards make it difficult for long-horizon language-model agents to assign credit to earlier actions. Learned critics provide value estimates for intermediate states, but these estimates can be inaccurate, while estimating intermediate values with repetitive continuation samples from points within a trajectory incurs substantial costs. To mitigate these issues, we introduce FOCUS, an RL method that uses selective branching to help the critic network learn its value estimator. The rollout controller facilitates active learning for the critic by sampling continuations from states that are expected to most reduce the critic's estimation error. These refined value estimates then score the segment between consecutive states with a TD($\lambda$) advantage, while the sampled evidence supplies training targets for the critic. FOCUS supports long-horizon agentic reinforcement learning through a modular context-management interface that decouples context management from rollout allocation. Our experiments show that using compaction-induced segment boundaries for value estimation and credit assignment, FOCUS outperforms GRPO and VinePPO with matched per-step generation budgets on legal reasoning, deep search, and code localization benchmarks, improving held-out accuracy at Qwen3.5-9B scale by 7.2 points over the strongest baseline on BrowseComp-Plus (55.4 vs. 48.2) and by 4.1 points on DeonticBench (56.5 vs. 52.4).

replace TGTV: Interpretable Multivariate Time-Series Anomaly Detection in UAV Telemetry

Authors: Zixuan Wang, Changbo Shi, Ji Liu, Shiping Chen

Abstract: Anomaly detection in multivariate fixed-wing unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) telemetry is complicated by operating-condition transitions, asynchronous sensor sampling, and control-system compensation that can obscure fault signatures. Existing temporal detection methods have limited capacity to represent coupled faults across subsystems, track their evolution, and constrain language-model diagnoses with quantitative evidence. This paper proposes TGTV, an interpretable framework for multivariate telemetry anomaly detection and traceable fault analysis that integrates spatiotemporal graph learning with a large language model. A temporal convolutional network and a graph attention network with weighted fused adjacency extract multiscale temporal patterns and spatial dependencies among sensors. A hybrid temporal Transformer and variational autoencoder then evaluates window-level anomalies using prediction errors and reconstruction probabilities. Continuous-time Shapley attribution identifies critical sensors and high-contribution intervals while preserving the temporal continuity of fault evolution. A structured evidence package combines detection results, graph relationships, temporal attribution, sensor attribution, and operating context. An evidence-constrained diagnosis protocol uses this package to generate flight-level conclusions with traceable evidence identifiers. Cross-flight holdout experiments on the public ALFA fixed-wing flight dataset yield a window-level area under the precision-recall curve of 0.976, accuracy of 0.973, recall of 0.902, and F1 score of 0.920. Flight-level fault diagnosis achieves recall of 0.943 and an F1 score of 0.944. These results demonstrate accurate anomaly detection and quantitatively supported fault-tracing cues, providing a basis for interpretable post-flight fault review and analysis.

replace Your LR Schedule May Not Be Principled: Effective Learning Rate Scheduling in Weight-Normalized Training

Authors: Qifan Yu, Xinyu Ma, Xingyan Bin, Di He

Abstract: Weight-normalized (WN) training is emerging as a compelling alternative to conventional weight decay (WD), with recent studies reporting substantial pretraining loss gains. We find that the effective learning rate (ELR) trajectory accounts for most of the loss dynamics difference between WN and WD. Matching blockwise ELR trajectories step by step closely aligns their loss curves. Schedule design should therefore target ELR rather than the nominal learning rate (LR). Building on this principle, we model directional-alignment dynamics under WN and derive $1/\sqrt{t}$ decay as the optimal schedule. For finite-horizon pretraining, we propose AIR (Affine Inverse-square-Root decay) to match prescribed peak and terminal LRs. Experiments on 128B- and 500B-token Mixture-of-Experts pretraining under WN show that AIR outperforms cosine and alternative schedules in loss, downstream performance, and hyperparameter robustness, reducing terminal training loss by 0.01 relative to cosine. We further identify late-stage scheduling as the primary source of this gain through controlled stage interventions, and demonstrate that a shared late schedule progressively attenuates alignment differences left by earlier LRs. Finally, transferring AIR's ELR trajectory to WD reproduces the same improved loss dynamics, demonstrating that these gains come from ELR scheduling itself and extend beyond fixed-norm training.

replace Profile-aware assessment of block-diagonal Hessian approximations in neural networks

Authors: Maxim Bolshim (ITMO University, Saint Petersburg, Russia), Alexander Kugaevskikh (ITMO University, Saint Petersburg, Russia)

Abstract: Evaluating block-diagonal curvature models in neural networks involves two questions: how much curvature they omit, and what a specific correction changes at a finite step. We measure the squared off-block fraction of the parameter Hessian against references for group sizes and the observed within-group curvature profile. In CIFAR-100 ResNet-18 and a skip-free counterpart, similar initial discarded fractions accompany different profiles. At saved trained ResNet-18 weights, changing from stage to layer groups moves estimated off-block mass from below to above the profile reference. An approximate assessment of probe uncertainty conditional on fixed weights and inputs resolves this crossing; some skip-free comparisons remain unresolved. Separately, in nine saved ResNet-50/Stanford Cars states from three training seeds, we compare candidates formed from off-group Hessian action, the remaining model residual and their combination. For each state, we construct two full-Hessian-informed maps on separate geometry draws. Within each draw, all three inputs share one map. replace Training Autoregressive Language Models on Rounded, Bounded, and Censored Numerical Labels via Exact Likelihood

Authors: Jingquan Yan, Yuwei Miao, Yang Xie, Junzhou Huang

Abstract: Numerical labels often specify a set of possible values: a measurement is rounded, a laboratory test reports only a bound, or a program is stopped at a timeout. We introduce boundary marginalization, the first method that trains an autoregressive language model on the exact likelihood of such observations through its existing digit tokens. A fixed-width, order-preserving numeral representation groups consecutive grid values under shared digit prefixes. An interval is a union of such groups, so its exact probability and gradient follow from at most two boundary paths, the successive digit prefixes of its endpoints, each of length logarithmic in the number of grid values. The resulting objective unifies precise, rounded, bounded, and censored supervision and trains digit decisions at the resolution supplied by each observation. The same computation supports exact distributional queries and unbiased gradients for the continuous ranked probability score on exact labels. Experiments show recovery of a conditional distribution from rounded and censored reports alone, improved likelihood on naturally censored molecular measurements, and more accurate code latency distributions on two backbones. Exact interval updates remain tractable as the numerical support grows to one million values.

replace Systolic Linear Attention: Co-Designing Tile-Grouped Gated Delta Attention for TPUs

Authors: Gagik Amirkhanyan, Nuojin Cheng

Abstract: Gated-delta linear attention layers such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN), Kimi Delta Attention (KDA), and GDN-2 are tuned for GPUs, where small tensor-core tiles and fast vector registers make unpacked heads, per-channel decay rescaling, and per-value-head chunk solves cheap. On Google TPUs, which concentrate compute in wide $256 \times 256$ systolic matrix units backed by a slower vector unit, direct ports leave half of each matrix tile padded (for $d_k = 128$ heads) and repeat decay rescaling and chunk solves across every value head. We present \textbf{Systolic Linear Attention (SLA)}, which reshapes gated delta attention around the matrix unit by widening keys to $256$ and sharing both keys and scalar write gates $\beta_t$ across grouped value heads ($R\times$ fewer chunk solves), grouping decay gates into four $64$-channel tiles, and fusing exact FP32 block-doubling chunk inversion and its backward reuse inside one on-chip kernel. With exact chunk inversion on TPU v7x, $2$-stage \texttt{SLA-R4} (four value heads per key head) runs $2.1\times$--$2.6\times$ faster than open-source MaxText KDA and $1.4\times$--$2.4\times$ faster than open-source Tokamax KDA, while using $49\%$--$61\%$ less temporary high-bandwidth memory (HBM). Across a $2{,}048$-chip TPU v7x pre-training study of sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models from $6.5\text{B}$ to $63.1\text{B}$ nominal parameters, SLA trains $11\%$--$24\%$ faster than MaxText KDA ($4\%$--$21\%$ faster than Tokamax KDA), matches KDA perplexity on three paired seeds at $12.5\text{B}$, and stays within $3\%$ at other scales.

replace Calibrated Uncertainty Quantification on TDC ADMET: What Can Be Resolved Across 22 Datasets?

Authors: Laurits Fredsgaard, Michael Riis Andersen, Mikkel N. Schmidt

Abstract: Absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion, and toxicity (ADMET) models often rely on small, heterogeneous datasets, yet their predictions may determine which compounds proceed to experimental testing. replace MAGE: Towards Generalizable Multi-timescale EEG Representations

Authors: Tuan-Kiet Doan, Van-Chien Nguyen, Trung-Hieu Tran, Quang-Hung Pham, Ngoc-Son Vu, Huy Phan, Phi Le Nguyen, Duc-Han Le, Nikola Simidjievski, Van-Tam Nguyen

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) data plays an important role in understanding human brain activity, with various major applications. replace Prescriptive Learning of Linear Policies with Feasibility Guarantees for Binary Decision-Making

Authors: Farzaneh Pourahmadi, Trine Krogh Boomsma, Pierre Pinson

Abstract: Binary decision-making under uncertainty arises across a wide range of applications, including energy systems scheduling, transportation, and workforce planning. While machine learning has been increasingly applied to accelerate or replace mixed-integer solvers in such settings, existing approaches rely on predictive learning, training models to reproduce optimal decisions from labeled data, which lacks knowledge of counterfactual outcomes and provides no guarantees that the resulting decisions will be feasible. This limitation is further compounded under distributional shift, where policies trained on historical data may produce systematically infeasible decisions. We propose a prescriptive learning framework for binary decision-making that addresses both limitations. Rather than predicting optimal labels, the framework jointly optimizes over linear decision policies and feasible assignments across all training scenarios, allowing feasible alternatives and their operational performance to be considered directly during training. We further extend the framework to a distributionally robust chance-constrained formulation using a Wasserstein ambiguity set, which enforces feasibility with a prescribed probability under the worst-case distribution in the ambiguity set and enables a tunable trade-off between robustness and solution quality. We demonstrate the framework on two case studies, namely a unit commitment problem and a staff allocation problem. Out-of-sample experiments under test distributions that either match or differ from the training distribution show that the robust model is more conservative when the two distributions coincide, while under distributional shift it substantially reduces unmet demand and lowers total cost by up to 76.5% relative to classical predictive learning.

replace Probabilistic State Space Enumeration in Neural Network Computation

Authors: Victor O. K. Li, Yang Han, Jacqueline C. K. Lam

Abstract: Many methods for pruning neural networks rank weights or groups of weights by importance and retain a single subset. We study how channel groups contribute jointly to the output of multilayer perceptrons (MLPs) in Transformer language models. Inspired by network reliability analysis, probabilistic state space enumeration (PSSE) represents the dense MLP output as the expected importance-corrected masked output over $2^G$ joint states of $G$ channel groups. Summing the most probable states' probability-weighted outputs gives an approximation whose absolute error is bounded by the omitted probability mass times the maximum masked-output norm. NORDER-II adapts ORDER-II to enumerate states in decreasing probability without sorting the full space. Its first $B$ states maximize the probability mass captured at that budget. A weight-entropy scorer fixes each layer's ordering from pretrained weights. We evaluate all 168 MLP layers across six models from the Llama, Gemma, and Qwen families on two datasets with 8, 16, and 32 channel groups. In all 1,008 comparisons, the 95%-mass prefix yields lower mean relative error than uniform sampling at the same state count, with or without bias correction. At all three group counts, it also yields lower median and 99th-percentile relative error than a single top-$k$ mask. For downstream inference, we use the number of distinct channels in each prefix to set pruned MLP widths. With one validation-selected group count per model, PSSE achieves lower perplexity than both top-$k$ pruning baselines in all 36 matched-FLOP settings. Our pruned decoder MLPs achieve mean speedups of $1.11\times$, $1.23\times$, and $1.36\times$ over dense MLPs at 10%, 20%, and 30% target MLP projection FLOP savings, respectively. PSSE offers a principled path toward both probability-mass-controlled approximation and more efficient inference in pretrained language models.

replace A Matching Lower Bound for Online Smooth Calibration

Authors: Kaixuan Ji, Quanquan Gu

Abstract: We study smooth calibration error in sequential binary prediction, a measure of how far probabilistic forecasts are from being calibrated that is robust to small perturbations of the forecasts. The minimax smooth calibration error is known to lie between $\Omega(T^{1/3})$ and $O(\sqrt T)$(Qiao and Zheng, 2024), where $T$ is the number of rounds. We close this gap for both adaptive and oblivious adversaries. We construct an adaptive adversary whose outcome mean moves with the forecaster's cumulative residual, which makes compensating for past residuals costly and forces every forecaster to incur $\Omega(\sqrt T)$ expected smooth calibration error. Simulating this adversary against the forecaster's average prediction yields an oblivious adversary with the same guarantee. These lower bounds match the known $O(\sqrt T)$ upper bound up to universal constants, and are therefore minimax optimal. Our results also imply matching $\Theta(\sqrt T)$ bounds for calibration distance and lower calibration distance. The proof ideas are partially developed by GPT-6 Astra under the authors' guidance.

replace Local Coordination for Efficient Small-Model RL on TPUs

Authors: Loki Chen, Gregory B. Rehm, Yang Song, Hongye Xie, Ming Lei, Chongyang Bai, Bob Kamma, Mingfu Liang, Kavosh Asadi, Yufei Li, Xi Liu, Jay Xu, Rui Liu, Maziar Sanjabi, Luke Simon, Hamed Firooz

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) increasingly targets small language models, and executing these workloads efficiently across multiple accelerators requires effective coordination. On TPUs, Pathways-based RL uses a single controller to coordinate computation across hosts. This execution model is effective for large models, but its coordination computational costs are harder to amortize when small models repeatedly invoke short computations. To characterize these computational costs, we sweep dense models from 0.6B to 405B parameters on TPU v7x under a decode-heavy workload and measure how host and network computational overhead vary with model size. At 0.6B, host orchestration occupies 36% of step time and network communication 2%, whereas for large models, estimated network computational costs dominate the non-compute time. Motivated by these findings, we present Local Coordination JAX (LoCoJAX), which implements local rollout and reward processing on each TPU host with synchronous global training. In controlled comparisons against a tuned and optimized Pathways baseline on Qwen3-0.6B across three workloads and 8-64 TPU v7x chips, its colocated mode (LoCoJAX-CO) is 1.3-3.6x faster per step; on GSM8K, it completes a step faster on 8 chips than Pathways does on 64. Finally, a Ray-managed disaggregated mode (LoCoJAX-DA) overlaps rollout and training on separate hosts, and placement experiments establish when this overlap compensates for dividing devices and transferring weights: only when rollout dominates the step and receives most of the hosts. On NuminaMath-CoT at 256 chips, giving rollout 75% of hosts cuts step time by about 15% compared with LoCoJAX-CO.

replace ReCALM: A Residual-enhanced Crowdsourced Analytic Learning Mechanism for Distributed Continual Service Construction

Authors: Kejia Fan, Yajiang Huang, Jianheng Tang, Jinfeng Xu, Huiping Zhuang, Anfeng Liu, Houbing Herbert Song, Yunhuai Liu

Abstract: Crowdsourcing has emerged as a promising paradigm for constructing AI services by harnessing the collective capabilities of distributed workers. As consumers' demands continually evolve, service models must acquire new knowledge while retaining previously learned information. However, due to the inherited reliance on gradients for backpropagation, existing approaches typically suffer from catastrophic forgetting during continual model updates on heterogeneous data. Recent studies have introduced analytic learning, a gradient-free paradigm that achieves invariance through recursive closed-form solutions. Nevertheless, existing analytic methods are typically constrained by single-layer linear networks, which tend to suffer from underfitting due to their inability to perform deep representation learning. To address this issue, we propose ReCALM, a Residual-enhanced Crowdsourced Analytic Learning Mechanism that achieves deep nonlinear invariance for distributed continual service construction. Inspired by the success of ResNet, we develop gradient-free residual blocks for ReCALM with closed-form solutions. To implement distributed continual training for ReCALM, we further devise a cross-recursive strategy via least squares. Both theoretical and experimental analyses verify ReCALM's dual superiority in heterogeneity invariance and deep representation. With 20 residual layers, ReCALM improves accuracy by 8.85%-9.83% over the single-layer analytic baseline, while reducing computational costs by over 94% compared with gradient-based baselines.

replace Auditing World-Model Explanations in Retail Forecasting: Faithfulness, Accuracy and Price Response

Authors: Karthik Iyer

Abstract: Forecasting models that read the price and promotion plan explain each forecast as a baseline, what would sell with nothing planned, plus the lift the plan adds, and merchants order stock and plan promotions on that lift. We show that an explanation can pass the checks used to trust it and still be wrong enough to cost money. We define eight checks for any forecaster that states a plan lift, six with a pass rule fixed before any result, and run them on a world model and on LightGBM. In generated store data with known effects, the world model passes the checks of construction, stability, price direction and agreement with sales and with LightGBM, yet when merchants plan into high-demand weeks it states 1.33 to 1.40 times the true lift, and 1.23 to 1.42 times in eight variants of that world; LightGBM overstates it too (1.26, and 1.16 to 1.35). Both still pick the right weeks to promote, but stock ordered to the world model's lift is off by 0.44 to 0.48 of the true lift. The world model's stated response to a lasting regular-price change is 4.7 to 6.5 times the true -0.5, an error in kind that only a measured cause finds. Sanity checks for explanations test whether an explanation depends on the model; a decision needs to know whether it is right about the world, and these results show the first does not give the second. For the final tuned world models of our companion paper on nine data sets, baseline and lift add up under each model's own plan on every window, but a deeper price cut lowers the forecast in 29% to 83% of item-week steps on Dominick's. Across 703 configurations from the settings searches, checks passed were not related to accuracy (Spearman 0.02, 95% interval -0.02 to 0.05). A noisy benchmark of price episodes in Dominick's data is reported as a check of direction only.

replace Synthetic Crystal Data Generation for Diffusion Pretraining in Material Property Prediction

Authors: Aamod Varma, Shuyi Jia, Victor Fung

Abstract: Diffusion-based pretraining has emerged as a powerful strategy for learning transferable representations in graph neural networks for materials property prediction without requiring quantum-mechanical labels such as energies, forces, and stresses for every pretraining structure. However, the method retains a critical dependency: it requires structures residing near local minima of a potential energy surface, which have traditionally been obtained through costly DFT relaxations of known or physically synthesized compounds. This reliance constrains both the scale and the structural diversity of available pretraining data, leaving large regions of crystal structure space unexplored. In this work, we connect diffusion-based pretraining with synthetic structure generation to reduce this bottleneck. We introduce a synthetic data generation pipeline combining three complementary strategies: perturb-and-relax, generative sampling via MatterGen, and protostructure enumeration. Where structural relaxation is required, we use fast machine-learned interatomic potentials instead of performing a new DFT relaxation for each generated structure. We further compare a coordinate-denoising control with continuous-time variance-preserving (VP) and sub-VP diffusion objectives using a randomly initialized Orb-v2 backbone. The source comparison covers seven MatBench property-prediction tasks, and an eighth task, \texttt{mp\_gap}, is included in the protostructure scaling study. Every synthetic-pretraining condition numerically outperforms random initialization on all seven source-comparison tasks. Scaling the protostructure corpus from 20k to 165k under a fixed 400-epoch schedule numerically matches or exceeds MP-20k pretraining on most tasks; because this protocol increases both data diversity and optimization updates, the result reflects joint data-and-compute scaling.

replace Pareto-Informed Smart "Predict, then Optimize" for Multi-Objective Combinatorial Problems

Authors: Aabhash Dhakal, Tim Lachner

Abstract: Many real-world planning applications, from logistics, routing to resource allocation, give rise to optimization problems that need to balance multiple, conflicting objectives. While machine learning is often used to predict the unknown parameters or costs of these problems, standard models trained on prediction accuracy can lead to suboptimal plans. Decision-Focused Learning (DFL) addresses this by integrating the downstream optimization task into the training objective. However, existing DFL methods for multi-objective problems rely on simple scalarization, which fails to capture the rich trade-offs of the Pareto Frontier. To address this gap, we propose a Pareto-aware extension to single-objective Smart "Predict, then Optimize" (SPO+) for multi-objective combinatorial optimization problems. Our loss function leverages the true Pareto Frontier, forming a target decision that aligns with a sampled preference vector, and minimizes regret against this target. Experiments on multi-objective shortest path and multi-objective knapsack problems show that our approach outperforms traditional two-stage Mean Squared Error (MSE) baselines. It also surpasses scalarization-based SPO+ approaches.

replace-cross Mixtures of In-Context Learners

Authors: Giwon Hong, Emile van Krieken, Edoardo Ponti, Esmeralda S. Whitammer, Pasquale Minervini

Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) adapts LLMs by providing demonstrations without fine-tuning the model parameters; however, it does not differentiate between demonstrations and quadratically increases the complexity of Transformer LLMs, exhausting the memory. As a solution, we propose Mixtures of In-Context Learners (MoICL), a novel approach to treat subsets of demonstrations as experts and learn a weighting function to merge their output distributions based on a training set. In our experiments, we show performance improvements on 5 out of 7 classification datasets compared to a set of strong baselines (up to +13\% compared to ICL and LENS). Moreover, we enhance the Pareto frontier of ICL by reducing the inference time needed to achieve the same performance with fewer demonstrations. Finally, MoICL is more robust to out-of-domain (up to +11\%), imbalanced (up to +49\%), or noisy demonstrations (up to +38\%) or can filter these out from datasets. Overall, MoICL is a more expressive approach to learning from demonstrations without exhausting the context window or memory.

replace-cross Reinforcement Learning with Reference-Based Rewards for Language Model Reasoning and Alignment

Authors: Shuai Zhao, Yunqiu Xu, Linchao Zhu, Ling Chen, Yi Yang

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) powers two major lines of large language model (LLM) post-training: reasoning, which elicits deliberate, verifiable problem solving, and alignment, which steers models toward helpful, harmless, and honest behavior. Both hinge on reward design. Reasoning relies on rule-based verification of final answers, whose binary feedback is blind to intermediate derivations, whereas process reward models (PRMs) require step-level annotations too costly to scale. Alignment depends on reward models (RMs) trained on binary preference pairs, making both data collection and model training resource-intensive. We propose RL2R, reinforcement learning with reference-based rewards, as a unified and inexpensive alternative. Using pre-existing or distilled references, RL2R measures semantic similarity with lightweight metrics such as BERTScore between a sampled generation and a reference answer or solution. Similarity preserves ordinal quality signals that binary feedback discards: responses closer to the reference earn higher rewards, and sound derivations receive credit even when the final conclusion is wrong. For mathematical reasoning, augmenting rule-based verification with answer- and step-level similarity provides low-cost process supervision and outperforms answer-only verification. For alignment, a critic-free REINFORCE-style instantiation covers general preference, safety, and confidence tasks on par with RM-based methods. Reference-based rewards thus offer a cost-effective, versatile reward source spanning reasoning and alignment.

Abstract: Deep generative models such as conditional variational autoencoders (CVAEs) have shown great promise for predicting trajectories of surrounding agents in autonomous vehicle planning. replace-cross When Features Beat Noise: A Noise-Driven Hypothesis Testing Method for Feature Selection

Authors: Mousam Sinha, Tirtha Sarathi Ghosh, Koushik Biswas, Ridam Pal

Abstract: Feature selection has remained a daunting challenge in machine learning and artificial intelligence, where increasingly complex, high-dimensional datasets demand principled strategies for isolating the most informative predictors. Despite widespread adoption, many established techniques such as Boruta, Recursive Feature Elimination and other wrapper based methods suffer from notable limitations; some incur substantial computational cost, while others offer no definite statistical driven stopping criteria or assess the significance of their importance scores. This paper proposes a novel feature selection method that addresses these limitations. Our approach introduces multiple random noise features and evaluates each feature's importance against the maximum importance value among these noise features incorporating a non-parametric bootstrap-based hypothesis testing framework to establish a solid theoretical foundation. We establish the conceptual soundness of our approach through statistical derivations that articulate the principles guiding the design of our algorithm. To assess reliability and practical utility, we validated the method on controlled simulations and diverse real-world datasets, where it consistently outperformed established feature-selection approaches including Boruta, Model-X Knockoffs, RFE, LASSO, Elastic Net, and mRMR in recovering meaningful signals. Hence, the method emerges as a robust algorithm for principled feature selection, enabling the distillation of informative predictors that support reliable inference, enhanced predictive performance, and efficient computation.

replace-cross Integrated Prediction and Multi-period Portfolio Optimization

Authors: Yuxuan Linghu, Zhiyuan Liu, Qi Deng

Abstract: Multi-period portfolio optimization is important for real portfolio management, as it accounts for transaction costs, path-dependent risks, and the intertemporal structure of trading decisions that single-period models cannot capture. Classical methods usually follow a two-stage framework: machine learning algorithms are employed to produce forecasts that closely fit the realized returns, and the predicted values are then used in a downstream portfolio optimization problem to determine the asset weights. This separation leads to a fundamental misalignment between predictions and decision outcomes, while also ignoring the impact of transaction costs. To bridge this gap, recent studies have proposed the idea of end-to-end learning, integrating the two stages into a single pipeline. This paper introduces IPMO (Integrated Prediction and Multi-period Portfolio Optimization), a model for multi-period mean-variance portfolio optimization with turnover penalties. The predictor generates multi-period return forecasts that parameterize a differentiable convex optimization layer, which in turn drives learning via portfolio performance. For scalability, we introduce a mirror-descent fixed-point (MDFP) differentiation scheme that avoids factorizing the Karush-Kuhn-Tucker (KKT) systems, which thus yields stable implicit gradients and nearly scale-insensitive runtime as the decision horizon grows. In experiments with real market data and two representative time-series prediction models, the IPMO method consistently outperforms the two-stage benchmarks in risk-adjusted performance net of transaction costs and achieves more coherent allocation paths. Our results show that integrating machine learning prediction with optimization in the multi-period setting improves financial outcomes and remains computationally tractable.

replace-cross Low-Dimensional Adaptation of Rectified Flow: A Diffusion and Stochastic Localization Perspective

Authors: Saptarshi Roy, Alessandro Rinaldo, Purnamrita Sarkar

Abstract: In recent years, Rectified flow (RF) has gained considerable popularity largely due to its generation efficiency and state-of-the-art performance. In this paper, we investigate how well RF automatically adapts to the intrinsic low dimensionality of the support of the target distribution to accelerate sampling. We show that, using a carefully designed end-point adapted time-discretization scheme and with sufficiently accurate drift estimates, the RF sampler enjoys an iteration complexity of order $O(k/\varepsilon)$ (up to log factors), where $\varepsilon$ is the precision in total variation distance and $k$ is the intrinsic dimension of the target distribution. replace-cross Targeted Synthetic Control Method

Authors: Yuxin Wang, Dennis Frauen, Emil Javurek, Konstantin Hess, Yuchen Ma, Stefan Feuerriegel

Abstract: The synthetic control method (SCM) estimates causal effects in panel data with a single-treated unit by constructing a counterfactual outcome as a weighted combination of untreated control units that matches the pre-treatment trajectory. In this paper, we introduce the targeted synthetic control (TSC) method, a new two-stage estimator that directly estimates the counterfactual outcome. Specifically, our TSC method (1) yields a targeted debiasing estimator, in the sense that the targeted updating refines the initial weights to produce more stable weights; and (2) ensures that the final counterfactual estimation is a convex combination of observed control outcomes to enable direct interpretation of the synthetic control weights. TSC is flexible and can be instantiated with arbitrary machine learning models. Methodologically, TSC starts from an initial set of synthetic-control weights via a one-dimensional targeted update through the weight-tilting submodel, which calibrates the weights to reduce bias of weights estimation arising from pre-treatment fit. Furthermore, TSC avoids key shortcomings of existing methods (e.g., the augmented SCM), which can produce unbounded counterfactual estimates. Across extensive synthetic and real-world experiments, TSC consistently improves estimation accuracy over state-of-the-art SCM baselines.

replace-cross SHAMISA: SHAped Modeling of Implicit Structural Associations for Self-supervised No-Reference Image Quality Assessment

Authors: Mahdi Naseri, Zhou Wang

Abstract: No-Reference Image Quality Assessment (NR-IQA) aims to estimate perceptual quality without access to a pristine reference. Learning an NR-IQA model faces a fundamental bottleneck because it requires many costly human perceptual labels. We propose SHAMISA, a non-contrastive self-supervised framework that learns from unlabeled distorted images through explicitly structured relational supervision. Unlike prior methods that impose rigid binary similarity constraints, SHAMISA shapes soft, controllable implicit structural associations that are both distortion-aware and content-sensitive. A key innovation is its compositional distortion engine, which generates an uncountable family of degradations from continuous parameter spaces and groups the resulting images so that exactly one distortion factor varies at a time. This construction enables fine-grained control over representational similarity during training. Images with shared distortion patterns are pulled together in the embedding space, while severity variations produce structured shifts. Dual-source relation graphs integrate known degradation profiles with emergent structural affinities throughout pre-training. The learned encoder is then frozen and paired with a linear regressor. Extensive experiments on synthetic, authentic, and cross-dataset NR-IQA benchmarks demonstrate strong overall performance, improved transfer in the reported cross-dataset comparisons, and favorable qualitative robustness diagnostics. Self-supervised pre-training requires no IQA opinion-score annotations or contrastive loss.

replace-cross Knowledge Localization in Mixture-of-Experts LLMs Using Cross-Lingual Inconsistency

Authors: Lucas Bandarkar, Alan Ansell, Trevor Cohn

Abstract: Modern LLMs continue to exhibit significant variance in behavior across languages, such as being able to recall factual information in some languages but not others. While typically studied as a problem to be mitigated, in this work, we propose leveraging this cross-lingual inconsistency as a tool for interpretability in mixture-of-experts (MoE) LLMs. Our knowledge localization framework contrasts routing for sets of languages where the model correctly recalls information from languages where it fails. This allows us to isolate model components that play a functional role in answering about a piece of knowledge. Our method proceeds in two stages: (1) querying the model with difficult factual questions across a diverse set of languages to generate "success" and "failure" activation buckets and then (2) applying a statistical contrastive analysis to the MoE router logits to identify experts important for knowledge. To validate the necessity of this small number of experts for answering a knowledge question, we deactivate them and re-ask the question. We find that despite only deactivating about 20 out of 6000 experts, the model no longer answers correctly in over 40% of cases. Generally, this method provides a realistic and scalable knowledge localization approach to address increasingly complex LLMs.

Abstract: Zero-Shot Object Navigation (ZSON) in unknown multi-floor environments presents a significant challenge. replace-cross HYPER: Hypothesis-Guided Planning for Evidence-Seeking Retrieval

Authors: Hangeol Chang, Changsun Lee, Seungjoon Rho, Junho Yeo, Jong Chul Ye

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) improves Large Language Models (LLMs) by grounding generation in external, non-parametric knowledge. However, when a task requires choosing among competing possibilities, such as a diagnosis among plausible alternatives, topically relevant context is often insufficient to drive the final decision. To address this issue, we propose HYPER: Hypothesis-Guided Planning for Evidence-Seeking Retrieval, a training-free pre-retrieval planning framework that reorients RAG from topic-oriented retrieval to evidence-oriented retrieval. HYPER uses a provisional answer hypothesis as a planning anchor. It then decomposes retrieval into three evidence roles. Support seeks evidence for the provisional answer. Distinction retrieves criteria that separate competing alternatives. Feature grounds salient question features needed for the decision. This planning step steers retrieval toward evidence near the decision boundary, enabling the generator to answer from context that is more directly aligned with the required choice. On MedQA and MMLU-Med, where single-query retrieval falls below no-retrieval chain-of-thought prompting, HYPER achieves the best average accuracy among ten baselines, improving over Simple RAG by 5.9 and 3.6 points, respectively. Its gains transfer to the 14 subjects of MMLU-Pro, at the cost of two additional LLM calls per question. Code is available at https://github.com/hangeol/HypothesisRAG.
