The agent succeeds zero-shot in 104 of 108 real-world trials spanning twelve tasks and three robot instances, reducing failure rates by 20x and 15x relative to strong VLA and WAM baselines. The basic harness supplies no grasping, pushing, or placing skills; the agent develops its own strategies, such as searching for hidden objects, using objects as tools, and calibrating its cameras. With the addition of a persistent memory store, the agent can also learn from experience: an augmented harness with a memory representation that is carried across episodes lowers the failure rate on RoboDojo bimanual manipulation tasks by 4.6x relative to zero-shot deployment. Our analysis suggests that AMPs are substantially more general and robust than specialist manipulation policies, providing a promising foundation for robot manipulation.

replace-cross Explanations that Transfer: Interpreting Medical Retrieval with Sparse AutoEncoders

Authors: Diane Bouchacourt, Ga\"etan Brison

Abstract: Medical AI systems that answer users' queries rely on retrieving medical knowledge from large document collections. In this context, finetuning general models on medical datasets can improve retrieval relevance. Yet the dense embeddings underlying modern retrieval systems remain largely opaque, making it difficult to understand the mechanics behind their results. Moreover, adapting to a specific medical dataset risks losing transferability to new clinical corpora. In this work, we tackle these issues by applying Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) to medical information retrieval, transforming dense text embeddings into semantically interpretable representations. To do so, our model combines a reconstruction objective with an InfoNCE contrastive loss that directly preserves query-document relevance. We evaluate three embedding models across six medical retrieval datasets, both in-domain and out-of-domain. Trained on a medical corpus, our sparse codes improve in-domain retrieval over the original embeddings, and lose less out-of-domain performance than their dense counterparts, especially when the target corpus is distant from the training one. Importantly, our experiments show that the method yields interpretable features whose semantic content can be inferred from the queries and documents that activate them most strongly. The interpretation of these features sheds light on why a particular document was retrieved, and transfers beyond the training distribution.

replace-cross FlashREINFORCE: Critic-Free Single-Rollout Asynchronous RL for Agentic Language Models

Authors: Jian Hu, Yifan Zhang, Hao Zhang, Binfeng Xu, Shaokun Zhang, Hongqing Peng, Zhiding Yu, Pavlo Molchanov, Jan Kautz, Yi Dong

Abstract: Asynchronous reinforcement learning suits long-horizon agents with irregular rollout times, but group-relative updates require repeated rollouts of each prompt. However, group-based sampling limits prompt coverage at a fixed rollout budget and introduces group synchronization barriers. One rollout per prompt avoids these constraints but requires learning from stale, variable-length trajectories without a sibling baseline. We present FlashREINFORCE, to our knowledge the first critic-free single-rollout framework that scales to long asynchronous training and large models. It combines One-Batch REINFORCE, a Sequence Trust Region, and Sample-Mean Optimization, which provide prompt coverage and batch centering while controlling policy drift and length-amplified negative updates. Each component is easy to implement, and the result is their combination: a batch-mean baseline is stable under sample-mean loss, and each common alternative degrades or collapses training. FlashREINFORCE remains stable through 6,000 updates on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-1.5B at policy lag four, and through 1,450 on the 30B MoE Qwen3-30B-A3B at lag eight. Under identical loss, data, and lag it matches a strengthened group-relative baseline within 0.5 points, and with half the rollouts exceeds published synchronous baselines on Qwen2.5-Math-1.5B (38.0 versus 36.3 five-benchmark mean). On Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct it maintains tool use where GRPO stops calling tools and achieves 98.3%/96.5% seen/unseen ALFWorld success.

replace-cross Ambiguous Examples Help, But Full Data Wins: Revisiting Dataset Cartography for Extractive Adversarial QA

Authors: Paul K. Mandal

Abstract: Dataset cartography argues that training dynamics can reveal regions of a dataset that matter differently for learning: easy-to-learn examples aid optimization, hard-to-learn examples often expose annotation problems, and ambiguous examples contribute strongly to out-of-distribution generalization. A striking implication of the original work is that a model trained on only 33% ambiguous examples can outperform full-data training on several OOD evaluations, including a QNLI reformulation of SQuAD evaluated on Adversarial SQuAD. We revisit this claim in a stricter setting: open-ended extractive question answering, where models must predict answer spans rather than classify question-sentence pairs. For each encoder family, we compute clean-source SQuAD training dynamics, train on full, random, easy-ranked, ambiguous-ranked, and hard-ranked subsets, and evaluate robustness on adversarial-only AddSent and AddOneSent rows. Ambiguous-ranked 33% subsets beat random 33% subsets on exact-match accuracy in all 14 model-evaluation cells and on F1 in 12/14 cells. However, full-data training beats ambiguous-ranked training in 12/14 exact-match cells and 12/14 F1 cells, with mean full-minus-ambiguous gains of 3.40 EM and 4.10 F1. The most thoroughly replicated ELECTRA-small panel is also the main exception to the ambiguous-over-random F1 gains. These results show that clean-source cartography remains useful for reduced-data selection, but its OOD advantage does not carry over as a general replacement for full-data training in extractive adversarial QA.

replace-cross Passable for Whom? Few-Shot Learning for Personal Calibration of Sidewalk Accessibility Perception for Human-Centered Autonomous Driving

Authors: Wesley Maia, Angel Martinez-Sanchez, Maitrayee Keskar, Ross Greer

Abstract: Whether a sidewalk is passable depends on who is trying to pass: people who use the same mobility aid disagree about the same image, so one label per scene, or per aid, is wrong for many of them. We introduce few-shot personal accessibility perception: from K answers of a person never seen before, predict their judgment of sidewalk scenes never seen before. PassCal separates a shared description of the scene from the person's interpretation of it. The description comes from the internal state of a frozen vision-language model (VLM) and from barrier type, severity and context properties predicted by a detector trained on 14.9k public audits; the interpretation, a calibration of the person's response style and a weighting of the properties, is fit from their answers. On 8,883 judgments in which each of 135 verified mobility-aid users rated the same 50 sidewalk scenes, with people and scenes both held out, eight answers lower the Brier score from 0.550 to 0.479. The same VLM prompted with those answers reaches 0.562, and the same calibration without the scene 0.521. Conformal risk control turns predictions into passable, blocked or "depends on you" statements with a bounded expected rate of wrong statements over people; at 5%, eight answers raise the share of decisive scenes from 27% to 42%. Audit-supervised properties improve all sixteen vision and VLM encoders we test and outperform attributes from a sidewalk navigation VLM.

replace-cross Heave-Ho: Decentralized Long-Horizon Multi-Humanoid Collaboration

Authors: Ting-Hsuan Chen, Siheng Zhao, Han Jiang, Qi Pan, Louison Lu, Jiaheng Luo, Henghui Bao, Yiyu Chen, Quan Nguyen, Yue Wang

Abstract: Collaboration promises to extend humanoid capabilities beyond what individual robots can achieve. However, realizing this potential requires coordinated whole-body interactions, coherent long-horizon planning, and precise synchronization across robots. We introduce Heave-Ho, a decentralized framework for long-horizon collaborative multi-humanoid loco-manipulation. At the low level, we develop a multi-agent post-training framework that adapts a humanoid foundation tracking model to physically coupled interactions between humanoids, enabling coordinated decentralized control from local observations alone. At the high level, we train a vision-language planner and design a speech-based leader-follower coordination mechanism to synchronize decentralized execution without network communication. Trained with two identical humanoids, our system scales to teams of up to ten robots and generalizes to heterogeneous teams without retraining. To benchmark these capabilities, we introduce HH-Bench, comprising 12 tasks involving teams of 2 to 10 humanoids, including heterogeneous teams. Systematic evaluations show that Heave-Ho consistently outperforms baseline methods, while real-world experiments demonstrate robust decentralized collaboration across challenging tasks.

replace-cross MoE--LoRA Methods for Multilingual Adaptation: A Systematic Empirical Study

Authors: Nikit Srivastava, Joel Dag, Ren\'e Speck, Axel-Cyrille Ngonga Ngomo

Abstract: Routed low-rank adapters offer a parameter-efficient means to improve the performance of large language models (LLMs) across tasks. Hence, it is assumed that they can also be used to improve the support of low-resource languages in LLMs, which currently serve English far better than the thousands of other languages spoken worldwide. However, this assumption has never been verified empirically under a shared multilingual evaluation protocol. We address exactly this gap by comparing eight low-rank adaptation (LoRA) methods on a frozen base model, using 146 training runs across 200 unique languages and more than 10,000 evaluations. Our results suggest that a reduced-scale search can identify viable configurations of LoRA methods but does not reliably predict their relative performance, as the top-ranking configuration is never best at scale. LoRA reliably improves language-model fit on translation data, consistently degrades reading comprehension, and benefits low-resource languages most. These findings suggest that LoRA methods should be regarded as targeted tools rather than general-purpose means for improving the multilingual capabilities of LLMs.

replace-cross Multimodal Masked Diffusion Autoencoder for Modeling Impaired Driving

Authors: John Gideon, Anshul Gupta, Megan Applegate-Kenton, Kayli Battel, Todd J Rowell, Patricio Reyes Gomez, Laporsha Dees, Emily Sumner, Avinash Balachandran, Guy Rosman

Abstract: Human cognitive state (e.g., distraction, stress), gaze, and behavior interact with the driving environment in subtle ways that AI could use to assist drivers, but ground-truth labels for human cognitive state levels are costly to obtain at scale. We propose a multimodal masked-diffusion autoencoder, ID-MAE, that adds a self-supervised objective to impairment prediction: alongside predicting the impairment label as a masked token, it reconstructs masked gaze and trajectory. To probe the effects of different modalities and types of impairments on model inference, we introduce DIVERT, a driving-simulator dataset containing 103 hours of driving data (n = 109) with ten impairment inductions spanning cognitive, visual, and emotional states, recorded with gaze, vehicle state, and scene video. We demonstrate how ID-MAE affords significant improvements in impairment inference results on both the DIVERT dataset and existing datasets, and enables strong results in additional tasks, such as trajectory and gaze prediction. For location-matched pairs, our model achieves a paired AUC of 0.706 for impairment detection on DIVERT, compared to 0.590 without reconstruction loss, improving over existing baselines. We probe the design space in terms of input modalities, masking scheme, positional encoding, attention mechanism, and decoding. We also investigate the interaction between various impairments in training and inference, demonstrating that visually demanding distractions are separable from cognitive distractions. Code and data will be available upon acceptance.
